!!Abstract Expressionism.	

!Abdul'allah, Faisal.
Artist who made an installation that invites the viewer to enter a large box like sturcutrue, those with light coloured eyes are to enter to the left, those with dark eyes to the right. Once segregated by genetic characiteristic, the viewers find themselves in one of two spaces. One is a small room with hglghy polished metallic floor walls and ceiling in a copper coloured metal that functions like a mirror and creates a glowing light. On the floor is an inscribed text that addresses  the viewer personally and asks them to conisider their feelings on arrival in this space. 
The other space the remainder of the larger box like structure and it is pitch black. On entry it can be seen that the small room projects into this space, and is in fact constructed from a material that allows one way vision. From the dark space the viewers in the small space can be seen clearly. However althoght this would seem like a priviledged space due to the materials ig its construction, the people in it seem to be imprisoned, regarded secretely by the watchers in the larger space. 
!Abramovic Marina and Ulay. 
Born 1950s, Abramovic first came to public attention when she invited visitors to a gallery in which she appeared naked, stating that the visitors could do anything they wanted to her and she would not resist or retaliate. She provided a number of implements with which to do this including knives and a gun. She narrowly avoided serious injury but her initial premise was correct: like the invisible barrier of etiquette surrounding an art work, there was a barrier of propriety and embarrassment protecting her that was only broached when the beginnings of a mob mentality formed.
Abramovic worked together with her partner Ulay until the early 1980s making performance 'sculptures' and endurance works. 'Breathe' involved them in a mouth to mouth embrace for as long as they could breathe using their combined exhaled breath. They braided their hair together and stayed sitting back to back for 8 hours. For another work they used a full size bow and arrow and stood facing each other, with Ulay holding the string and Abramovic holding the arrow at the point on the centre of the bow string from which it is released. They maintained this state of equilibrium through tension until the strain of holding on meant they had to either stop or let go of the tension in the bow which would have resulted in the release of the arrow which was aimed at Abramovic. In Doorway they stood naked either side of the entrance to the gallery in the doorway facing each other. This meant that those wishing to enter were forced to brush past them and had to choose who to face as they turned sideways to enter. 
These partner works focused on endurance through concentration and on the tension in partnerships manifested in physical mutual support. They are notable for the degree of politeness and gentleness combined with a power and implicit violence. The artists described them as sculptures.
After the relationship with Ulay ended Abramovic continued to make endurance works but while these maintained a formal sculptural quality they acquired a ritual aspect, as in the work in which she sat in a circle of salt surrounded by poisonous snakes and a work made in an amethyst mining town in Brazil for which she made and wore clogs from blocks of amethyst. Abramovic made a work for a tree sited in a public square in which she placed a number of speakers playing birdsong so that it seemed that the tree was teeming with tiny birds.

!!Art Deco.
!Abts Tomma. 
German painter living in UK born 1970, Abts paints abstract geometric works which rely on a long period of reworking and on very carefully chosen colours. The surface of the works shows reworking and over brushing, evidence of the time taken to make the paintings.
!Acconci Vito.
US artist born 1940s, Acconci made several endurance works in the early 70s, notably Seedbed, for which he constructed a false gallery floor which sloped up form the door to the rear of the space. Acconci was reputedly sealed in the vodi under the floor masturbating. In another work he constructed a shelf high up on the gallery wall in such a way that he could not be seen by those in the gallery from any angle and stayed on the shelf for the hours during which the gallery was open. In these works he made the gallery space and the viewers own feelings about being in that space the subject of the work, by his invisible presence having the maximum possible effect on the viewer. 
A smaller scale work is a video playing on a monitor placed on a furry rug on the floor. On the screen Acconci is seen, lying on his stomach, his face right up against the camera, talking to the viewer and inviting them to lie down on the rug, to join him on the floor.
!Acres Reverend Ethan.
Ordained clergyman from California who also works as an artist, making live works that combine a religious service with performance, usually involving a sermon, and other performers.
!Act Up. 
Group of gay rights activists working in US chiefly NY in the 1980s, consisting of artists and others. Poster campaigns and subverting advertising, us of official statistics to point out discriminatory practise. See guerrilla girls.

!!Fluxus.
!Adams Ansel. 
Born 1880s, died 1970s, American photographer working at the beginning of 20th century, Adams was a pioneer of landscape photography. Chiefly known for his images of American national parks and wildernesses, such as Yosemite and Yellowstone. When he made these Photographs the wildernesses were not well explored and many parts remote and inaccessible, necessitating many days trekking with equipment to get the images he wanted. The photographs capture the enormity and grandiosity of the landscape and came to represent the American wilderness to the rest of the world. Because of their huge scope and precise depth of field they seem to be photographic records of time passing although of course they only record a moment in time.
!Add N to X.
A musician duo one male one female, both born in the 1970s who made video works in the late 1990s early 2000s, for example a continually changing colour grid controlled by the soundtrack which accompanied it. Also Angela Bulloch
!Affrassiabi Shahin.
British artist born 1960. Affrassiabi conceptual installations employ household items and materials to make formal sculptural constructions which reference other art.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Agar Eileen.
British artist working in the 50 and 60s, made sculptures and reliefs, both abstract and symbolist, some small objects. Dead.
!Aitchison Craigie.
Scottish painter born 1940s best known in the 1980s for his paintings of his pet Bedlington Terrier. The dogs are painted in profile on large brightly and flatly coloured canvases.
!!Expressionism.

!Aitken Doug. 
American film and installation artist born in 1975. Aitken first worked in commercials and used the experience gained in this field to make a series of multi screen and projected video installations, winning a Golden Lion at the Venice Biennale in 2001. Diamond Sea featured a deserted mining town in Namibia, which is not marked on state maps to ensure that its location remains secret. A sense of the people who worked there is evoked (and their exploitation implicit) and a haunting empty beauty. Aitken works all have soundtracks made by him which form an integral part of the installation , sometimes several films will play at once resulting in a blend of soundtracks. Another work, a film of people attending a travelling cinema in India, was screened in a tented room with sand on the floor. Aitken's works deal with location and presence, with a sense of place being a priority.
!Akkerman, Philippe.
!Albers Josef.
US painter born 1900, died. Albers made geometric abstracts usually based on the square, using dull versions of primary colours. He was interested in the optical effects of colours on each other. Albers was a lifelong teacher of art and was Eva Hesse's professor. He is discussed in Hesse's diary.
!Allen Philip.
British painter born 1970s, Allen's almost abstract paintings of the late 90s and early 2000s are a change from his previous figurative work. The paintings have a roughly painted background and a multicoloured scallop motif on top, referencing landscape and abstract painting. 
British painter born in 1970s moved from figurative paintings to abstraction, making brightly coloured abstract landscapes painted roughly and lumpily, with more neatly painted lines of scallop shapes over the top. He makes reference to the impossibility of painting, a dilemma for many late 20th early 21st century artists.

!!Group of Seven.
!Allington Edward.
Allington is a British sculptor who was part of the group of artists born around 1950 making large scale ambitious work in the 1980s and 90s. his best know work is Horn of Plenty, a cornucopia of found objects. He used the cornucopia extensively as motif, and was one of the artist invited to propose a work for the empty plinth in Trafalgar square in London in 2001
!Almond Darren.
British artist born in 1970s. Almond interested in time. He made a large  clock the type that has 24 hour time display in figures that flip over, to be mounted in a public institution or railway station that displays the time going very fast so that a minute is a second and an hour is reached in a minute. He also made a large aeroplane propeller painted white and mounted horizontally on the floor of a gallery so that its blades nearly touch the walls leaving just enough room for a person to pass. He set up a live video link between a gallery and an empty? prison cell, permitting the viewer a fly on the wall experience of visiting a gallery to visit a prison cell. Almond also made a video titled Ghost Train which is filmed in a ghost train at a fairground somewhere in Austria, in black and white. Filmed from one of the cars, the film is grainy and visually lyrical as the low light conditions of the video alter what the visitor would see. The sound of the train and the sound effects of the ride which are edited with a musical soundtrack when represented somehow demand a different attention from the viewer. Recreated a bus shelter in a town where there was a Nazi concentration camp, the viewer is able to 'wait for a bus' to take them as a tourist to visit the camp, whilst pondering on the wait for transit
what else?

!!Neo-Expressionism.
!Alys Francis.
Born in the 1950s, Brazilian or Mexican artist who made a series of works in the early 90s in which commissioned a sign painter to paint a sign, which he then set up in his studio and asked the painter to paint the scene including the sign. Interested in appropriation and reproduction.

!!Bauhaus	Impressionism.	
!Andre Carl. 
Born 1948, Andre leading artist in a generation of US minimalist sculptors working in the 1970s such as Donald Judd and Robert Morris. He is notorious for his 'pile of bricks' purchased by the Tate gallery in London in 1980, which was derided in the press for being nothing but a pile of ordinary house bricks. This work, an arrangement of house bricks in a plinth two bricks thick was similar to his other works, chiefly floor works consisting of metal tiles arranged in rectangles and lines. It was an important part of the work that the viewer was free to walk on the sculpture. These works are both self effacing in their near invisibility and grandiose in their ambition, Andre said that they represented footprints of much larger structures  and that he wanted to imply monolithic structures through minimal and economic means. A tension between the monolithic and the invisible. 
Andre also made a number of text works, some with 'x's and 'o's in rows, and others a kind of concrete poetry made by fitting words into some kind of geometric system.

!!Ancient & Classical Art.	
!Andrews Michael.
British painter
!Anger Kenneth.
Underground filmmaker born in the 50s whose best known film Scorpio Rising had an influence on a whole generation of artists and filmmakers.
!Antin Eleanor.
American artist born around 1948. In her best known work Carving: a Traditional Sculpture made in 1971 Antin put herself on a strict diet and had a male doctor weigh and measure her every day for a month, on of a number of works in which Antin investigated the effect on women of the ideals forced on them in a patriarchal society. In this work she attempted to literally sculpt her own body, making a comment both on women's control over their own bodies and the centuries of depiction of the nude female body by men, and on women's continuing need to make themselves attractive for men. The performance was documented by one photograph every day which were placed together in a grid and framed, and by a video. During the 1980s Antin began to make more theatrical performances, using cut out puppets. She also developed an alter ego, Eleanora Antinova a trans gender Russian ballet dancer who wore a tutu and a long beard.

!!Bloomsbury Group.	
!Antoni Janine.
American artist born in the late 1970s who made a series of performance works and installations. Chocolate Gnaw and Lard Gnaw involved the artist chewing as much chocolate as she could from a one metre cube and melting the resulting chocolate to make chocolates. The lard chewed from a similar sized block she made into lipsticks. The residual blocks are displayed as part of the work. She also cast busts of herself from the chewings. 
The chocolate and lipstick products were displayed in luxurious packaging in a mirrored and gilt edged display case such as might be seen in a department store, making a connection between the basic act of chewing and eating, female relationships to food and body image (fat and chocolate) and cosmetics and the role of packaging in the stimulation of desire. In 'Harmony Hair Colour' she used her long dark hair as a brush to make paintings with hair dye on large pieces of paper on the floor, making a reference to Yves Klein's blue paintings made using women's bodies as brushes. 'Sleep' was an installation in which Antoni slept at night, wired up to a machine which recorded her R E M patterns. She transferred the resulting line on the graph drawn by the machine onto a continuous sheet of paper onto a specially constructed wooden loom in the same room and wove on it a white fabric with the sleep patterns as a blue line. The resulting fabric length was a long as the sleep graph whose length was defined by the number of days Antoni slept in the gallery. As soon as she hade enough, Antoni made the fabric into a nightgown in which she then slept, sleeping clothed in her sleep as it were.
!Appel Karel.
Dutch abstract painter working in the 1940s and 50s, Appel formed the CoBRA group of painters in Amsterdam. Bright colours, semi abstract impasto paintings with smiling toothy faces. Died.

!!Fauvism Op-Art.
!Applebroog Ida.
American artist born in 1930s, Applebroog's paintings and free standing paintings are concerned with memory, childhood and narrative. She often uses the device of the predella form, coupled with the film negative, in an allusion to the votive object and the memory preserving photograph. The narrative of her work is often about childhood and hints at abuse, and it is very much the work of someone who is a mother. The paintings are often constructed from several canvases butted together. Freestanding works are propped on the floor upright or placed horizontally. Applebroog made a film with her daughter, film maker and artist Beth B, which is a loose narrative of unhappy childhood with a dreamlike quality.
!Aramesh Reza.
British artist born 1960s works in mixed media, made painting of the branches of trees, and installations with furniture and real stuffed animals.
!Arnold Liz.
British artist born 1963 died 2002 painted women and anthropomorphised cats, very often with flowers, inhabiting story book universes, painted in strong colours with lots of black in a neat way. Arnolds creatures inhabit the real world in an unreal way and often seem to have very serious but mysterious business to undertake. They are fashionably dressed and wear jewellery and have long eyelashes, sometimes there is a kind of science fiction atmosphere.
!Arp Hans.
Dutch artist worked in mixed media and painting made a lot of wall hung reliefs and construction, using primary colours and white, and simple shapes, usually curved. Died.
!Art and Language.
British artist duo born around 1950 known for their dry conceptualism dealing with art and text as a subject. A number of paintings with text added.
!Artschwager Richard.
American artist born in 1940s who made sculptures of furniture in the late 70s and early 1980s, at the same time as Ettore Sottsass's Memphis group was making furniture from laminate in Milan. A table represented by a box covered in printed laminate legs, and a pink tablecloth he rendered a useful object into an unusable sculpture constructed from banal materials used for cheap furniture and never for sculpture. Artschwarger also made a series of Chippendale chairs from laminate, their form flattened out and clad in unlikely patterns and designs. He was interested in these works in making unlikely combinations of generic form with generic material, forms from classical era or from antiquity with modern materials, in doing this he was following the ideas of the architects Alice etc who wrote a book about the architecture of Las Vegas. !!Bloomsbury Group.	
!Audeoud Fabienne.
French artist born around 1968, Audeoud's work comes from her interest in classical music specifically opera. Video works show Audeoude as a diva singing to the point of collapse, and as a fading diva on a self destructive binge in her bedroom. There is a spare and often painful quality to these works which are superficially abject and comical which make them serious in their effect on the viewer. A project to unlearn the piano took many months, in which she set herself the task of unlearning everything she knew about playing the piano, this like many of her works was done daily, all day, in a gallery. A performative and endurance aspect to all work. Filmed a brass band rehearsing, they are static but their discordance is at odds with their calm appearance. Other performances. Manifestos. She also made several collaborative painting exhibitions with former Bank artist John Russell. These were room sized canvasses painted quickly and fairly badly, figurative, vaguely narrative with text added.

!!Situationism.
!Baader, Johannes.
German artist involved in dada and cabaret Voltaire from the fringes, Baader also carried out a number of extreme public performances, often involving mass leafleting and physical risk to himselg
!Bacon Francis.
Very influential British painter born in 1920s Bacons paintings are conceptual but theatrical figurative works with abstract qualities. They are nearly always portraits, in which the figure is distorted, often to grotesque effect, perhaps looking if they have been melted or brushed away, often the figure appears to be in discomfort. The figures are rendered with rough brushstrokes, and are placed on a contrasting flat colour background, often within a delineated 'three dimensional space'. Bacon made many self portraits, one of his best known works is the diptych Three screaming Popes 1960? Three large canvases each with a portrait of a pope in a cope and mitre, within a kind of abstract geometric frame structure, on dark orange and red grounds. The faces of the popes are blurred and distorted. Also painted a dog which is either curled up or chasing its tail, as with Bacons other work, there is an impression of movement as if a photograph has been taken on a long exposure and the sitter has moved, leaving a trail of the movement on the image.
Bacon was almost as well know for his lifestyle as for his work. A heavy drinking habitué off bars in Soho in London he live a solitary and obsessive working life, rarely spending a day without painting. His studio was famously untidy and dirty. Bacon died in the 1990s.
!Baechler Donald.

!!Medieval & Gothic Art	.
!Bag Alex.
Us artist who worked in video in the 1990s. Made a video about being an art student.
!Baker Bobby.
British performance artist born 1950 who came to prominence in the 1980s with her food performances. Baker returned to art in her late 40s after raising a family and trained as painter, but began to make performance art when she found that a painters life was not compatible with bringing up a family and that painting and its history did not offer her what she needed to make work about what interested her, namely the creativity inherent in domestic and family life. Baker sought a way to represent this creativity, which is routinely unacknowledged and deeply unfashionable as a subject for artists. In her first performance, 'Drawing on a Mothers Experience' she made a 'painting' by telling the story of the night one of her children was born through the food that was consumed by her before and during her labour, producing this food and using it to make a painting on a bed sheet and black treacle, steak used to 'print', milk, jam. 
The resulting work bore obvious and intentional comparison to an abstract expressionist work specifically Pollock, she then obliterated it by sieving icing sugar over it and rolled herself up in the sheet. All conducted with much humour and joking and audience interaction. Baker making a point about traditionally male 'work' of painting by making a painting which far from being a commodity is merely the residue of a creative act, ready to be washed and dried. An Edible Family in a Mobile Home - baker constructed a family from cake and icing and placed them in a caravan. A performance in her own home, the audience invited into her kitchen to watch her discuss menial domestic tasks and what she enjoys about them, for example the freshness of washing cabbage leaves, the fun of icing (she poses on a revolving cake stand). After each action she pins a symbolic item onto her white coat so that she is a living relic of her day at home. Apparently casual yet very choreographed. Also a performance in which she gets into a bath of chocolate custard. Baker drawing on the work of 1970s feminist artists who made the domestic their subject to point up the inequality of domestic work and its consequent effect on themselves as artists. However in her re-appropriation of this work focussing on food, eating and the artists body, she added humour making it substantially different.

!!Medieval & Gothic Art.
!Balkenol, Stefan.
German artist who carves large, often over 3 metres high, figures from whole tree trunks. The figures are carved roughly and all the chisel marks are visible, no attempt beign made to smooth the surface of the wood. the sculptures are painted. Balkenol has made site specific work such as the 6metre high figures he placec on wooden pile in the river Thames in London. His carvings rfefer both to folk art and to historical plychromed sculpture whilst the figures are clearly of their time.
!Ball Hugo.
Dada artist and prominent member of Cabaret Voltaire. Ball instigated many of the collaborative performances and wrote many of the poems and scripts for them. He performed regularly.
!Balla Giacomo.
Italian artist born 1870s died 1960s member of the group of futurist artists centring around Boccioni and Marinetti in Italy in the 1920s and 30s. Balla chiefly a painter pursuing with obsession the ultimate means of depicting speed. A portrait of a dachshund walking with spoke like lines emanating from each of its legs particularly memorable.  
!Balthus.
Painting around the turn of and early 2oht century, died 1980s, Balthus was interested in the domestic interior and in young women and girls. His paintings have a restrained palette and a dream like quality, a feel of quite afternoon about them. The features of his female sitters are flattened and idealised. Balthus continued to paint until he was a very old man. 

!!Medieval & Gothic Art.	
!Bank.
British collaborative artists John Russell, Millie Thompson and Simon Bedwell, born in the 1960s, worked as Bank in the 1990s until Russell left in 2001. Thompson and Bedwell continued to work as Bank until 2003. Bank produced a large body of painting, sculpture, printed matter and texts as well as curating several seminal group exhibitions. They directed their attentions at the art world making ironic and satirical work underpinned by a detailed knowledge of and love for art history, a sense of humour and a disrespect for the establishment. In fact this last could be seen as Bank's trademark. 
Bank painting spanned a range of styles from highly realistic still life featuring the detritus of their studio painted in old master style, 'bad' figurative painting, 'serious' abstraction
still lives, self portraits. They explored the world of painting as iconoclasts. Most exhibitions accompanied by a printed text a polemic on the state of art, of the world and of artists. Irreverent and downright rude, those (especially their targets) who failed to get the joke found nothing in Banks work for them.
Bank Newspaper was self published for 10 years, featuring news about the art world, drawings and photographs on a tabloid model. Slanderous, even libellous, the paper had several favourite targets and no one was safe from their cruel wit.
In 2000 Bank published their on going fax back series as a book. For several years they had been returning press releases to the gallery that sent them, edited, corrected and with suggestions. These served to highlight the pomposity, inaccuracy, bad grammar and inflated claims made about artist by their dealers to hilarious effect. 
Curated exhibitions the first of which was in a bank giving the group their name were mostly held in Bank's studio in London's East End. Other shows were titled galleries poo poo, cocaine orgasm featured a life size self portrait sculpture of the group members on crucifixes?? Also skip By who? Lolly Batty, etc They showed a group of old master still lives in Cork Street and a group of abstract paintings featuring poured paint and some screen prints with text. In 2003 Thompson and Bedwell posed semi-naked for a series of photos dressed as classical figures from paintings but using their untidy studio as a background and anything to hand as costumes and props. 
!Banner Fiona.
Banner is a British Artist born in 1965 who uses text as her material, first coming to prominence with a work called ' the Hun t for Red October' in which she wrote out the entire synopsis of the film of that name by watching it, and then typeset this and printed it on paper in landscape format on a large scale. The net effect was of a text that could be seen to represent the entire film but was not really possible to read due to the line length, once the reader got to the end of a line it would be necessary to walk back along the work  to find the beginning of the next. This produced a tendency to read vertically, skipping form line to line, thus the viewer produced their own reading of the text which gave the impression of the film. 
Banner followed this with several similar film synopses works, culminating in The Nam, a huge paperback book which contains her synopsis of the film Apocalypse Now. She also made an audio work of her reading this synopsis, which is produced in a flat vocal style rendering it difficult to  listen to, but its strength lies in the effect t on the listener...etc. Banners work has most usually referenced film, the book and the printed page, but she has also made work about songs and music, including bob Dylan song a large work on canvases. The words of the song are spelt out in large stencil style letters on un primed canvases 3 metres high, then cut out so that the canvas is mostly gone. The song stretches over several canvases.
Latterly Banner has made several sculptural works in which she produces three dimensional versions of punctuations marks in particular fonts. These are carved from polystyrenes and painted or actually cast in bronze. She was a turner prize nominee in 2002 and showed a number of works dealing with pornography, including some in which she removed all the text from a narrative leaving only the punctuation.

!!Medieval & Gothic Art.	
!Bar Amotz Guy.
Israeli born artist born 1970s living and working in London. Bar Amotz's work deals with sound and music. He makes sculptures that often incorporate speakers made from household objects, or especially made fibreglass structures, which have a hard core futuristic look about them. A giant karaoke machine which could be entered by the viewer. An amplifier which is worn as a back pack housed in a rigid fibreglass lacquered shell. Bar Amotz sometimes uses metallic car spray to finish his objects which gives them an ambiguous but functional appearance. In 2003 he made a machine to be worn by a dancer whose movements triggered various sensors, thus the movement created the music.  
!Barker Sally.
British artist born in 1960s who assumed an alter ego Tela, who was a goddess or warrior queen. Barker performed as the goddess sitting on a throne, with acolytes, dressed in a cross between Roman and Viking costume, looking a bit like Boudicca, with gold sprayed greaves, wristbands and a helmet..

!!Medieval & Gothic Art.	
!Barney Matthew.
US artist living and working in New York born in 1960s. A sports enthusiastic, super fit from working out and rock climbing, his prolific work includes video, film, and sculpture. Came to attention at Documenta Kassel in 1993 with a performance Drawing Restraint in which he was tethered to the back wall of a squash court with a rubber bungee and suing his own strength to get himself to the opposite wall, made a drawing on that wall. He also scaled a lift shaft in a multi storey car park naked, using climbing equipment, with a surgical/machine tool implement inserted in his anus. These performances documented on video. 
They are followed by the sculptures made from petroleum jelly. This is known both as a medicinal aid and a lubricant with sexual associations, and barney used it to cast sculptures of pseudo gym equipment, rendered very realistically and shown in refrigerated spaces. Like and perhaps even in a tacit acknowledgement of Joseph Beuys use of lard, Petroleum jelly became a significant element of barneys later work. The sculptures have a strange atmosphere - they seem made to be used, yet unusable, familiar yet mysterious, everyday yet perverse and slightly sinister and to allude to sexual practises.
Barney made a series of feature length films Cremaster I to V. These are shot in 35 mm to commercial cinema standards, but made by a tiny crew with the exception of the final film on a shoestring budget. The series explores the world of Barney symbolism, perversion, fetishistic, oneric, surreal. Started by making 2, then 1, 4, 5 and ends with 3. The films conceived as a whole and made in the order the artist wanted to over a period of 10 years. 
Cremaster 2 is set on an ]American football pitch. There is a long sequence of synchronised marching shot from above on a crane and from pitch level., featuring women dressed as glamorous grown up cheerleaders moving in tightly choreographed formation. They begin at the edges of the pitch and gradually from the shape of ? one of the Cremaster logos. Jewelled harp?
Cremaster 1 is filmed on a frozen lake on which a kind of snow fort has been constructed
4 is filmed on the Isle of Man and take the Manx symbol the three legs of man and the annual TT motorbike race as its main subject. Barneys character is a kind of fawn with red furry legs and pointed ears, as well as a kind of goblin and a peasant girl and her parents. There are other exotic creatures and all have strange mutant genitalia, introducing a consistent theme throughout Barney's work. Much is filmed on giants causeway, and there are shaking giants footsteps. Barney says the film is based on a Celtic myth of a pebble being thrown into the sea and becoming an island and something to do with the giant of the causeway. 
5 set in the Guggenheim museum in new York and in the interior of an airship with stewardesses in very chic uniforms seated round a table with a large display of grapes. One of the stewardesses is under the table on a small plinths curled round the tables pedestal holding on precariously while trying and succeeding in making a hole in the table cloth above using hairpins and then gradually pulling grapes through. The grapes are red and white. She eats the grapes. This is intercut with animated film of grapes in lines forming various symbols. In the Guggenheim Barney, dressed as a Scottish piper in full ceremonial dress slowly ascends the outside walls of the huge spiral stairway using climbing ropes and shackles. On each level different people and groups are encountered, for example a long line of can dancing women who place one extended leg on the edge of the balcony for barney to crawl under, an extremely beautiful woman, and a small child.
Segment in empty warehouse with Norman mailer and lots of water and Ursula Andress in black jewelled outfit. 
V is shot in the G??baths in Budapest, and features an opera singer. It is acknowledged be the least successful of the 5 and is the most ordinary in a sense. Barney wanted to make a film about singing. Features Ursula Andress
3 the most recently made is shot entirely on a set and features the Chrysler building in New York. The film opens with a long sequenced involving a hermaphrohditic almost dead person recently disinterred from a living grave being deposited in a black sedan in the centre of a hexagonal room which also contains a crow. The arena is eventually recognised as an empty floor high up in the Chrysler building, and the corpse to be Gary Gilmore. As we imagine the crow to be pecking out the eyes of whoever it is in the car, 5 large apparently driverless cars emerge from the corners of the space and begin a kind o f ballet, repeatedly hitting and gradually crushing the black sedan until it is no more. Bar staffed by a stereotypical Irish comedic barman who in pouring Guinness becomes overwhelmed by foam. The main character (barney) is a construction worker on the building involved in steel erecting of the top structure.
He is a young handsome Irish character who has some mysterious secrets, revealed when he visits 2 'doctors' in a room in the building who reveals under his tattered clothes which appear to be Masonic - a leather apron with Masonic items in the pocket his mutant genitalia. He excretes a petroleum jelly 'turd' which immediately mutates. This is a scene of intense peculiarity.
As the top of the building is constructed the man is involved in a kind of wedding at the pinnacle, under a floral arch with ribbons which are finally unfurled and thrown over the side of the building to float down until they are caught by the workmen and drawn in throw the windows in a carefully choreographed order to create a spiral of ribbons on the exterior of the skyscraper as if it were a  giant maypole. one the pair who is married has their head crushed by falling steel.
Barney says that this film brings all the others together and is about nationality and origin (he being the grandson of an Irish emigrant labourer who did indeed work on the Chrysler construction crew). The mutant genitalia, gym equipment and logos and symbols of all the films relate to the title of the sequence, Cremaster. The Cremaster is a muscle located somewhere between the scrotum and the anus, and it is somehow significant in both sexual performance and procreation, but most people are unaware of it. Barney treats the muscle as if it were one that could be worked, and as a metaphor for his own ideas about creativity and the individual.
!!Medieval & Gothic Art	.
!Bartolini Massimo.
Italian artist born second half of the twentieth century.
!Basquiat John Michel.
Graffiti artist from the Bronx in New York, Basquiat was born in 1973 and died in 1998. He was discovered by a dealer and began to paint on canvas. Fostered by his patron dealer who provided him with studio and materials. Basquiat became hugely successful and his work commanded high prices. Feted by the art world for his work, his looks and his rebelliousness. He used graffiti tags combined with idiosyncratic signs and symbols creating a way of making a painting that successfully combined street and afro Caribbean consciousness with abstraction. The outline of a crown a reference to his own self-determined kingship, was his signature, the skull often appeared. Mostly painted in black and bright colours on white ground, drawing in outline and rarely filling in, sometimes used white on black to give a blackboard effect. The apparent naïve style used to issue a series of commands and demands on the world and his viewers, which are both egotistical and irresistible. Perhaps unable to cope with his success h became addicted to heroin and died of overdose before he was 30. Andy Warhol befriended Basquiat towards the end of his life and they made large collaborative paintings together. Basquiat later mytholgised and feature film of his life made.

!Batchelor David.
British artist born in 1950s who uses coloured acrylic sheet to make strange utilitarian low trolleys in a multitude of colours which are then arranged to make an installation, or stacked to make a tower of coloured light.
!Batty Lolly.
UK artist born in 1960s made a series of abstract sculptures of three dimensional polyhedrons, carved in polystyrene and painted to a smooth and apparently solid appearance so that they appear to have been carved. A kind of solid waist high diagrammatic snow flake in appearance, very smooth, clean and white.
!Baxter Glen.
British artist born late 1930s, cartoon drawings accompanied by text of events, happenings and people that illustrate a typical Englishness with a self deprecating humour, and a surreal, slapstick narrative. These cartoons were used by advertisers in the 1990s and Baxter's work although anonymous became extremely widely known. 
!Beardsley Aubrey.
Born in 1830s, died around 1900, working in 1860s in London and paris, Beardsley's finely executed an intricate art nouveau graphic style has become instantly recognisable. Illustrated Oscar Wilde's book of the tale of Salome, and made a large number of pornographic images. Influenced by art of China and Japan as were many at the time, Beardsley's characteristic flat highly decorative black and white pen and ink drawings used patterns on textiles and clothing of his subjects to great effect, and he was an extremely skilled draughtsman.

!Bechler Berndt and Hilla. 
German husband and wife working together born around 1950. their work became known in the 1980s series of black and white photos of industrial sites, silos, water towers etc. that imbue the subjects with a formal sculptural quality notable for absence of any form of life.
!Beckman Max.
German painter working in the 1920s and 1930s. Beckman painted in an expressionist style, choosing the people and social spaces of the day, in pre war Berlin. A self portrait of himself standing glaring at the viewer wearing a large black overcoat is particularly characteristic. Beckman used a lot of black to outline his figures. 
!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Beech Amanda.
British artist born 1970s.
!Beecroft Vanessa.
US performance artist who uses professional models to make her room sized installations. The models are chosen for their perfect bodies and dressed alike, usually in underwear or naked but always wearing shoes and make up and usually wigs. In an early work they are wearing yellow bobbed wigs and wearing items of school uniform. A more recent work has natural hair but identical face and body makeup to give them an ethereal gleam, and high heels. The models stand sit or lie, however they feel comfortable, for the duration of the work usually 4 hours, and may engage the viewers eye or not as they chose. They do not speak. Although the viewer is free to wander among them none does as taken as a group this experience is intimidating. This of course is Beecroft's intention, to show that the supposedly objectified naked beautiful bodies of women can, simply by their presentation and context, be rendered very powerful permissively because of our objectification of them and our habit of looking at naked women for pleasure. The control of the situation is given to them by their very muteness, their make up and their beauty, traditionally seen as means of oppression. Of courser there are those that would argue that in appropriating and reusing these means Beecroft is doing no better, merely relying on our curiosity and our desire for vicarious pleasure.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.
!Benglis Lydia.
An American sculptor working in the 1970s, Benglis made sculptures from brightly coloured polyurethane foam, poured onto the floor and allowed to expand and cohere.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Beuys Joseph.
Born in early 1930s, One of the most significant German artists of the second half to the 29oth century, He was a professor at the dusseldorf academy for many years, where he had a huge influence over several generations of younger artists. Beuys was an eccentric character who always dressed in the same canvas hat and black canvas shooting waistcoat with multiple pockets. He is extremely prolific, working in all media but chiefly known as a sculptor, performance and installation artist who proclaimed that 'life is art' making his every action part of his work.
Beuys is notorious for the myth of a near death experience that surrounds him and although long since discredited has proved impossible to ignore. It was said that he had been shot down when flying for the Luftwaffe in the second world war over snowy mountains. His life was saved by local villages who found him and kept his body temperature up by smearing him in pig fat and wrapping him in felt. 
Whether apocryphal or not, this event would seem to have had a profound influence on most of Beuys work. His favoured materials are felt and fat. However Beuys personal lexicon is often impenetrable and inaccessible, although s
One of his best known works is Portrait of the Artist Talking to a Dead Hare, which was made for his first solo show in New York in 1979. Beuys sits in the gallery, his head covered in honey and gold leaf (also highly important symbolic substances in his work), cradling a dead hare in his arms talking to it. There is some reference to the city of riches and capitalism in the gold leaf
Politics are a major feature of Beuys work, he is a socialist and an anti-capitalist, (although some claim that he is more of a reactionary). In fact he was sacked from his post at Dussledorf for inciting student riots, although this to is subject to speculation. In his second New York show he made a work with a live wolf. Beuys sat in the gallery behind a chain link fence separating him and the viewer, with a wolf for company to which he talked. The two stayed there for a week. The wolf symbolised the plundering and appropriation of the American wilderness and by his interaction with its spirit Beuys was trying to heal the wounds caused to the indigenous people. 
Sculptures the best known is his Fat Chair, a wooden chair with lard piled at a slant towards the back of it. He made a number of works from stacks of plate steel and felt which he described as batteries, some kind of life preserving power source. A number of vitrines which contained an array of mysterious but apparently utilitarian metal wood fat and felt objects. 
A series of performance had the format of lectures often conducted in a studio, in which he used blackboards to illustrate his talk, usually about the possibility of art being life and in particular to make his famous claim that 'Art is a Social Sculpture'. By this he meant that art could not exist with the interaction of the viewer, it was therefore subjective, and thus a social event. In this sense art was by extension political. The blackboards with their drawings diagrams and writing remained as a document of the lecture.
Beuys made himself a felt suit and various other garments and items, these usually had a cross cut from cotton fabric stitched onto them.
In 1895 Beuys showed in London. He installed rolls of felt the height of the gallery all round the walls and across the entrance, leaving only a small 3 foot high gap from the floor up for the viewer to enter. Inside was a grand piano. A total damped silence was implied by felt and experienced by the deadened acoustic.
VW camper van with a number of wooden sledges emerging from its door, linked by a harness.
5000 trees planted in a small town.
Beuys made many works on paper, using charcoal, paint, ink and in particular a particular brand of rust/dried blood red coloured paint the kind used to preserve sail cloth or paint door steps. The name of the paint and the colour was personally symbolic.
Beuys made a cast of an underpass under a road by pouring melted lard into it and then cutting it out, a work which must have influenced the British sculptor Rachel Whiteread.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Bick Andrew.
British artist born late 1960s makes wall relief which have depressions filled with wax and the whole then sanded to a smooth shiny surface.
!Bickerton Ashley.
American Bickerton was part of the Neo Geo group of artists who were working in New York in the 1980s. He made a number of wall mounted sculptures that consisted of closed metal boxes, shallow but projecting some distance from the wall, which he covered with stickers and logos, which gave the sculptures the appearance of both an item of luggage and a sign, its function entirely ambivalent. The stickers themselves were created by Bickerton and were generic types that had no actual reference in the real world to a company, an instruction or an advertisement. Bickerton also made a work in which a life-size rubber shark was hung from the ceiling with block and tackle, hanging from the ropes were also a number of small yachts fenders along with sealed transparent plastic film bags containing some kind of blue gel. This ambiguous work seemed to represent the sea (the blue gel) and its dangers (of yachts shipwrecked, shark attacks), as well as having a particular tough almost fetishistic aesthetic.

These spaces would be usable for their projected purpose, such is the attention to detail, but their remain functionless and empty, and such is their purpose. They are three dimensional facsimiles, all the more unreal for being so real. Bijl is interested in expanding the boundaries of 
!Bik Lisbeth and Van der Pol.
Dutch artist partners who make collaborative investigations of a sociological nature, setting up forums for investigation into contemporary art practises and in particular encouraging and facilitating cross-nationality projects and interaction. 
!Billingham Richard.
British photographer born around 1960 who made a series of photographs of his own family. These reveal an chronically alcoholic father who is either comatose or the life and soul, and an overweight mother who likes to laugh. The pair watch a lot of TV, drink, smoke and play with their other children and their dog. Their house is untidy. The photographs are graphic and show his family for what it is, revealing his parents asleep, happy, sad, playful, and drunk. The degree of intimacy that the viewer is given is startling, and the photographs ask the viewer to consider their own preconceptions and to think about making judgements based on appearance. For on closer inspection, a family that might have been considered dysfunctional appear also to be very happy and loving. The photographs are all taken in the house with ambient light, so often to get the image he want Billingham places the camera low down or high up, using any angle necessary to get the part of the room or the number of people he wants into view. They have an immediacy, an unstudied air, yet the series as whole is a comprehensive insight into other peoples life. 
Billingham followed the photographs with a film, made in the same way, which reveals that the father is evidently going to die of smoking related disease, and that the younger brother is very good at speaking to camera. Bellingham's work is characterised by its combination of casualness and thoughtfulness. It could not have been made by anyone other than a family member, for it would have been intrusive, vicarious. Equally it could not have been made unless the convention of fly on the wall documentary had been established and a fascination for the minutiae of ordinary live become commonplace. Billingham was one of the first artists to combine this with portrait photography.
!Birnbaum Dara.
US performance artist known for her performance in which she dressed as wonder woman.

!Bjornsdottir Olaf.
Blake Peter
British pop artist working from the 1960s onwards, Blake made figurative paintings that are very detailed. They often contain elements of patterning, decorative borders and are meticulously painted. Blake was chosen by the Beatles to paint the cover image of their Sargent Pepper album, depicting the band members dressed in uniform, amongst a group of fabulously dressed people. He often chose to portray British national identity in some way. He included a self portrait in many paintings - he always appears with long hair and a long beard. Blake is still painting in the 2000s.
Blake William
English poet and artist known for his visionary drawings and for his illustrated poetry books the Songs of Innocence and the Songs Of Experience. Made a drawing with watercolour of God measuring the world with a pair of compasses which has been much reproduced.

Also works on paper. Easy to interpret Bleckner's work as having a memorial quality - this the era when many leading figures in the visual arts died from AIDS related illness.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.
!Boccioni Umberto.
Italian futurist artist born around 1890 working in the 1920sadn 30s, Boccioni one of the leaders of this movement, which rejoiced in modern design and futuristic ideas, much of the work dealing with how to represent speed in two or three dimensions. A bronze sculpture about one metre high called simply 'Speed' of a striding man, with finlike structures representing trails of movement leading backwards from his limbs and the back of his head. Giving the figure a sinister warrior like quality which given the futurists association with fascism?? may or may not have been accidental.
A head of Mussolini, made by modelling a profile from a photograph and transferring this to a piece of wood and using the profile to turn the wood, resulting in a very odd but inspired object looking like a strange vase but with the unmistakeable profile of a man from every angle.
Also many paintings of the city (Milan) usually with lines to represent movement.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Boetti Alighiero.
Italian artist born early in the century working between the 1960s and 90s, Boetti made quite a lot of wall hangings using embroidery, chiefly maps of the world, but also tapestry panels showing the letters of the alphabet. Naming and writing were his interests and also the time consuming handiwork of embroidery and tapestry along with the very similar method of using the ballpoint pen to colour large areas. He also made a series of drawings in ball point pen of invisible words. Reading from key along the top and sides of the drawing it is possible tot discern the words in the drawings by referring to inverted commas on the grid of the drawing. The whole paper is coloured by intensive use of the blue or red ballpoint, leaving only the inverted commas white. Boettis work seems somehow anti-art, using craft techniques and the tool of the scribble the ball point pen.
!Bohm Katrin.
German artist born in 1970s who made social environments as gallery installations. Bohm designed a colour coordinated café/meeting/waiting space, and with various printed paper items, paper cups pens to match, and placed literature and other printed material in the space. An anthropological sociological interest is the main part of her work in which the viewer is made into a participant. 
!Boltanski Christian.
East? German? Artist who made large scale installation and site specific works particularly interested in socio-political issues, in the memorial, and in the legacy left for the living by the dead. One work was sited in a gap in a terrace of houses, a bombed out house never rebuilt. Boltanski discovered the names of the people who had lived in the house and inscribed them on the walls which still retained details of the interior of the house such as fireplaces, wall paper and paint. 
Leather suitcases WW2 aesthetic.

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Bolton Nick.
British artist who has made installation and video, born in the 1960s. A telephone box with etched glass has a sensor attached to the mouthpiece. When someone speaks on the phone the light in the phone box becomes brighter or dimmer related to the pitch of the speakers voice.

British artist born early 1960s painted abstract works often using blobs and dots, investigation paint and surface and simplification of colour. 
!Bonnard Pierre.
French painter 1890s known for his domestic interiors painted in warm and subtle shades with soft dabbing brushstrokes, and his attention to the pattern and detail of the textiles in the room as part of the whole composition. A woman seated at a table at the top of the canvas, the table extending towards the bottom, a solitary dreamlike quality of week day afternoon to the image. Perspective in Bonnard's work always slightly odd, elongated, seeming to be designed to draw the viewer in and give the impression both of welcome and of distance.
!Bontecou Lee.
Working in the 1960s and 70s Bontecou made wall based constructions from metal, acrylic sheet, perspex and rivets. Often these were flower like but due to their materials their appearance was more hard core, more like an exploded aircraft fuselage.
!Borland Christine.
Scottish artist born around 1970 who made a number of works featuring hand guns. She displayed a police blanket with a bullet hole in it. She presented a caravan in a gallery which contained some evidence elf a crime or the suggestion that a crime could be committed, with a handgun lying on a table. Borland's work displays an interest in how material evidence can be represented as an art work and in how that visual evidence can be used by the viewer to construct a narrative. 
!Bosch Hieronymous.
The Garden of Earthly Delights is an extremely well known painting. It is a small triptych approximately 70 cm tall with a squarish central panel and two rectangular side panels. Bosch's Painting depicts paradise to be an extraordinary place, with dreamlike creatures and objects, some beautiful pink glass spheres, some castles and towers. It could be said to be one of the precursor to the fantasy and science fiction form for its fantastical creations are wholly of the imagination. The painting is very detailed in its composition and painted with the smallest of brushes. 

!!Arts & Crafts Movement.	
!Botticelli Sandro.
Italian painter working around1540 best known for his Nativity and Venus, and also for a drawing cycle depicting the various levels of heaven and hell. The Venus is one of the most recognised images in the western world, and beyond thanks to its frequent use in advertising. The very beautiful Venus is standing in a giant clam shell which floats on the sea, just away from the shore. She is naked but her long hair covers her pubic area. She is surrounded by cherubs who pull the clam shell along by blue ribbons. Pale pink roses fall around her from the sky. The whole painting is rendered in luminous pale colours, blues, pinks and sand colours. 
The nativity a small painting about 50 cm tall is set in a forested landscape, with a timber and straw shelter in the centre of the picture which houses the Holy family. The shelter has no walls. The Mary is seated with Jesus on her lap. Joseph kneels beside her. The shepherds are there. The ox and ass are in the shadows at the back of the stable. Above the thatched roof are angels, arranged in a row, some blowing trumpets or playing harps, their feet dangling, their softly coloured red blue and purple robes falling in folds around them, with gilded belts and halos hovering. Above the angels heaven can be seen though breaks in the clouds, and there are cherubs everywhere. Below the stable is a twisting path with leads to the bottom of the painting crossing horizontally, turning and recrossing, until the flames at the mouth of hell are reached. All along the path are devils poking out of burrows enticing lost souls, they have black skin, horns and forked tails.
The drawing cycle shows the different levels of purgatory and the crimes associated with them and the particular torture or punishment. Each level is then shown in detail with souls in torment suffering accordingly. The route to heaven is then shown culminating in the entry in to heaven, which is a beautiful garden. The drawings are made in dark brown ink and are very detailed. They were originally presented as a book.  

!!Ashcan School	.
!Bourgeois Louise.
Bourgeois is an American sculptor born in the 1920s. She has had one of the longest careers of any female artists of her generation. Bourgeois has made works that are both intimate and monumental in scale. She is ahs made carved and cast sculpture and installations. Notable her carved marble works of the 50s and 60s often table top, organic curvy shapes suggestive of the inside of the body or of plant growth. One in pink marble. Installations include room structures that have furniture and objects in them. Her work has a strong female sensibility. Most recently she has made a monumental 30m high bronze spider. Bourgeois has many repeatedly used motifs, the shoe, the spider etc 
!Bowery Leigh.
British fashion designer, club promoter and artist whose work defies definition but was very influential. Bowery died in the 1990s in his late thirties. Bowery always wore his own designs, usually involving heavily padded lycra to change his shape substantially, mask like make up often with big black eyes, rubber and fetish wear, extremely high heeled shoes and boots. His look morphed between male and female, sexual and asexual. His club night Taboo inspired a generation of people to dress up, and Bowery became an icon for many. Bowery exhibited himself in the window of a London gallery, a living work of art. 
!Boyce Sonia.
British artist working in the 1980s onwards who made work dealing with racial awareness and black identity. 

!!Ashcan School.	
!Bradshaw Philippe.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes large scale installations often using chain curtains. These have photographic imagery on them made using a commercial technique of using different coloured links to make the image. An installation in New York in a darkened room contained several curtains some with photographs on, one with a 19th landscape on, another with a Franz Hals painting on, place around the space in the centre of which was a trampoline. Video monitors displayed a montage of imagery and also a film of Bradshaw jumping on the trampoline naked, which has been a performance as part of the installation. The effect was that of multiple images, hard to grasp (the chain curtain images are hard to see at close quarters), disorientating but visually very rich.
