!!Ashcan School.	
!Brancusi Constantin.
Eastern European sculptor who lived and worked in paris at the end of the 19th century. Brancusi made a lifelong investigation of the plinth as a sculptural form. His works were often no more than elaborated plinth shapes, with similar forms placed on them. Plinth and sculpture becoming one. Brancuse worked in plaster, bronze, wood and marble. One of his best known works is Endless Column, a polished bronze column on a wooden plinth made of truncated three dimensional diamond shapes placed on top of the other, about 10 metres high. He also made a small carved head of a sleeping person which is so smooth and almost featureless as to almost appear to be a large stone. Brancusi's studio can still be visited in Paris, it has been preserved as it was.
!Brandt.
German photographer who made many photographic images that are know very well known. 

!!Ashcan School.	
!Braque Georges. 
French artist born around 1880 who along with Picasso was a major exponent of Cubism. Beginning as a painter of landscapes and portraits, Braque made a painting with a piece of collage in 1911 Still Life with Guitar, which was of the paintings which initiated the development of cubism. He depicted a table a bottle and a guitar as if there were no conventional perspective and represented them by flat planes and areas and shapes of colour, including famously a piece of newspaper. By this action he began a revolution in painting, and the possibility of making conceptual painting was born. No one had ever stuck a piece of found paper to a canvas as part of the image before. The newspaper signified the prescience of a newspaper in the still life, but was not a newspaper and did not look like one. However it was enough to create an image. Braque's achievement was to ignore conventional perspective and the realist reproduction of form and represent form by other means. Including collage, using old tickets, printed paper (in one painting he uses paper printed with a rattan pattern to signify a cane chair), in another a piece of wood effect paper for a guitar for example. He made many such collages often of bottles, tabletops, wine glasses, with the planes of the objects opened out and repositioned, before going on, as did Picasso to attempt the same thing in paint.
The challenge in painting was to represent all the planes of three dimensional objects on a two dimensional plane, to think in terms of the surfaces of a cube for everything. This route had already been followed by Cézanne, who was the most important influence on the Cubists. This was a radical restructuring of pictorial space. The resulting paintings were made in browns and greys initially, and Braque often used an elliptical canvas. They gave the impression of crystalline forms with recognisable elements attached, such as a head, an eye, part of a guitar, the side of a bottle, the front of a table, the back of a chair. 
Sculptures?
Gradually Braque began to make freer images, the effect of cubism had been to free the picture plane from the constraints of realistic representation and pictorial space. The surface of the canvas became the pictorial space, so that the painting was both a representation and an exploration of surface. 
!Bratby John.
British painter working in the 1960s and 70s, one of a group known as kitchen sink painters for their choice of domestic subject matter. Bratbys interiors showed his own home and family, painted with thickly encrusted painted often squeezed direct from the tube and with a palette knife, in an almost naïve style.
!Breakwell Ian.
British artist whose career began in the 1970s, Breakwell is best known for his written diaristic works. He made a number works with large amounts of densely handwritten text, often detailing his own life and observations about life. He also used photographs. In the 1980s he made a performance in which the audience was confronted with a stage curtain. When it opened they were faced with a painted audience and the back of a proscenium arch, and in fact they themselves were on stage.
!Breton Andre.
Most outspoken member and leader of the surrealists in Paris at the beginning of the twentieth century. Breton made paintings and sculpture, and wrote the Surrealist manifesto which set out to define a new kind of art for new times. His best known work is a clothes iron with spikes set into it. Elephant vacuum?

!!Ashcan School	.
!Breughel Peter the elder.
Dutch medieval painter 1400s, world famous for his instantly recognisable paintings of the seasons, for example Hunters in the Snow, and there is one showing the summer harvest, one showing spring and one autumn life. In each case paintings show a village scene, with everyday life going on, a large number of peasants, children, domestic and farm animals, buildings. There is activity in every corner of the painting, down to the smallest detail such as a person urinating, or a dog stealing some meat, a couple kissing, children being naughty, someone sleeping. The scenes are realistically painted for the time and are notable for their depiction of the lives of ordinary people with no imposed narrative, no moral or religious overtones, no theme other than the representation of life as it was. The colours, the drawing of landscape, vernacular, architecture , the detail of the clothing, of tools, house hold items, makes these works extraordinary documents of their time. Above all they present four very different atmospheres and moods. The paintings are so famous as to be instantly recognisable whether or not the painters name is known, and to be used on calendars and other merchandise, and in advertising the world over. Details from the paintings are frequently extracted and used in this way.
!Breughel Peter the younger.

!!Ashcan School.	
!Brietz, Candice.
Artist born in 1970s
!Broodthaers Marcel.
The work of Marcel Broodthaers occupy an eccentric personal world which centres around his Musée des Aigles, and the mussel shell. A Belgian artist working in the 50s and 60s Broodthaers was something of a surrealist, with his use of puns and irony, and juxtaposition of unlikely objects. Joseph Beuys work has similar visual qualities to that of Broodthaers, as does his creation of a fake institution and the didactic ( if ironically so) aspirations of his work. He created his own sometimes impenetrable lexicon of signs and symbols, and indeed signs were part of his work, he made several signs displaying diagrammatic objects such as a pipe, with 'fig.1', 'fig 2' etc underneath he signs were pressed sheet metal reliefs. He made a number of small sculptures and wall reliefs using mussel shells (possibly as an emblem of Belgian nationality) stuck together. The Musée des Aigles - French for eagle but also a pun on something else like epaulet - contained everything the artist had collected with eagles wings, such as military crests, logos and such like. All Broodthaers work has a spare, monochrome appearance.
!Brown Cecily.
British artist born in the 1970s Brown paints very large splashy, dribbly and apparently messy works with tightly controlled colours choices. At first glance these appear to be abstract expressionistic paintings but almost immediately they reveal themselves to be figurative. Hidden in the mass of brush marks and paint splashes is a meticulously constructed figure, or figures often in pornographic poses. The works therefore sets up an expectation into eh viewer and then almost immediately confounds it. It also makes an analogy with abstract expressionism and male sexual gratification. 
!Brown Glenn.
Brown is known for his excessively meticulous paintings that reproduce exactly other works of art, or more accurately that reproduce reproductions of other works of art. Brown scales up a reproduction, chosen from among many all of which are different in colour due to the printing processes. He then paints this reproduction faithfully in oil paint, using the tiniest of brushes, reproducing not only what the work depicts but also the actual brushstrokes, giving the painting a trompe l'oeil quality. In fact it is debatable whether Browns paintings are copies our trompe l'oeil since they do seduce the viewer into thinking that their surface is thick paint rather than flat and smooth. This is particularly evident in a series of de Kooning copies, where the thickly encrusted paint is entirely realistic. However on closer inspection the 'impasto' has a plasticised quality. Browns de Kooning series culminated in a number of painted 'lumps' - apparently lumps of paint palette scrapings which are probably what do Kooning used to have in his studio but in fact bronze casts painted by brown to give the illusion of paint scrapings. Brown subsequently copied Fragonard's Boy in a Hat, and girl on a swing, which he showed upside down, and a number of science fiction paintings by Chris Foss, which were the subject of a copyright legal action. Browns work deals with reproduction and its importance in the dissemination of the images, question whether what people know best is the image itself or its reproduction, and questioning the importance of the hand of the artist. His main idea is to make painting the subject of itself
!Bulatov, Eric.
Russian artist working in the 1980s and 90s who combines socialist realist imagery, blue skies, fields, with large text painted across the image.

!!Ashcan School.	
!Bulloch Angela.
British artist born in the late 1960s, Bulloch's sculptures and installations deal with the social structures of society, the rules by which we live and the deconstruction of art and image making. Bulloch's 90s works include a mudslinging machine which created a mud spatter 'painting' on the wall, a drawing machine which drew a blue spattered line along a wall. These machines were viewer activated in some way. Often a piece of furniture designed by the artist would trigger the machines to work if sat on. The machines would be presented in an environment which invited comptemplation, the artist would fill a space with bean bags made to her design, often large enough to seat groups of people. The bean bags symbolised communality, and were a striking alternative to the customary stand and don't touch regulations in galleries. Bulloch was one of a number of artists in the 90s who were exploring the relationship of the viewer to art but more particularly to the institutional context in which they found it. She questioned the social norms and the normalising structures in which we live by presenting such structures in new ways.
Her Rules works are an example of this - presenting rules designed for specific purposes as if they are general rules to live by, rules that may be written in a small book are put on a wall using transfer lettering, giving them a perverse authority
In early 2000s Bulloch made some small sculptures out of chewed gum which was then cast and painted. She continued her series of works made from pixel boxes, an innovation of her own which consists of a wooden box with one opaque acrylic face, inside which is a light source with the capacity to change colour infinitely. This is linked to a computer, which is able to control the change of light colours, using as its instructions of the changes the pixels in a video image, or a piece of music. Bulloch stacks the pixel boxes vertically in a variety of formations and also horizontally to create the effect of an illuminated dance floor and minimal abstract sculpture. In the latter case the pixels were controlled by sound. Bulloch often works with sound, and in 2003 she made a number of small sculptural objects, smooth amorphous shapes sprayed with white lacquer, which on handling revealed an inbuilt and incongruous means of generating sound, perhaps in response to light levels for example.]
!Burden Chris.
Burden became known in the late 60s early 70s for his performance work mostly involving physical endurance. In 'Shoot' he had a friend shoot him in the arm in the gallery at a specified time. The shooting was documented and like all Burdens works of the period the documentation became the work. He was not seriously injured. In Locker Piece he had himself locked in a left luggage locker at a railway station for 8 hours. In Freeway he lay in the middle of a freeway. He also had himself nailed to the bonnet of a VW Beetle through his hands and feet. Burdens was interested in making formal sculptural work using his own body and in the visual power of pain and danger. The purity and simplicity of the works made them very powerful. His later work he developed an interest in war and military capability though his pacifist beliefs and in environmental damage. A work involving hundreds of tiny model submarines, a large meteorite like lump suspended from the gallery ceiling with model railway track running all over it and in and out of it. A large and complex computer controlled machine for making and throwing paper aeroplanes.

!!Ashcan School.	
!Buren Daniel.
Buren painted lozenge shapes on buildings and architectural structures, making site specific works that use the lozenge shapes as a visual intervention to prompt a reconsideration of the scene presented to the viewer, and attempt to alter the reading of the picture plane, treating an environment as if it were no more than a photograph. The lozenges allude to signage and logos are entirely without function. Buren's work is as much to do with reconsidering landscape painting as it is do with installation.
!Burgin Victor.
American artist who made conceptual work in from the 1960s onwards with a strong theoretical slant evident both in his work and in his writing about art.
!Burr Tom.
American artist working in the 1990s onwards.
!Burton Scott.
American artist who made work with furniture.

!!Harlem Renaissance.	
!Bustin Jane.
British painter born 1960s makes severe abstract works that are both investigations into the effect of colour and pigment, surface and also into votive painting and the painting as symbolic or spiritual object of contemplation. Bustin uses natural colours, dark reds, blues, black white. There is never a figurative element in her paintings, merely subtle shifts in tone that may hint at a from. 
!Byars James Lee.
Byers worked in the 1960s onwards. One sculpture is a clear acrylic case, containing white sand, a white book, and a cream coloured (almost white) snake. the work can be recreated anywhere as long as the artists instructions are followed, he specifies the white sand, the dimensions of case and book, which must be made by a bookbinder and that the snake be as close to white as is available. 

!Cahun Claude.
French photographer working in the 20s and 30s, who made a number of self portraits printed very small, dressed often as a man without disguising her sexual identity.
!Caivano Varda.
Painter working in the early 2000s. Caivano paints still lives that have a look of late 19th and early 20th century.
!Calame Ingrid.
Calame's apparently abstract paintings are in fact take from the outlines of stains she finds on the street and in public places. She photographs the ground and then draws the outlines of the stains, then paints the canvas in subtle colours to create an abstract image from something very mundane and ephemeral.

!!Harlem Renaissance.
!Calder Alexander.
British sculptor working in the 1940s to 60s who became best known for his kinetic sculpture and mobiles. One of the very few artist to have used the mobile successfully as a form in sculpture. Calder's sculptures generally use circles spheres and triangular shapes, often the triangles have curved sides, and rods. They are metal, painted typically in black white with red yellow and blue. 
!.Callan John
British sculptor born in 1960s who pushed paint through pin pricks following the line of pages of text in an open book to create painted text. Built a small house or dog kennel out of bricks and mortar. 
!Calle Sophie.
French artist born in the 1950s, Calle has made a large body of project and research based work. Her Following Piece is perhaps her best known work. In this performative project, made in Venice she selected a man to follow for a month. He never became aware of her constant presence until the project was over. Calle adopted the techniques of undercover surveillance in her project and kept a detailed diary with photographs, which she made into a series of text panels and presented as a documentary installation. For another incognito work, Chambermaid, Calle got a job as a chambermaid in a hotel. She searched the rooms and luggage that she cleaned and made lists of what she found and what she deduced about the occupants of the room - the kind of people they were, what their occupations were, why they were staying in the hotel. She photographed the rooms and things in them that interested her. This work was presented as text and image on the wall. Calle's interest in these works was in what we can discover about an absent subject from the visual world that surrounds them and in how we can reconstitute that subject. This process gives rise to an intense awareness our own subjectivity which is included in the final work by the use of her own voice in the text of the work. The viewer of the work is made aware of their position as voyeur which they share with the artist.
Calle subsequently continued her interest in subjectivity but began to work with people rather than observing them unseen. The Blind involved asking blind from birth and those who had lost their sight since birth to describe images. These descriptions are presented in place of images on the walls of a gallery giving a privileged place to the images of the minds eye, images that would never normally become concrete.
For a similar work made with Museum attendants Calle asked those who worked in a certain museum to describe the paintings that observed daily in the course of their employment when those paintings were not present because they were on loan. The resulting descriptions were placed on the wall in the place of the loaned exhibit. The discrepancies in the descriptions made the viewers subjectivity very obvious; descriptions of the same work would vary greatly, placing an object in a painting that was not there for example, or adding something new to a painting, changing the composition of the colours. 
!Callery Simon.
British artist born around 1960 Callery made a series of white paintings of banal urban landscapes in the early 1990s which relied on impasto and tiny amounts of colour in the white to create the almost intangible image.

!!Harlem Renaissance.
!Canaletto.
Italian artist who was working in the mid sixteenth century. He is world famous for his panoramic scenes of Venice, the grand canal and its palazzos with water traffic passing, painted in very accurate detail and bathed in a golden sunlight. He subsequently spent some time in London and painted the Thames river front in a similar way, including the Royal Naval College at Greenwich, St Paul's Cathedral. Canaletto's painting are among the most frequently stolen in the world
!Canova.
Classical sculptor - his Three Graces - 5 foot high white marble of 3 women in flowing robes holding hands in an circle, was stolen.
!Cantor Ellen.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes video and drawings.
!Caravaggio.
Italian artist classical painter who invented chiaroscuro

!!Harlem Renaissance.
!Cardiff Janet and Georges Bures Miller.
Canadian artist and musician/sound engineer partner working in from the 1990s onwards. Cardiff made a work for the Whitechapel Library in London which took the viewer on a walking tour - the viewer took a personal cd player which directed their steps and used sound to interpret and accompany what they were seeing. She made a gallery installation with a choir in which the choir members, performing a medieval choral work, were recorded individually, and then played through individual speakers placed on speaker stands at ear height in a circle in the gallery. Thus the viewer could choose how to experience the work by where they stand, and hear each voice individually. This gave a sense of what it is like to be part of a choir, and also a sense of the physical space which a choir occupies and how that space is experienced aurally. The gallery space is also articulated by where the sound is coming from - this varies according to whether the whole choir is singing, or just the sopranos in one segment of the circle, or a few of each type of voice dotted round the circle. The work give the non participant a participatory experience and inverts the performer listener relationship. 
!Carnegie Gillian.
British artist born in the late 1960s who began her career in the 1990s. Carnegie makes paints in two distinct ways. She paints her own and other female bodies, nearly always the back view mainly featuring the buttocks, in a realist way using muted lifelike colours. These paintings are small, about 40cm high. She also paints landscapes in extremely thick impasto, the canvases ranging from 40 cm to 150 cm high. These landscapes are usually woodland scenes, sometimes with lakes, rarely featuring people. The painting is sometimes squeezed straight from the tube. Close to the image is nearly impossible to read, in particular in the paintings which are predominantly one colour, often blacks or very dark colours, appearing as if at night. In these paintings the image is revealed by the sheen on the squeezes and smears of the paint, the texture and the shadow created. In other landscapes more typical colours are used, blues, browns and greens, but in all the landscapes the vegetation is rendered in detail, as is the bark of trees and the ripples on water. There is a sinister atmosphere to the images, although they appear timeless there is somehow the implication of a contemporary narrative. Similarly with the nudes, although a traditional subject, the fact that they are often self portraits coupled with her obsession with bottoms, gives a contemporary twist  to the sexual projection of these images and hints at both onanism and voyeurism
Carnegie subsequently began to paint floral still lives, using a much lighter palette although still restrained, in which the background and any vessels holding the flowers are painted smoothly and the flowers rendered in Carnegie's thick impasto and squeezings, a few judiciously placed ones making very convincing flowers. Carnegies paintings to date make an investigation into the history of painting styles and subject matters, subverting her traditional subject matter by making anachronistic and unexpected justaposition of style and subject matter. 
!!Harlem Renaissance.
!Caro Antony.
British artist who began his career in the 1950s becoming very well known in the 1960s for his welded steel sculptures. Died at the very end of the twentieth century?? Caro was the most successful of a group of British sculptors who made large scale abstract steel sculptors in the 1960s and early 1970s. These were either cut out of steel plate, or made of round and square cross section steel tubes and rods and I-beams. They were sometimes left to rust, but sometimes spray painted primary colours. Caro was one of the first sculptors to make semi-abstract works on such a large scale. It was really only Henry Moore who had attempted this in Britain but his work was much more or
Caro was influenced by the scale of ambition of his American contemporaries but his subject matter was initially the English landscape. His sculptures although they appear abstract depict landscapes. Caro used steel to make very open structures, often rather long and low, about twelve metres long and 2 metres high, that describe a kind of vista in space. One might be painted bright green, another bright yellow. Smaller works might be made from shapes cut from sheet steel put together. The ragged edge from the cutting heat tool or saw is often left unfinished.
In the 1990s Caro made a number of small steel sculptures of a chair based on the chair in Van Gogh's painting of his own bedroom. Caro became synonymous with a school of sculpture that was predominant in Britain for a decade, not least due to his years of teaching at St Martins school of art in London, then a leading sculpture school. This type of sculpture was mainly made by men and it began to be less popular as the effects of 1970s feminist activism in visual art took hold in the 1980s. Furthermore the status of the antonymous art object began to be questioned during this decade. 


!!Realism.
!Carter Tony.
British artist born around 1950. In the 1980s Carter made a number of installation works, based around an existing narrative or historical event and highly theorised, for which he created highly finished elements often including printed text and drawings. One such work contained the silver buttons from an eighteenth century character's jacket, for which he cast some silver buttons. Carter gave minute attention to the display of the elements of his installations, fabricating highly finished display cases and plinths.
!Carty Kirsten.
German artist born who makes intricate drawings in pen featuring women, landscapes, clothes, buildings and plants. The drawings have a decadent atmosphere.

!!Realism.
!Carvahlo Melanie.
British artist who is interested in how people conceptualise their world. In a work from 2002 she invited a large number of people from all walks of life to make a painting of 'home'. She displayed the resulting works in a gallery as part of an installation. Carvahlo also makes complex photocollages that include people buildings and brightly coloured exotic flowers and sunsets.
!Casebeere James.
Casebeere made a series of photographs in the late 1980s of scenes that he had constructed entirely from cut, folded and shaped paper. For example a rural scene with a river, trees and a canoe. This is photographed in black and right. There is not a particular attempts at realism or trompe l'oeil as in the later work of Thomas Demand, rather the scenes a combination of fairytale and cartoon, given a sombre seriousness by the use of black and white. This then relates them to early twentieth century American landscape photography.
!Catttalan Maurizio. 
Sculptor and installation artist who uses stuffed animals in his work. Hung a stuffed horse from ceiling in a horse sling, stuffed to look slumped and therefore very realistic, as if it were either dead or tranquillised? An effigy of the pope toppling to the floor pierced by a crucifix. A table football game for two teams of 11 to play simultaneously, a squirrel slumped at a tiny table, a gun on the table an obvious suicide. A small boy wearing a hooded sweatshirt sits at a school desk and has speared a pencil through his hand. Cattalan's works is often intended to be disturbing and provocative. 
A large format photograph of the back of the Hollywood sign reversed so that it reads the right way, apparently installed in a mountainous area, it is unclear whether a huge deconstruction was undertaken to make the photograph or whether it is digitally created

!!Realism.
!Caulfield Patrick.
British artist working from the 1950s onwards who is known for his very distinctive hard edged representative paintings. Caulfield's subject is architecture and the interior particularly the domestic or leisure interior. He chose to paint interiors or elements from interiors such as furniture, lighting, ceramics, and developed a way of making his paintings that he never deviated from. Caulfield takes a photograph and copies its most basic structure rendering forms with the minimum of line possible. He treats light and shadow as a solid object and draws it in the same way. Thus the light from a table lamp will be represented as a solid yellow shape, the shadow behind its shade as a black shape. All the objects in a painting are given equal importance, since every element is treated the same. The outlines are painted with a schematic, dramatically simplified line, in black and the objects, walls, floors, light fittings, furniture filled in bright colours. The black lines are vaguely reminiscent of the lead in a stained glass window, yet they also call to mind Matisse's black outlines. The acrylic paint is applied entirely flat, the edge of every shape outlined in black, the edge of every colour hard and smooth although not made by masking. Colours are every hue and shade, bright, sober, pale and intense.
Caulfield had several favourite items, the lamp and the vase among them. He made many small paintings around 50cm high of one or two items, as well as many of entire interiors on a larger scale usually two metres high or more. He favoured the portrait format. His paintings can be defined as still lives and interiors much in the manner of seventeenth century  Dutch artists, and he painted the aspirations of an equivalent social group in his own day. Modern design, the latest in home interiors and fittings featured in his paintings. 
Caulfield's best known works are some of the simple still lives, like a single lamp, a goldfish in a bowl, an ashtray with a cigarette, a vase, an oval mirror which somehow conveys the idea that it is reflective even though it is painted in black and white. The schematic, iconographic way these things are painted makes deceptively simple to look at. 
Of his larger works, perhaps the interiors of a Swiss bar is the best known. This interior is painted mainly in blue (with the usual black outline) which signifies the quality of light in the place rather than the materials of which the furniture is made. In this Caulfield uses another characteristic technique, he paints the photo mural on the wall behind the banquette seating realistically, in that it looks like a view of mountain scenery rather than a schematic. This is the only element in the painting that he renders in this way. This idea pays homage to Braque and Picasso, who collaged printed material or imagery onto their canvases as part of a cubist painting. There are other examples that make this more explicit, for example where Caulfield paints a piece of wood grain realistically (in direct appreciation of the Braque, who stick a piece of wood grain onto a canvas to signify a guitar. He also paints the pattern of textiles in an interior in this way. But only ever one element in the whole composition is painted realistically.
Caulfield never painted a person. 
!Cellini Benvenuto.
Italian renaissance sculptor and goldsmith who around 1540 made a bronze statue of the biblical David with which he attempted to rival Michelangelo. He also made a bronze of Medusa. Cellini worked for the Medici's who ruled Florence and he as well known for his skill in the techniques required to make precious objects. Particularly well known is an extravagant salt cellar in the form of a calm shell with Neptune and many sea nymphs. This was stolen in 2003. Cellini wrote an outspoken and lengthy autobiography as a vehicle for his evidently large ego, which was ultimately his undoing, as he fell into a long running dispute with the other sculptors of the Medici court about whose was the best statue of David.
!Celmins V J.
Artist born in the 1940s or 50s who has made a large number of paintings and drawings of the sky and the ground. Celmins paints on small landscape format canvases about 60 cm wide or slightly more, using very small brushes. Or makes a drawing of a similar size using pencil. In either case she will draw the most minimal view - a patch of dust on the ground with a few tiny chips of gravel becomes as important as if it were the surface of the moon. She draws the surface of the moon. A tiny part of the night sky becomes a deep blue painting g of tiny diamonds. Celmins work brings the minute and the gigantic together but the works have a deceptively modest unassuming appearance. They are painstakingly detailed in their execution. 

!!Realism.
!Cézanne Paul.
Known as the father of modernist painting, Cézanne was a French painter working in the mid-nineteenth century. He lived in Paris and the south of France in a house called Jas de Buffon in provence. The Provencal countryside featured in much of his work and Cézanne liked to work out of doors as much as he could. He also painted still lives and portraits. 
Cézanne was working around the same time as the impressionists, but his ideas took him on a very different route. He had no interest in creating an impression, in depicting light, in optical fragmentation of the image. Rather he was concerned with how the three dimensional world could be rendered on a two dimensional plane. Cézanne was one of the first artist to paint a representative image giving as much importance to the picture plane as to the illusory space of the painting. In his landscapes and still lives he considered how much of an object he could paint, seeing no reason not to paint the parts of an object that could not be seen, to bend perspective, to remake nature. He composed his paintings so that the picture plane seemed to come towards the viewer, using judicious touches of white to control the path of the viewers eye. It is in this that he was revolutionary and laid the ground for Picasso and Braque to develop Cubism, for as young artists they were astonished by what Cézanne had done. He gave them the idea that a three dimensional object could be rendered in two dimensions not by creating an illusion, but by unfolding its various planes and presenting them with no relation to the actual physical integrity of the object. 
Cézanne was also daring in his use of colour. He never covered the ground of the canvas completely, always leaving parts of it bare white to function as a part of the painting as a whole. He applied colour with an abstract sensibility, for its own sake as part of the painting as a whole, rather than as a part of a faithful depiction. He used the juxtaposition of pure colours to create shadows of other colours.
A number of paintings are immediately associated with Cézanne. The Bathers (a number of works bear this title) are a group of female nudes in a variety of poses, their bodies represented as planes in a landscape. The painting is brown and green the bodies flesh coloured. This is Cézannes best known and least understood work, and it is the work which has the most influence on other artists. His still lives of oranges and apples are also characteristic, as are some of his portraits, for example the woman with a green face (the green representing the shadow cast on her face by the hat she was wearing. Most of his paintings are fairly small, under a metre high.
He painted the land around his country home endlessly, including the water cistern, many trees including an almond tree in blossom, views from the house. He often painted his wife, including a notable painting of her in the bath under greenish water.
!Chadwick Helen.
British artist born in the late 1950s, died about 1998. Chadwick made sculptural installations and photographic work, often combining the two. Her work dealt with the female body and ideal beauty. One work consisted of light boxes on plinths with photographic images of luxurious and sensual arrangements of hair, ribbon and entrails, length of intestine for example, braided together. Chadwick often associated the visceral with the luxurious, the interior of the body with the its clothed exterior. She was regarded as a feminist artist and in common with other women artist of her generation produced much work dealing with the female body. Another work consisted of a series of wooden boxes of different shape and dimensions, produced to correspond with the size of her own body at various ages in her life. Thus the box for her birth is new born child sized, another is curved as if a child were curled up, others are standing adult sized. The boxes have photographic transfers in black ink of images of Chadwick at these different ages. They stand in a group. Around the walls of the space in which the boxes are installed are wooden panels with similar images with Latin texts running round the four edges of the panels. The texts describe the ages of a woman. 
The work for which Chadwick is best known is probably Piss Flowers. These are painted casts taken from the space made in snow when Chadwick and her male partner urinated in it. When cast as a positive this space is revealed to have a central column going down and a petal like circle surrounding it, made by the splashes of hot urine on the snows surface and the main stream melting deep into the snow. The male 'flower' has a much wider petal arrangement and a shorter 'stalk'. The female 'flower' is the opposite, a result which fired Chadwick's interest. The Piss Flowers are a nod to Duchamp's casts of the negative space of male and female genitalia.

!!Realism.
!Chadwick Lynn.
British artist who worked in bronze, making figurative sculpture from the 1950s to the 1980s. Chadwicks bronzes range from life size to very small around 30 cm high human figures. These are angulare, with stick like legs and flattened irregualar trapezoid bodies. The heads of the figures are often pyramid or cube shaped to denote female and male figures. Chadewick was interested in the difference between male and female forms body language. The bronzes mostly have a blue green coppery patina. 
!Chagall Marc.
French artist born in 1880 who made paintings and designed stained glass. Chagall is usually associated with images of people and animals flying. His narrative paintings are executed in a faux naïf style, with soft colours, and have a sentimental quality. He often painted images of love and marriage, for example a painting shows a bride and groom resting on a giant chicken in the sky, with symbolic objects of the artists Jewish faith in the background. As well as the chicken and the rooster Chagall like to paint horses, usually white with large green eyes and long eyelashes, again often in the sky. 
Chagall was commissioned to design the stained glass windows for a chapel designed by le Corbusier in the south of France.
!Chakur Bhupen.
Indian artist born around 1930, died 2003, one of the few Indian contemporary painters to become well known outside India. Chakur's figurative paintings depicted his life often intimately, and included sexual acts with male partners. But in the main Chakur showed India in a contemporary way unhampered by the history of Indian art but at the same time making it a part of his work, which shows a particular the influence of Indian miniatures.
!Chalkley Brian Dawn.
British artist born 1950s, Chalkley works as both a man and a woman as he is a cross dresser. Sometimes his work is authored by both Brian and Dawn. He uses video and photography to investigate identity. For example a film in which Dawn is seen in various dangerous scenarios, alone in isolated places having possibly been attacked. 
!Chamberlain John.
working in the 1960s US artist Chamberlain made sculpture from compressed dumped cars which he either retrieved from the compressing dump or took to be compressed. The resulting cubes were exhibited as sculpture. He sometimes extracted compressed parts of cars and combined them to make sculptures. The Spanish artist Angela de la Cruz's trashed canvases can be considered in relation to Chamberlains work.
!Chapman Jake and Dinos.
British artists born in mid 1960s the Chapman's are brothers who work together. They became known in 1993 for Disasters of War, a sculpture that recreated Goya's disasters of way as a 1:100 scale model. This grisly scene was presented on plinth the size of a table top, realistically made and leaving nothing to the imagination. Flayed corpses hang from trees, severed limbs are strewn about, headless torsos are propped up, heads stuck on bayonets. They later made a series of etchings based on this work in homage to the original.
Another early work was Little Death Machine, a working machine built on model scale in a vitrine on a plinth that presented a rubber model of a brain that functioned as a pump to spurt out white liquid. 
On one had the Chapman's work is deeply rooted in art history, politics and subversion with implied criticism of Western capitalism and of the failure of art to deal with important issues. On the other hand the artists delight in the puerile, in a childish humour and set out to deliberately and defiantly shock. 
It is the Chapman's life size sculptures of mutant children that are most notorious in this regard. These children, which have the appearance of shop window mannequins made in what has become their main medium of cast fibreglass and realistically airbrush painted, have genitalia on their face and smooth skin where their genitals should be. A girl might have a vagina as a mouth, or several orifices on her body where none would usually be. a boy may have a penis as a nose. The children are posed in apparently compromising positions and situated in a beautiful garden built of model material. There are trees and plants with large fantasy flowers and grass made from a material that resembles a scaled up version of the green sand-like material used as grass by model makers and model railway enthusiasts. The children appear to be frolicking in a strange Eden. The Chapman's have in this work as in all their work taken a basic human urge, such as the urge to kill, the sexual urge and its many perversions, and urge to power and represented it in order to confront their viewer with the possibility of discovering such urges in themselves. In this sense their work is highly politicised, but their use of shock tactics sometimes works against them. 
The life-size mutant children are in fact a scaled up version of a 1:100 scale model, and led the artist also to recreate details from the disasters of War as life size work. On this scale the latter seems less shocking. The Chapman's made a porn film featuring people with these same prosthetics genitalia attached to their bodies.
The Chapman's continue to make etchings and other works on paper, and to make large scale installations and models. Their McDonald's Concentration camp 2000 is a very large 1:50 model of a fictitious concentration camp no doubt based on a real one, which has McDonalds logos. The camp processes people, we see the tiny figures begin brought in, living in squalor, being killed and 'processed' in various ways. Much of what the model depicts is familiar to us not through experience but through fictitious depiction on film and television and the model relies on this to animate it. The work makes the daring if literal analogy between a fast food company bringing cheap food to the masses with famously no concern for their detrimental and social impact and the Nazis. The have also made larger scale sculpture again in their usual medium of cast fibreglass, realistically painted with an airbrush, which represents a kind of gothic heavy metal concentration camp monument with massed skulls, very black and dark.
The Chapman's most recent work is a darkened room full of apparent African tribal art, carvings in wood which are painted and have added grass, beads and teeth. The room seems to be in a museum, darkened to preserve vulnerable and light sensitive materials, which lends a familiar reverence to the sculptures set on plinths. There is a deadened acoustic. On closer inspection the viewer can see that the carvings all in some way feature the MacDonald's logo, the golden M, or represent a burger or Ronald McDonald the company's clown mascot. These are cleverly worked as 'African' looking sculpture, Ronald McDonald a totemic figure, the burger often the mouth of a figure, the M appearing as a decorative element. The ironic pairing of the colonial collection with the symbol of capitalism.

!!Realism.
!Chardin.
Sixteenth century painter of still lives, usually flowers and vegetables.

!Chetwynd, Lali.

!Cheung Lisa.
Korean artist born in the 1970s who makes photographic works often shown as slide projections.
!Chicago Judy.
American artist Chicago born 1950s changed her name from the patronymic to that of her birthplace as a feminist gesture in the 1970s. Chicago's work 'The Dinner Party' 1972 became a iconic feminist work. It is a narrow table arranged in a triangular format, with place settings for all the famous and unsung heroines of American life, for example Mrs Lincoln. The place settings are actually embroideries depicting plates and each one is made by a different woman artist. Similar to the way women make church kneelers. Chicago wanted to draw attention to the under representation of women's achievement by staging this imaginary dinner party of these great women, and to use traditionally female but culturally undervalued materials and methods to make it. She brought the women together my means of the dinner table because this is the means by which women had traditionally brought people together. The work came to be seen as representative of the struggle for recognition of women artists as well as in other areas of life of the time, and was influential for several succeeding generations of artists. 
!Child Andrew.
British artist born in the 1960s.
!Childish Billy.
British artist, poet and musician born around 1960. Childish has made a large number of naïve figurative paintings often of himself and his friends on found wooden boards, the sides of boxes, and other unconventional material. He is known equally for his poetry which is written with and makes a virtue of his dyslexic spelling, and the punk band which he fronts, Thee Headcoats. Working in this way since the early 1980s, Childish achieved notoriety beyond his cult following in the late 1990s when he was immortalised by ex-girlfriend Tracy Emin in several works, including her embroidered tent 'Everybody I Have Ever Slept With'. Since then Childish formed the Stuckists, a self proclaimed movement that promotes the idea that British art in particular the work of the artists that make up so called Brit Art, is simply 'stuck'. The Stuckists set out to critique it, by means of pamphleteering, demonstrating, fly postering and other public protestations and interventions. 
!Chillida Eduardo.
Mexican Sculptor born in the 1930s who made monumental steel sculptures usually site specific, often in natural environment. For example one of his characteristic M or comb like structures is placed on a cliff top to rust and acquire its orange red colour. 
!Chilvers John.
Born in the 1960s the British painter Chilver has made paint itself the subject of his conceptual paintings. He uses paint squeezed or piped in the way icing would be to decorate a cake, through a Square nozzle, to produce a three dimensional line on the canvas. He adds paint from a traditional round nozzle. The paintings depict simple scenes, for example a fence with a bird on it. Or a style with a clump of grass. The fence or style will be piped in square lines often black or dark grey, the bird in many colours of thinner round lines, the whole giving the impression of casualness and speed of execution when in fact the reverse is obviously the case. Chilver often uses puns in his title, for example style for both the object and the other meaning. He investigates the nature of painting the painted mark, without actually painting at all since the backgrounds are entirely flat and the paint never touched by a brush or worked at all. 
!Chodzko Adam.
British artist born 1960s. Looking for Jesus a work made in the 1990s. Chodszko placed a classified advertisement requesting people who look like to send him passport photos. Often they bore no resemblance at all. Chodsko made a work with Afghan refugees living in Hastings in 1990 in which he asked them to sort through and select from the archive of Cubitt, an artist run organisation in London. The selection was placed in a watertight box and buried below the high water mark on the beach at Hastings. Another work from 2003 was site specific, Chodzko placed a billboard in a field in Texas on which was printed the directions to a billboard at Cubitt in London, outside which was a billboard with the direction to the billboard in Texas. A kind of transatlantic endgame with the idea of encouraging a sense of place, geography, time and space. A photo of the US billboard displayed at site of UK one and vice versa.
Chodzkho's work usually socially engaged and often involving research, correspondence or mass participation.
!Christo and jean Claude.
Beginning in the 1970s French artist Christo and his partner Jean Claude began wrapping objects, to transform one thing to another, solidify forms, change a hard form to an apparently soft one, graduating to larger and larger objects. They began to work with fabric on a large scale, either wrapping buildings or the landscape. They used parachute silk, vinyl and other kinds of industrial fabric with the required properties. Several works exemplify what they do best. For example, valley curtain, made in the early 80s, a large orange curtain was hung across a valley somewhere in US probably. California. Running fence is a mile long fence of red fabric that follows the contours of a hill. A large number of table umbrellas were installed dotted over a hillside. The island skirts surround a number of minute islands, following the shoreline and floating on the water for 30 metres or so. As with all their work, the proportions of the intervention always take the view from a distance into account, since the work by its nature temporary finally only exists as a documentary photograph it is therefore made with this photograph in mind. Thus the island skirts were designed to be wide enough to be clearly visible in an aerial photograph. And the locations are very carefully chosen with the final photograph in mind. In the 1990s Christo and jean Claude completed their most ambitious projects, the wrapped Reichstag and the wrapped Pont Neuf. These structures were wrapped in a fabric that was light enough to fold and gather round the forms they covered, and bound with rope. the Pont Neuf was wrapped in a gold fabric. The resulting transformation was a triumph of planning, negotiation, construction, and there are still postcards of the work on every Parisian postcard rack
!Clarke Roger.
British artist born in 1960s who made large scale sculptures during the 1990s often site specific, from perforated lacquered steel, folded into clean geometric shapes.
!Clemente Francisco.
Italian American painter born in the late 1940s who was extremely successful in the 1980s. Clemente makes figurative painting and works on paper, as well as small scale sculpture and ceramics. His work shows his interest in Indian art in its colours and in its often spiritual themes. Clememte's main subject is the male body.
!Close Chuck photo realist.
US artist who began working in the 1960s. Close initially made photo-realist paintings but he is best know for his gridded painting. Inspired by the printing process and the way an image is broken down into dots of three colours, Close developed a way of placing a grid of squares of about 3 x 3 cm over the image he wanted to paint. Very often this would be a self portrait. He then produced the image by painting each square a different colour so that from a distance the image would be readable but close to the coloured squares would be predominate. In doing so he was investigating  both the effect of printing and painting, making a work that looked photographic, as painting had been trying to do, but that was neither painting in the most obvious sense or photograph. This was after all another way of doing what painting has often been concerned with, namely applying colours in such a way that an image is effective from a distance which inevitably means that it wont be so close up. All this work was made before the digital revolution; after this Close's paintings look like exactly like pixels. As well as paintings, Close also worked on paper in watercolours. He made a series of self-portraits using blacks and greys that emulate black and white photos. 
!Collins Anne.
US artist who makes photographs, for example of women in urban and rural landscapes, with a precisely controlled banality. 
!Collins Cecil.
visionary painter
!Collins Hannah.
Collins Susan
UK artist who pioneered the use of holograms in art in the early 1980s, Collins has contoured to be a the forefront of technological innovation in visual art. Recently she has been working with sound and with the internet.
!Collishaw Matt.
British artist born in the 1960s who made a large format photographs of a bullet entrance hole in a persons scalp. 
!Condo George. 
American artist born around 1960 who makes paintings that use art historical references, often nearly remaking a particular work but with Condos often fantastic twists and distortions so as to create an entirely contemporary version of a painting from history. The paintings use a variety of styles and usually bright colours, and although they have a clearly historical reference they often have a science fiction/fantasy reference too, sometimes they contain absurd elements. Condo also paints LP covers, turning them into large paintings around a metre high.
!Constable John.
An eighteenth century English artist who painted one of the most famous paintings of the English countryside, The Haywain. This shows a country scene in which a cart loaded with hay is travelling down a lane. The cart is very small in relation to the picture as whole as are the farm workers that are shown. The painting consists mostly of trees and bushes, thus is full of green browns and ochres. It is a peaceful image of a beautiful summers day with the harvest being brought in. Flatford Mill is another almost as well known painting, showing a house on a millpond by a river. Again trees and vegetation predominate with a strong sense of human presence sublimated to the landscape. A sense of the weather is in these paintings too. They seem to represent and idealised version of the countryside in the past that people value. 
Constable always used something red in his paintings, a tiny element of red that served to draw the eye in to the part of the painting that he wanted the viewer to e directed to. It might be the roof of a distant cottage or as in the Haywain, a red garment on a person on the cart. 
!Cooke Beryl.
British artist born in the 1940s, usually termed 'naïve' who paints women, usually rather large both in size and in personality, going about their daily life. The paintings are humorous and have a strong narrative. They are simple in their appeal and quite close to illustration. Cooke is extremely well known in Britain.
!Coplans John.
British artist born in the 1950s who makes black and white photographs of his own naked body and parts of his body. Coplans has exhaustively documented his aging body, which is a more or less average male body, including his genitalia, in detailed close up. The texture of his skin, the hairs, the wrinkles, nothing is left to the imagination. Coplans seems to be presenting the male body as it is as a counter to the over exposed female body. His presentation of the male body reveals its frailty, and significantly it is an entirely unsexualised presentation. 
!Corcia Lorca Philiipe de.
Photographer who takes images of the everyday and shows them in groups.
!Corot.
French nineteenth century painter known for his still calm scenes of the French countryside, often roads and tracks feature in his landscapes, and always trees, which he was particularly good at painting. Corot painted some notable snow scenes. The paintings are small, no more than 60 cm high.
!Counsel Melanie.
British artist born around 1960.
!Courbet Gustav.
French painter known as the first realist painter. Courbet painted the French landscape.
!Coventry Keith.
British artist born in 1960s who paints white on white paintings that reproduce famous paintings in oil paint without colour.
!Cox Stephen.
British artist born in the 1950s who made some carved stone tondos with classical imagery and inscriptions in the 1980s.
!Craig Martin Michael.
British American artist born in the 1940s who makes wall paintings depicted much larger than life ordinary objects - furniture, paperclips, tools, bicycles, lamps, globes, fittings, juxtaposed without reference to relative scale, at various angles. The objects are placed on a very brightly coloured ground painted with matt emulsion paint and outlined in black plastic tape, except for a period in the 1980s when Craig Martin used square sectioned steel rods bent to shape and welded to create the outlines. The wall paintings may fill a whole gallery or museum, or just one wall. 
Craig Martins earlier work made in the 1960s and 70s is conceptual. His best known work is the Oak Tree, which consists of a glass of water placed on a glass shelf above the viewers head and an accompanying framed text which explains to the viewer why the glass of water is an oak tree, by means of a question and answer between the artist and a fictitious interlocutor. The artist claims that it is by the authority invested in him as an artist that the glass of water is an oak tree, thus this is a work about authorship and subjectivity. He also made a work with a row of clipboards and plumb lines hung on a wall, a series of boxes with hinged lids placed on the floor, the lids taking progressively larger parts of the box until the lid was more box and the box more lid.
!Cranach.
German medieval artist working in the 1300s who painted an altarpiece that was shocking for the time since it accurately depicted the misery suffered by Christ on the cross, the pain and the physical degradation. Cranach also showed the plight of ordinary people of the  time by painting them with festering sores, deformities and other afflictions. He painted very realistically in quite bright colours. 
!Crash.
a group consisting of an writer and an ex graphic designer called Scott King who produced a magazine called Crash! Was produced in the mid 1990s and was notable for its mix of comment about art music advertising and consumer culture, and its political stance which was anti-capitalist, presented in a humorous and irreverent but highly intelligent way. Crash!'s poster of Cher the pop singer dressed as Che Guevara the revolutionary, pristine in black and right in the graphic style adopted by Marxist revolutionary movements, was widely successful. 
!Creed Martin.
Scottish artist born around 1960 who makes sculpture and installation from almost nothing. All his work is assigned a number and a description for the title, For example, Work No.XX: A door opening and shutting, Work No.XX: The lights going on and off, Work No. XX: One A4 sheet of paper screwed into a ball. Further works include a cube made of layers of elastoplast, a cube made of layers of masking tape cubes, a bulging protrusion from the wall of a room which is plastered and painted to be as part of the wall. 
The work is sometimes active, for example A work which consists of a room full of white balloons through which the viewer must push references Yves Klein's 'Void', and empty gallery space. The door that opens and shuts is operated by a door closer on a timed switch. The lights going on and off is similar (this work has also been 'performed' by creeds band Owada - whenever the lights came on the band played, whenever they went off the band stopped, as if the electricity supply for the amplifiers were linked to the lights. Or the other way around. Owada which can be described as making minimal conceptual music gained success as an art band in the late 90s and early 2000s with tracks such as One to A Hundred, which featured Creed speaking these numbers in order and the band playing rising scales one note per number. 
!Crewsdon Gregory.
American artist who makes photographs of staged events that appear to be potential crimes scenes, places where a crime might happen. There is something of the supernatural about the images, they have an unreality due to the highly controlled lighting and decisions made in the printing. A small town petrol station may be seen from above at night for example and the disposition of people and vehicles might seem strangely static, leading the viewer to guess at possibilities, perhaps to wonder whether there has been or is about to be an alien invasion. Another work might show a person looking at the sky, presaging some unknown possibly extra terrestrial event. The photographs are complex and ambitious in their construction of a subtly warped reality shot on location rather than in a studio. 
!Cross Dorothy.
Irish artist born in the mid twentieth century who make work from leather and fabric, a kind of feminist surrealism, for example a corset made from leather tanned from cows udders with the udders sticking out.
!Crotty Russell.
American artist born around 1960 who makes obsessive biro drawings sometimes on large sheets of paper, also in very large books, at least 80 cm high. The drawings are made by colouring in with blue biro, using the pen fro the purpose fro which it is least suited, on a scale which is plainly absurd. 
!Cruz Juan.
British artist born in the mid 1960s who works with sound and text. Cruz made an installation performance in which he translated the text of Cervantes great Spanish novel Don Quixote in real time over a number of days. He also made a work based on the Duende, an untranslatable Spanish word that describes the 'feeling' needed to perform flamenco. Cruz is chiefly interested in language and translation.
!Cucci Enzo.
Italian artist who made large scale drawings of classical nudes using an obsessive cross hatched style for which he became very well known in the 1980s. 
!Cullen Shane.
Northern Irish artist who made a large scale text installation for which he printed or stencilled or used transfer lettering to inscribe the letters written to him during a correspondence he had with political prisoners on remand in the H block, the notorious prison in Northern Ireland. These covered the walls of the exhibition space. 
!Cumberland Stewart.
British painter born in the 1970s
!Currin John.
American painter born in 1962 who became extremely successful in the late 1990s and early 2000s. Currin made his name by painting women with unfeasibly enormous breasts. The paintings are made in a mixture of kitsch and old master style. The women have exaggerated cartoon like features yet the colours used are entirely realistic. The women seem to be there to hold the male gaze and fulfil male desire but they are often painted in pairs, absorbed with each, ignoring the viewer. Currin also painted portraits of real people, still lives and interiors. His knowing and slightly anarchic use of virtuoso painting techniques and undeniable skill in painting and drawing allied with subject matter that would have been entirely unacceptable in the 1980s or early 90s brought him to attention. 
!Cyril Griffiths Brian.
British artist who has made a number of installations from cardboard, discarded materials, empty plastic containers and any number of banal materials. Griffiths space ship command module control panel made in 1997 was an adult sized version of a child's dream. Griffiths has made many objects that seem like things that the viewer thinks they could make themselves but the scale of them makes them much more than this. A world in which everything can be made from anything is created.
!Da Messina Antonello.
Italian renaissance painter who made a painting of Saint Jerome in his study showing the room as a construction that sits on its own in a landscape, giving us access through the open front wall. Perhaps what we are seeing is monks cell, consisting of two rooms linked made of dark coloured wood in one of which the saint is seated at a desk. 
!Da Vinci Leonardo.
Quintessential Italian renaissance artist, Da Vinci is often referred to as Renaissance Ma. Meaning that he defined the era. He worked as a court artist for the Medici family in Venice. The artist is perhaps the most famous in the world, or has certainly made the most famous painting the Mona Lisa, which has been reproduced by the billion, forged and stolen many times and still draws huge crowds to its site on the walls of the Louver museum in Paris. This painting depicts a woman shown head and shoulders turned very slightly to one side. She has on a simple yellowish gold dress a plain necklace and has brown hair. She does not look particularly wealthy. There is a landscape in the background. There is an inscription LHOQ below her on a painted scroll. 
What made the painting so famous is the woman's expression, in particular her smile. She seems to be looking straight at the viewer, and has a look of cool calm appraisal, the hint of a smile on her lips. However her eyes seem to be fixed on the viewer wherever they stand in relation the painting, leading to the myth that the painting is actually following the movements of her viewers, that she is somehow real. This is an extraordinary achievement for a painting which has come to symbolise great beauty. The term Mona Lisa has almost become an adjective to describe a beautiful woman
The myth attached to the painting has led to many copies being made and to the work featuring in other artists work, in advertising, in newspaper cartoons, in fact it is an image that has currency everywhere in the Western World and often beyond. Mona Lisa copies appear in fairgrounds with holes cut for heads so that people can be photographed as her. Automatic photo booths have the Mona Lisa as a backdrop or as a body for the sitters head shot. Copies of her with the eyes cut out have been used in films for comedic effect - someone behind the painting will look through the eye hole so that it appears that she is following a person around the room and Marcel Duchamp famously graffitied a moustache onto a reproduction of her.
The author of this image succeeded in painting a very beautiful portrait, but it is only one of his achievements. He made many religious paintings and altarpieces. A cartoon for a Madonna and child is particularly well known. He did a vast number of drawings from the models, paying endless attention to hands and feet, eyes or other parts of the body, drawing them separately to get them right. 
Da Vinci was a polymath and his interests extended to anatomy and engineering. He performed many dissections and recorded what he saw in drawings, expanding his sense of how to draw the human form this way as well as his medical knowledge of how the human body worked. Notoriously he drew a foetus still inside a woman's uterus.
Da Vinci's other famous work is a drawing of the golden section. A male figure is drawn standing with both arms stretched out horizontally, and one leg pointed outwards at an angle. The man has flowing hair. The ink is brown and the paper cream probably the effects of age on the drawing. Measured lines radiate form the figure, which represents the proportions taken from the human body used to make perspective and on which architectural construction is based. A copy of this drawing was placed on the Moon by the crew of the Apollo in the 1960s.
Da Vinci was also an inventor. He was interested in the myth of Icarus and built a flying machine.
!Dali Salvador.
Spanish, born in 1900 perhaps the most famous of the surrealist and a candidate to be the best known artist in the world, Dali's paintings are instantly recognisable, indeed details of his paintings. Melted clocks are instantly associated with Dali even though they are only part of one of his paintings. Dali's surrealism is fantastical. He created dreamlike landscapes filled with distorted objects, nightmarish scenes and apocalyptic wastelands and religious iconography. One painting shows mutant human bones that seem to have a life of their own in a grey green no-mans land, the aftermath of a war. Another shows the crucifixion from Christ's point of view, showing what he would have seen looking down from the cross, the whole a luridly coloured hallucination. A landscape with dead trees has clock faces draped on the trees and on the ground apparently melting and dripping. A giant red rose floats in the air above a desert. Lobster telephone. Dali's use of obvious symbolism and his lurid hallucinatory images have made him enduringly popular. 
His personal eccentricity has only increased his popularity and is difficult to separate from his work. Dali dressed as a dandy, often wearing white, and sporting a very long waxed moustache that extended beyond his face and pointed up. He lived with his wife Galia in a house in Spain at Figuera da Foz where the couple were host to many of their contemporaries.
!Dalwood Dexter.
!Dalwood Hubert.
British sculptor born in early 1930s died in the 1970s. Dalwood made carved, cast and constructed sculptures from stone, bronze, acrylic resin, acrylic sheet and metal. He also made a few paintings. His early works were totemic stone objects that owed something to Jacob Epstein. He began to make table top subcultures that were a bit like mini abstract sculpture gardens, cast in aluminium or bronze. He used tubular or square forms and wavy lines placed on a rectangular base that would be cast in one piece. He made sculptures from Plexiglas with metal wire threaded through. 
As he became successful Dalwood began to be commissioned and he made a number of public works, for example a construction in a park made from brushed steel tubes of a large diameter fixed vertically to the ground in a cluster, reaching about 5 metres in height, curving around at the top like giant geometric plant stalks. Or an egg shaped bronze sculpture about one and a half metres in length lying on its side with some detail on its surface.
!Dan Flavin.
American artist who worked form the 1960s to the 1980s. Flavin's material was the fluorescent lighting tube. He made very simple and considered sculptures by combining various coloured tubes in different ways and placing them on the wall, leaning the against the wall, or placing them on the floor. One work uses multiple bluish white tubes placed on the wall to form a crucifix shape. The intense cold light produced seems to describe a place beyond the earth. In another he leaned several tubes of different colours against the wall. The cables of the tubes are visible and form part of the work. Flavin thought of the works in the same way that a painter might - using light and colour to depict an atmosphere.
!Dant Adam.
British artist born in the 1970s who makes wry satirical cartoon style drawings about art, artists, and the art world. Dant's project is serious and comprehensive in its ambition to comment upon everything that happens but to make sure that art doesn't take itself too seriously. He began by making a weekly self published pamphlet, much in the vein of 18th century pamphleteers soapbox speakers. The pamphlet was had drawn and written by a pseudonymous character invented by Dant to represent himself (who was often shown in the drawings). It was a bit like a diary and featured topical news about art and advice written in mock serious style. It was photocopied and folded into a pocket sized booklet which Dant himself distributed wherever he went free of charge. He did this every week for several years, and it was the long term nature of the project that made it successful. Dant constructed a portable booth from which to distribute the pamphlet and became a presence at art fairs and museum exhibitions. By a simple weekly action Dant produced a major work.  
!Darboeven Hannah.
Artist working in the 1960s and 70s who made works using text. Darboeven was interested in catalogues, indexes and diaries. One work for example took hundreds of index cards on which the artist had written and displayed them carefully categorised. The text on the cards however consisted only of 'x's. This display of an organising system which contained no information served to make visual a practise which is in use in daily life but which in never actually seen and rarely considered. Darboeven's obsessive writing projects are always made by hand.
!Daumier Honore.
French artist 
!Davenport Ian.
British artist born in the 1960s who began to make the drip paintings for which he became known in the 1990s. Davenport poured liquid paint onto a canvas from the top, allowing gravity to take the drip to the bottom of the canvas in a perfectly vertical line. He continued in this way across the width of the canvas using different colours until he had achieved a striped painting made without a single brushstroke, or any touch of the artists hand. At first sight the stripes would appear to have been painted with a brush perhaps even using masking tape, but on closer inspection tiny irregularities in the pour and the quality of the edge of the stripes made it obvious how the painting was made. Sometimes the paintings were in sombre colours, others were rainbow striped. Later Davenport developed various ways of making the pouring easier (fixing the paint pot to a runner for example) and he was able to make bigger canvases and even to pour directly onto a wall.
Davenport also made paintings by pouring a coating of one colour of paint onto a canvas placed horizontally, and then adding in a stripe, a line, and arc in a contrasting colour by pouring directly into the wet surface. The two colours when dry formed a perfect flat shiny surface, in the way that the technique of enamelling can be used to combine colours. 
!Davey Grenville.
British artist born around 1960 who made very large cast and lacquered fibreglass sculptures in the 1980s and early 90s. Davey created abstract forms that had a functionality about them, like giant lids, plugs or covers, often round, sometimes pairs that seemed that they might fit together. These were made from technical drawings, perfectly constructed and painted in shiny cream, grey, or white. The sculptures were placed on the gallery floor, often on their curved sides rather than on any flat plane they may have had. 
!David Enrico.
British Italian artist born in early 1960s who made a series of very large embroideries mounted on stretchers in the late 1990s. These were taken from collages and drawings he made from fashion magazines depicting women in fetish wear, extremely stylised clothes, very exaggerated bodies, very high heels, masks, drawn in a geometric style often with a 1920s or 30s feel. Although the images were of women they depict a non-specific sexuality, the women often have large masculine bodies and may seem more like cross dressed men. David used woollen thread to fill in the figures with satin stitch, and due to the scale of the image often 2 metres high or more, the stitches sometimes needed to be over a metre in length. Sometimes the stitches represented rays of light, too, or in one case silver thread was used on a blue canvas to represent the tight binding on the body of a model.
David used anything from fashion, craft and design that he likes to create these creatures. One had a butterfly entirely concealing her face, like a mask. Another is like an African queen. They are decadent often sinful and sexual images, their materials (those of handcraft) at odds with the subject matter. He also made items of furniture and lamps. He continued to make collages and began to work on some photo collages in 2000 using photos of himself wearing coloured paper constructions - red and blue, black and white, something like the costumes designed by the dada artists for cabaret Voltaire. Other collages used images from magazines and often the background paper was cut in a certain way so that the found image could be raise from the background. He used brooches and other found jewellery and began to use carpentry, furniture making and decorative arts materials.
In 2000 David began to draw men, (although it was not really the case that he had been thinking of the characters in his previous work as women). A man in a top hat and a morning suit, some kind of dandy, appeared in his drawing. A man in evening dress appears before the closed curtain of a theatre in a drawing in back and white that looks very much like a woodcut. A sculpture of a male figure is made of plywood coloured with wood stain; the profile of the figure is cut twice from sheets of ply and slotted together at right angles to each other. Turned wooden knobs are added to represent testicles. 
A large rag doll three metres high is made of canvas, stuffed, and embroidered with facial and bodily features and decorative patterns, and presented sagged against a plywood cabinet and lying on the floor. A gallery door is replaced by an etched glass and metal door that has an art nouveau style. A very long plywood screen covers the length of a gallery wall, on it a repeating interlinked pattern of a stylised male figure that has a 30s look is made by incising the wood and using wood stain to create a kind of faux marquetry.  A giant leather medal hangs on the wall. 
David was briefly a member of Donatella a group of artists and musicians who produced music videos and cds, and made work in collaboration with fashion designers Eley Kishimoto for catwalk shows. 
!David Jacques Louis.
French sixteenth century painter who made one of the first topical political paintings ever, the Death of Marat in ???. This showed Marat, dead in his bath by his own hand, the knife with which he had stab influences bed himself on the floor near to his hand. His arm trails over the side of the bath which is of stone, as if carved from a single block on a pedestal base, he wears a white cloth wound like a turban around his head which lolls on the side of the bath. In his other hand he clutches a book or a letter. His skin is looks cold and blue. The date is inscribed the corner of the painting. David was one of the first artist to paint topical subjects. Relation to Delacroix?
!Davies Peter.
British artist born around 1970 who began his career making paintings consisting of many coloured dots on a white ground on huge 3 or 4 meter wide canvases, creating patterns of waves, like an abstract pointillism. Davies always uses acrylic paint applied as flat solid colour. He went on to make text paintings in the later 1990s. For example The Hot 100 is a numbered list drawn up as a table in two columns with the lines painted in white, of the artists personal hundred favourite artists, painted in white on a multicoloured ground, a different colour for each line of text. The list functions as an illustration of subjectivity presented as a formally considered abstract painting. Davies subsequently made a diagram painting describing 20th century art in terms of interlinked artists and movements. The text is painted in white on coloured elliptical or circular shapes to fit each name or title, which are linked or cross linked by coloured lines, the whole on a white background.
Davies then began to make a series of very large pattern paintings at least 4 meters wide. The paintings are abstract three dimensional pattern that cover the picture plane and create a surface pattern, some painted in bright colours, some in monochrome, a bit like geometric doodles that play with simple perspective and convex and concave illusion. Like the text paintings these are painted in flat acrylic paint, with hard edges to the colours painted by hand rather than masked.
