Some of them allude vaguely to Robert Delaunay's paintings and to thirties textile patterns, others to futuristic cityscapes. 
!Davis Peter.
British artist
!Dawson Verne|.
American artist whose paintings show an apparent utopia, a kind of modern pastoral world, with tiny people on the scale of the people in a Bosch painting populating woodland scenes. 
!De Chirico Giorgio.
Italian artist who painted views of towns and architecture, which are very distinctive for their depopulation, strange atmosphere and strong light. In a painting of an empty town square, there is a statue, a tower and a row of arches, a kind of arcade. The shadows cast by buildings and statue and under the arcade are black. The ground is ochre. Another painting has a the fallen head of a classical marble statue, very like Michelangelo's David, seen lying on its side on the ground, with a broken off hand in the background. 
!De Cosimo Piero.
!De Hooch Pieter. 
Seventeenth century Dutch artist who painted domestic interiors. De Hooch painted the life of the Amsterdam merchant class in intimate detail. He was painting at a time when this newly wealthy class wanted to display its wealth to the world, but women were still largely confined to the home. So windows were always open, light streaming in to spaces otherwise candlelit, food was plentiful, fruit a luxury. Paintings portrayed a range of subjects. One shows a toddling child reaching up to the mound of apples on a woman's lap. The woman is sitting on a stool and there is a door leading into a room with a window in it behind her. The floor is stone flagged. The child is rosy cheeked and blonde. Another shows a back yard with red bricks laid and plants growing along a wall. De Hooch favours women at home. He also painted landscapes. 
!De Keersmacker Anna Teresa.
Dutch artist
!De Kooning Walter.
a Dutch painter working in the US, he painting bright and gestural large abstract expressionist paintings with figurative elements. Like Rothko he did not subscribe to the notion of the artist as knowing subject but to the Kantian idea of pure art. Enjoyed great success but struck down by Alzheimer's. Continued to paint and a museum exhibition in the late 90s showed his post Alzheimer's work to greatly controversial reaction. Many claimed that he was still a genius, many that the work could now only be compared to 'outsider art' and the art of the insane. Vilified by us artist Paul McCarthy in a film in which McCarthy appears dressed as a paint tube, squirting paint every where and chanting de kooning De Kooning in an infantile way.
!de Kooning Willem.
American artist who worked from the 1950s to the 1990s making paintings in a great variety of styles from figurative to abstract. He is best known for two periods of his work, the first in the 50s is a series of small portraits often of women grinning with lots of teeth, painted in an almost impossibly thick and encrusted impasto, the surface of the canvas heavy with blobs of paint that project from its surface, in browns, blacks, reds and yellows. The second period is de Koonings controversial late paintings, made after the artist was diagnosed as suffering from Alzheimer's disease. These are very large paintings, thin bright primary colours of paint are applied in single brush strokes but the white primed surface of the canvas is barely covered, giving the impression of a white painting roughly covered in gestural marks. These paintings were highly controversial. Some considered them to be an extraordinary later flowering, other to be inconsequential in de Kooning's oeuvre due to his mental state and to be more akin to so called outsider art. Married to Elaine de Kooning.
!De la Cruz Angela.
British artist who make sculptures from collapsed and destroyed paintings. De la Cruz method is to construct a canvas which may be any size (from tiny to the largest which was a large scale installation of a canvas about fifteen metres squared). She paints the canvas in a plain colour, and then destroys it by breaking the stretcher and then twisting the whole into a heap. This is then fixed and either displayed on the wall or floor. These deceptively humble objects assume the presence of a sculptural object, thus a painting is transformed.
!de Monchaux Cathy.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture and installation. De Monchaux made a number of wall mounted works in the early 1990s from machined metal templates, bolted together with velvet, fur and leather often red or purple or black which was clamped between the metal but spilled out in carefully contrived folds. These constructions had a sexual fetishistic quality, they hinted at a purpose but remained ambiguous. They have a slightly gothic appearance but also a heavy metal aesthetic. Put simply they contrasted the supposedly masculine with the feminine, the functional with the luxurious to create decidedly feminine and visceral forms, which often alluded to the female body and genitalia in particular. De Monchaux continued to develop this way of working by expanding the scale of her sculptures, making repeating units and larger more complex forms, using a wider palette of coloured fabric and other materials, and sometimes making floor based installations. 
!De Rivera José.
!De St Phalle Niki.
French artist who makes large and small (ranging from table sized to outdoor public sculptures) papier mache and firbreglass sculptures of human and aniumal forms. These are somewhat like totem poles or other tribal art objects, beign mainly highly stylised white smoothed forms which are richly decorated with hand painting particluar on the head and limbs. 
!De Stael Nicholas.
Dutch painter
!De Stijl.
!Deacon Richard.
British artist Deacon began very large sculptures in the 1980s at a time when sculpture was at a zenith of popularity. Deacon and his contemporaries such as bill Woodrow, Grenville Davey etc. made abstract works largely from cheap easily available materials. Deacon used reclaimed wood which he cut into strips and ply and glued and bolted together to make large hollow snail shell like structures, which he later began to design on a computer. The nature of the materials belied the complexity of the shape and the effort required in their construction. The means of construction were very evident, for example glue spilled over from the joins where they had been compressed. What would usually be hidden or on the inside of such a large work, was actually the surface of the work. 
!Dean Tacita.
British artist born in 1960s who uses 35 mm film to make installations and simple projections. Lighthouse. Donald Crowhurst. Stormy ship blackboard drawings
!Debord Guy.
founder and leader of the Situatoinists, a Paris based group of international artists who were interested in Debords revolutionary ideas (and politics). Debord took Baudelaires idea of the Flaneur, the wanderer through a city and developed it into his personal concept fo the 'derive', a directed walk through urban scenery. Debord wanted to make the life of a city his subject and he believed that people should make themselves part fo the city. He was responsible through the poster printing intitiative he was part of, for many of the slogans familiar from the protests of striking workers in paris in May 1968, for example 'Under the Pavemnet is the Beach' and 'Beauty is in the streets'. Debord had various ideas including putting light switches on street lamps so that the people could turn them on and off at will. 
!Degas Edgar.
French painter working in the middle of the nineteenth century, Degas is known best for his scenes of ballet dancers rehearsing, which are instantly recognisable and much reproduced in particular as calendars. He mainly painted women, including several paintings and drawings of women bathing. Degas was very good at drawing as the many surviving works on paper in pencil and pastels testify. One of these shows the back of woman with red hair as she crouches in a tin bath. There is a blue towel next to her on the floor. Degas made many bronze sculptures of ballet dancers, most quite small around 50 cm high, as well as some of curled up dogs. His best known and very popular sculpture The Little Dancer is larger, around a metre high. The little girl is realistically modelled and wears a net tutu a bodice and ballet shoes. It is rare to find a bronze sculpture which has added clothes.
!Delacroix Eugene.
French painter of mid eighteenth century. Painting in France had up to this point depicted classical themes. Delacroix began to depict these in a realistic way, showing everyday details. His classical realism led him to develop a new way of painting and the way was opened for many to follow.  Before Delacroix no painter had tried to represent events of the present day. 
!Delaunay Robert.
French painter of the Cubist era who made semi-abstract paintings using lots of colours. He took the cubist planes and surfaces and used them to depict vague landscapes, introducing brighter and more luminous colours. Married to Sonia Delaunay.
!Delaunay Sonia.
French painter turn of the century and early 20th century, who made abstract pattern paintings, designed textiles and wallpaper and made clothes. She was unusual in that she regarded all her work as having the same importance. Delaunay's was known for her choice of glowing but subtle colours
!Della Francesca Piero .
Italian Siennese renaissance painter 1450s painted a number of notable frescos in churches in central Italy. In particular a virgin now significantly restored in a tiny village near Siena. A painting of the baptism of Christ hangs in the national gallery in London showing Christ being baptised by john the Baptist who is standing in a stream wearing a cloth round his loins. The Italian countryside stretches away in the background and can be explored for its convincing detail and perspective, standing in for the middle east.
!Deller Jeremy.
British artist born in the late 1960s who is interested in different social groups and how they are creative. The authorship of Deller's work is always in question (an essential part of Deller's work) - the creative work is as much the participants as it is Deller's, who acts as the facilitator of an idea. 
In the mid 1990s Deller began working with groups of people brought together by a particular shared passion. One of the first of these was his Manic Street Preachers project. Deller asked young fans of the band to write and draw their feelings about them and their music. The resulting document was collected into a book and is a testament to the power of music to represent the feelings and hopes of a generation. Deller subsequently drew a chart linking acid house music with brass band music via many other forms of popular music. The chart was hand drawn in white on black employing circles containing text linked and cross linked by arrows.
Acid Brass was a project in which Deller commissioned a brass band to play acid house music. The resulting hybrid was performed in concerts halls and galleries, and released as a CD. Deller's most ambitious work, Battle of Orgreave, was a full scale re-enactment of a battle fought between the police and striking miners at the peak of the miners strike in the early 1980s in Britain. The police charged the miners on horses. Deller arranged for several historical renactment societies to come together to stage a full scale renactment of this event. The battle and the filming of the battle was filmed by Mike Figgis a film director and intercut with original news footage. The resulting film was a very accurate portrayal of what happened that also revealed the lengths to which the governments went to ensure a media cover up of the brutality of the police and their determination that the scale of the protest should be played down. The Battle of Orgreave was shown on national television. Guest list was a work made for Imprint 93 an artist pamphlet series published my Matthew Higgs, which reproduced the guest list for an exhibition opening at the Institute of Contemporary Arts in London. The list which featured a number of celebrities revealed a degree of social ambition and perhaps sycophancy on the part of the institution.
The Folk museum was made by Deller with Alan Kane. Together they collected hand made craft and folk objects of no intrinsic value and displayed them as if they were valuable collection of artefacts. 
At the Venice Biennale in Deller made button badges with a stop/end sign (red circle with diagonal line bisecting it on a white ground) and  the world Vogue on it. No Vogue referred to the magazine - Deller commented of the ubiquity of the magazines presence at any art event, and the ever increasing tendency of the art world to collide with the world of socialites and celebrities and be photographed doing so. Cardiff.
!Demand Thomas.
British artist who make photographs of complex paper 'sets' that he constructs himself. The sets replicate a real space, for example an office, which Demand then remakes entirely in card and paper. The resulting construction is carefully lit and then photographed in large format and presented as a very large (about 2 metres high) print. The image provokes a double take - it seems to be a plausible space although oddly empty. Once the viewer begins to try to decode the image, and look for clues, it becomes apparent that the surfaces of the furniture for example are too homogenous, there is no detail. Even then it is difficult to establish why the space depicted seems unreal. When the process behind the work become clear then the work can be viewed more an achievement in terms of its construction.
!Denes Agnes.
US artist who planted and cultivated a field of wheat in Manhattan on a 2 acre landfill site in 1982. Denes wanted to make a point about world trade, the economics of food production and famine. 
!Dieroff Xenia.
German artist living and working in Britain, Dieroff makes photographic work. A slide installation projected onto a hospital screen uses silhouetted figures and clever dissolves to create the illusion of a patient undressing that morphs into a striptease. 
!Dine Jim.
A contemporary of Rauschenberg and Johns in New York in the 60s and 70s, Dine best known work features his trademark hearts. Drawn in outline or painted often in red or pink on white.
Dion Mark
American artist who in interested in anthropology, material culture, ecology and natural history. His usually large scale multi part installations are the result of research projects into specific locations or subjects. For example in London he searched an area of the muddy Thames riverbank above and below the timeline with the painstaking care of an archaeological dig. The resulting 'artefacts' or trash was cleaned up and exhibited in a number of vitrines and display cases in the Tate Gallery. 
!Dix Otto.
Painted first world war atrocities in a very exaggerated way, including limbless and horrifically injured soldiers one with an eyeball hanging out, the best known being a drinking scene with two veterans sitting at a café table.
!Djistra Reineke.
a Dutch artist who became known in the late 1990s for her photographic portraits of young people. Like Thomas Struth and Thomas Ruff Djistra asked her subjects to look straight at the lens and not to smile. The resulting images are a true document of a generation. In 2000 she began to make video portraits. A film which portrays young people at a club shows them standing against a white backdrop, moving slightly. They are listening to music which the viewer cannot hear, but Djistra has asked them to stand still. Their body language reveals much about them as do their clothes. Although it is a film the participants have been captured in time as if it were a photographic portrait. Their youth is very expressive.
!Docherty Willie.
Northern Irish artist, Docherty made a number of photographic series and video works in the 80s and early 90s at the height of the troubles in Northern Ireland. These featured empty roads, empty landscapes, cars and checkpoints. A video work in his Turner prize nomination exhibition was filmed from a car driver point of view and purported to be a night drive with sinister intentions, although this merely suggested with the greatest economy of means. Docherty's work has an oneric quality although it deals with political issues. He give a glimpse of life on the border and of political strife in urban and rural landscapes.
!Doig Peter.
British painter who grew up in Canada born 1957. Doig is chiefly a landscape painter and was among those British artists who created a resurgence of interest in painting in Britain in the early 1990s. His landscapes are often oneric, usually populated by a single figure if at all and have a strong sense of the unknown and of adventure in them. Doig paints the seasons, the mountains, forests, lakes, vernacular architecture, and occasionally sports such as fishing, skiing, snowboarding. He paints thinly and loosely, scattering the surface with blobs of thicker paint when the image is complete which often serves to represent light for example the white blobs in an early work The Architects House. He painted a Corbusier housing block in a similar way, both are seen through trees and use a lot of green brown, back and white with touches of yellow white and red. Often the touches of paint on the surface do not read as part of the image but become very apparent on close inspection. They may even be bars of colour that are not part of the image at all, or splatters.
 Another early work the Bean Eater shows a boy walking in a field empty except for a red wooden house or barn. A leafless branch of a tree with some kind of seed or flower painted like glowing orangey white bobbles. Later works from 2000 onwards depict a steamboat apparently steaming past a jungle on a river reminiscent of (apocalypse). This is a very green painting, lush. a tree in the moonlight with a girl sitting on a branch high up, painted in greys and lilacs, as if it were a night scene or a nightmare. a lake with a canoe on it paddled by a man with long black hair and a beard, the canoe is orange and the lake very blue. This image is taken from an album cover. A painting of a mountain ski slope called Red ski jacket is very white with only the minute people bringing colour. One of a snow boarder shows the boy in the air upside down at the top of the canvas with a group of people watching at the bottom.
Doig became extremely influential in the mid 1990s. 
!Donachie Kay.
British artist who born in 1970s who made process painting during the 1990s, using a roller to transfer an image to a prepared canvas, resulting in an often haunting shadowy painting, the image always seeming slightly removed from reality due to the technique. Donachie often painted isolated houses, a tent, a Christmas tree, the interiors or Paul Klee's studio, somehow everything looks unpoplulated. She uses a monochrome palette, choosing a predominant colour for a painting. The transfer technique brings the concept of the photographic print into the painting although no such process is involved. The viewers uncertainty about how the image is made is paralelld by the uncertain status of the subject of the painting. Following this Donachie began to paint figures, for example groups of young women, and although not necessarily using the same technique, she gives the image the same once removed quality that places it in an uncertain time. Lighter colours but still monochrome begin to appear. The paintings are small, mostly under a metre squared.
!Donagh Rita.
British artist who makes geometric drawings.
!Donatello.
Italian renaissance or baroque sculptor, several sculptures of David and other classical characters in Florence.
!Donegan Cheryl.
US video artist of Irish extraction, Donegan's work investigates the sculptural and painterly possibilities of everyday activities. In Sunflower, she spins on grass with a bottle of yellow paint attached to her waist, it sprays out and slowly paints a circle. In Kiss My Royal Irish Ass she paints her buttocks green and sits on sheets of paper, three time on each to make a clover leaf painting. In another work she suggestively and lasciviously drinks milk that is spraying slowly from a hole made in the side of a plastic bottle and when her mouth is full she sprays the milk on the wall. In another she holds a French stick between her knees and eats it as far down as she can reach. In many ways Donegan's concerns bear comparison to the work of Sarah Lucas, with her interest in how the banal and the domestic can be both sexually suggestive and formally sculptural and humorous. Donegan also makes reference to abstract expressionism and to the ejaculatory possibilities that can be seen in the work of Jackson Pollock, and mocks the machismo inherent in his work with her own abject attempts. 
!Drummond Fiona.
British artist born 1962 who takes portrait photographs and still life often using flowers and referencing seventeenth century Dutch painting especially in terms of light, using studio set and lighting, also paints dogs and flowers. 
!Dubrofsky and Vinograd.
Russian painters born in the 1970s who paint collaboratively. Dubrofsky and Vinograd are among the few Russian artists to gain commercial success in the US and Europe. They are accomplished painters and draughtsmen, and their canvases allude to both history paintings and to Russian Socialist Realist art. They often ask other artists to paint part of the work for them and they may make several versions of the same painting or may make small version of sections of larger works, for example a book on a snowy muddy ground with a frog diving into a hole.
!Dubuffet Jan.
faux naïf, used sand to make paint rough textured, large simple figures and faces, graffiti African like.
!Duchamp Marcel.
Known as the father of conceptualism, the French artist Marcel Duchamp was among the artists escaping Europe either from censorship or persecution as Jews. He founded a movement called Dada, (a nonsense word with vague associations with children's language and the French fro hobby horse).  He became notorious for his controversial works usually sculptural particularly the ones that became known as ready-mades, most obviously Fountain, (title not this) exhibited at the Armoury show in New York in 1913. this was a gents ceramic urinal placed on its flat (wall mounting) side to resemble a Buddha like object, or fountain, signed R. Mutt (one of the invented punning names that Duchamp used to sign his work). This created a furore, and must be considered in the context of its contemporary art, largely post-impression and cubism. Duchamp's ready-mades were the first example of an artist taking a found object and either altering it or simply representing and titling it to make it into an art object. This simply but revolutionary act changed the parameters of what art could be for ever.
Other similar found object works are a bicycle saddle and handlebars put together to resemble a bulls head, a cycle wheel attached to the seat of a stool, a bottle dryer, 
Duchamp employed pseudonyms and puns as part of his work. His alter ego Rrose Selavy a pun on C'est la vie authenticated and signed many of his works including the urinal. He photographed himself dressed as Rrose (an unconvincing manifestation). She produced a perfume named La Belle Haleine a pun on La belle Helene, Helen of troy and an allusion in its literal translation good breath, to bad breath. 
Infra mince was a series of minimal works. Infra mince or infrathin was a concept involving the declaration of an invisible or intangible but banal act or action to be a work of art. For example the static friction of velvet trouser legs rubbing together, the warmth left on a recently vacated seat, 
Almost as notorious as the urinal was the reproduction of the Mona Lisa that Duchamp defaced. In a pun on the letters LHGC that appear below the face of the Mona Lisa he declared that 'elle a un grand cou' which translates as 'she has a fat arse' of thereabouts. He drew a moustache on her face. This re-appropriated image became one of the best known images in contemporary art. Duchamp took an icon of 16th century western art, defaced it and made his defaced version almost as famous. 
cast sexual parts. Duchamp made cast of the space around the female genitals, a negative of the genitals which produces a potential image of the real positive, and begin concave rather than convex, a kind of visual pun. These cast in bronze. 
origin du monde. One of Duchamp's last works was a large peepshow containing a wax female torso, laid on an apparent mossy bank, the head out of view, with the genitals exposed. This was a comment on a painting/drawing (by whom?) of same title. The apparently innocent tableau with its celebratory title became through the secret viewing through a peephole tainted with an atmosphere of sin and possible wrongdoing, even abuse or murder. 
The Large Glass, also known as The bride Stripped Bare by her Bachelors Even is a large sculpture comprising a screen print onto two pieces of glass held in a metal freestanding frame which was made in an edition. This work has more than any other 20th century work of art resisted interpretation. The images printed on the glass are some geometric rays, and lines, and a coffee grinder with 3 buckets, which was a motif Duchamp often used in other work and had made several drawings of. The buckets on the grinder are sometimes supposed to represent the bachelors, but in general the meaning of the work remains opaque.
Duchamp also made paintings several of the coffee grinder that appears in the Large Glass.
He was photographed by Man Ray with a star shaved out of the back of his close cut hair. Duchamp played chess every day of his life
!Dufy Raoul.
French painter date? of idyllic sea side scenes, using bright colours and a speedy under worked technique resulting in cheerful paintings which are extremely popular. Dufy commissioned to paint a mural for the head quarters of the national French electricity generating company celebrating electricity. This is a large panorama showing people going about all sorts of activities in the countryside, the city, at the sea side with electricity pylons lacing the scene together. The panorama is curved. It is now in the Musée Modern d'Arte de la Ville in Paris
!Dunham Carroll.
Abstract paintings made from the 1970s to the 2000s, brightly coloured but using a lot of white and the forms outlined in black, featuring blob and disc shapes, painted thickly, like lumps or pom poms.
!Durham Jimmy.
A Native Australian artist who makes junk sculptures, which appear to fetish objects. The sculptures often have text applied. Durham's work is concerned with the displacement of the Aboriginal people and their culture. 
!Gainsborough, Sir Thomas.

!Egglestone William.
American photographer who made black and white photographs of working people and city scenes in the 1930s and 1940s
!Eichelman Volker.
German artist born in the late 1970s who made photo collages using interior design magazines and strips of text.
!Eisenman Nicole.
American artist who was one of the so-called 'bad girls', women artists working in the 1990s in New York who used pornographic or otherwise potentially offensive imagery in their work. Eisenman made wall sized paintings depicted pseudo classical scenes. 
!Eliasson Olafur.
Norwegian artist who makes minimal and sometimes barely visible interventions, as in his installation of frosted transparent tape applied in vertical stripes the whole height of the glass curtain wall in the foyer of the Museum of Modern Art in New York subtly but substantially altering the visitor's experience of this public area. 
!Emin Tracy.
British artist and in the forefront of the Brit Art group of artists who came to prominence in the 1990s in London. Emin was born in the 1960s in Margate, a British seaside town which features prominently in her work. Emin's work is essentially a diary of her life, reflecting on her state of mind particularly in relation to her love life and her emotional life. Emin relates experiences that for most remain personal, such as her abortion, her rape, her breakdown, drunken and regretted sex, good sex, the break-up of relationships, her problems with alcohol and her feelings about her absent father. These are represented in a wide variety of media: video, drawings, mono-prints, text, neon signs, textiles, embroidery, and installation. Emin's consistent poetry and diary writing underpins her work.
Her video works include 'Why I never became a dancer' made in the early 1990s showing Emin dancing in Margate. In 2000 Emin made a video in which she appears (achieved with editing) twice as good Tracy and bad Tracey, sitting together and chatting on a couch. The toe dress differently and behave differently, both are likeable in different ways, one is drinking a cup of tea and the other a glass of wine. It seems to be an interview of some sort, or perhaps a therapy session, and the glass of wine emerges as the main difference between the two Tracy's for this film is a tale of the Jekyll and Hyde effect of alcohol.
Emin achieved notoriety when she embroidered and appliquéd the inside of an ordinary one person tent with the names of all the people she had ever slept with. This was quite a long list of mostly men including several male artists, but also siblings and women friends who she had shared a bed with. In order to view the work the viewer had to crawl into the very small tent (which had only room for two),, recline since there was no room to stand and become very intimate indeed with both Emin's work an possibly the other occupant of the tent. 
Emin also embroidered an upholstered chair and made a series of appliquéd and embroidered quilts. The quilts bore text legends and patterns, using patterned and plain fabric and basic embroidery stitches onto bedspreads and blankets. The legends were of life and love and art, (they also featured in the neon signs); 'Indeed art like I need god' being one of the most memorable. The quilts were usually dated. The use of such homely, amateurish and not spectacularly well executed craft techniques allied with such explicit personal statements brought Emin much attention.
Emin had a number of works made in neon, either from her writings or her poetry. The neon letters either a single sentence or a verse, often in pink, were mounted on the gallery wall. 
Emin consistently made drawings and monoprints, often accompanied by descriptive sentences or titles as part of the drawing. These are often explicit, showing her menstruating, lying in a drunken heap, happy and in love, masturbating. They are very simple drawn in a childish hand in pen, pencil or a blue black ink for the monoprints, that belies their effectiveness.
In the mid 1990s Emin opened a shop with another artist Sarah Lucas, which sold trinkets, souvenirs and t shirts all of which were made by the artists. For example an ashtray with a picture of Damien Hirst's head on it (which has since become a collectors item). When the shop closed Emin opened the Tracey Emin Museum, essentially a live work space which she opened to the public regularly each week and sold work from. Her insistence on non-exclusivity as manifested by the shop and the museum, coupled with her appropriation of 'anyone can do it' craft methods and materials captured the zeitgeist of the time, and contributed to a surge in public interest in contemporary art, which had always been seen as elitist, even if this interest was often prurient, Emin was ultimately persuasive. She also took up many of the areas of investigation of women artist in the 1970s and updated them. Emin was extremely influential to a whole generation of artists, especially women.
The work that crowned Emin's reputation (for notoriety or otherwise) was Bed, exhibited in the 2000 Turner Prize exhibition in London's Tate gallery. This is the bed in which Emin spent many days during her breakdown, drinking, smoking, eating, having sex. The bed and the surrounding detritus, empty bottles, food plates, glasses, pill packets, dirty clothes including underwear, overflowing ashtrays, a soiled tampon, books and magazines, was exhibited on a plinth to great public outcry.
Since 2000 Emin has continued to make works on paper and also to make more sculpture, including a rickety wooden tower which can be climbed, and a stuffed bird on a plinth. Birds have been a part of her personal iconography throughout her career. 
!Ensor James.
Belgian symbolist artist who lived in Ostend where his former home can still be visited, During the 1890s Ensor made a painting of a crowd of  people including several in clowns masks, one white masked person wearing top hat making a particular impression. The painting has a slightly nightmarish effect.
!Epstein Jacob.
British sculptor working between the 19450s and 60s, Epstein is now dead. His best known work is 'Rock Drill' in Tate Gallery in London, the head and torso of a futuristic automaton-like creature with a head the shape of an animals skull that is quite sinister. The breastplate ribcage is carved with angular geometric lines. The head is turned to one side. Some of Epstein's work reflects the agony of war. Epstein worked mostly in stone although he did make some bronze casts. Jacob and the Angel is a massive male and a female figure both with huge thick limbs embracing each other. It is carved from a tan coloured but translucent stone. Epstein made a similar carving of a prone figure, a little like an Egyptian mummy.
!Escher M C.
German artist who made a number of extremely detailed and precise drawings of fantasy interiors and architecture, always with an impossible perspective or optical trick. Escher was extremely skilled at drawing. Example of his work, which was reproduced on a vast scale, are an interior with a staircase descending around four walls which never goes anywhere, a pattern of black and white doves which becomes something else, and a hand drawing itself which becomes a hand drawing itself. The endgame of Esher's work is characteristic, everything appears to return to itself. He uses graphic optical illusion, including tricks of perspective such making something flip from convex to concave continuously. 
!Espaliu Pepe.
South American artist born in the 1960s who made a piece of work with a large quantity of old clothes, involving him cycling round the trunk of a tree on a platform that encircled the trunk just below the canopy.
!Evans Walker.
American photographer who took photographs of rural America during the thirties Depression.
!Fabre Jan.
Belgian artist who is very interested in insects. Since the 1970s Fabre has been making large scale theatrical performances usually featuring himself and others dressed as insects or in costumes covered with insects. Fabre also makes objects and paintings encrusted with insects, particularly beetles. The dark iridescence of their wings and shells fascinates him. 
!Fahlstrom Ovind.
!Faier Jonathan.
German artist who made a video with Johannes Rust and one other artist in which they put together a large number of clips from news footage and advertisements featuring corporate logos, editing it and giving it an ironic and humorous soundtrack to represent their appropriated material as a fast paced and amusing look at global capitalism and consumerism. This video is an example of a way of working that emerged in the late 1990s as video editing technology became universally accessible so that anyone with a camcorder and a TV could edit video. Johan Grimonprez's D-I-A-L history is another example.

!Fairhurst, Angus.
British artist who makes drawings and collages and digitally animated drawing videos.
!Fairnington, Mark.
Faithfull, Simon
British artist who has made a number of works for the web.
!Falconbridge, Brian.
Born around 1950, Falconbridge has made very small an detailed finely constructed sculptures, often relying on balance and tension in their construction.
!Fanni Tutti Cosey.
British video and performance artist who has been working since the 1960s. Fanni Tutti's most recent work is painting. The work for which she is best known is a series of live events made on her won and with the band COUM transmission (later to become Throbbing Gristle). Fanni Tutti often appeared half naked. One image is always associatied with her showing her  reclining on a bed, wearing underwear but bare breasted and wearing a blindfold. She made a number of photographic works which imitate soft porn photography. 
!Fecteau, Vincent.
Artist working in the 1990s onwards who makes sculptures from wood sometimes painted that have strong references to modernism
!Feinstein, Rachel.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures from particle board and other timber sheet material, polystyrene, paint and a variety of other media. The sheet material is cut into shapes and assembled as the framework of a sculpture. Sometimes it is spray painted. Feinstein's work has a theatrical feel to it, as if she were making a kind of prop or stage set. She made a number of works that seemed to be futuristic flowers. Later works are made only of painted board cut into shapes, often silhouettes of people. The sculptures are usually no more than 2 metres in height.
!Fend, Peter.
Artist born in the 1960s who made a film of himself jumping around with only a jumper and a pair of shoes on.
!Fernandez, Sam.
American artist working in the 1960s and 70s, Fernandez made psychedelic paintings, which were sometimes used for record covers. They consist of a richly dense background of patterns and flowers which flows into the main element of the image. Fernandez's work influenced graphic design and was re-discovered in the 1990s by a new generation of artists. 
!Fetting, Rainer.
German painter born in the 1950s who made large figurative paintings, for example one of a row of men in shower, painted in blues and whites, in an expressionistic style.
!Finland, Tom of.
Pencil drawings of gay men variously dressed in leather, naked, sometimes in a state of sexual arousal. The drawings have something of the style of the drawings that used to be shown in barbershop windows to demonstrate hairstyles, realist, detailed and making use of the full range of pencil drawing techniques. The men in Tom of Finland's Drawings are often shown in pairs, but sometimes alone. They document specific pleasures and codes of dress very simply and straightforwardly, but for all their sexually explicit subject matter they have a strange ambiguity in terms of their purpose. 
!Finley, Karen.
Karen Finley, an American performance artist whose work, like Mapplethorp's attracted the attention of the US Christian Right. Finley received NEA money and became notorious when she was banned from performing a work in which she inserted pieces of yam into her vagina. When she brought this work to London the Institute of Contemporary Arts was forced to bow to local authority pressure and ban it. Finley continued to make controversial performance work centring around themes of child abuse, sexual identity and the female body, often in the form of a monologue by a character who was in turns aggressive and profane.
!Finn Kelsey Rose.
British artist born in the 1950s, Finn Kelcey made a number of performance works in the 1980s and early 1990s, one using shadow puppets. In the late 1990s she made Matador, a work for which she trained as a matador in Spain and took part in a bullfight. The work itself existed as documentation. She later made an installation titled Steam Bed which consisted of a large square brushed steel trough from which copious amounts of steam were emitted. The steam was sucked towards an extractor fan of the same dimensions as the trough, creating an illusory solid form, a cube of steam between the two.
!Fischli and Weiss.
Swiss artists born in the 1950s who work in partnership. Peter Fischli and David Weiss make installation, photographic and video art. They are interested in nature, animals, cause and effect and the human condition. Early works consist of the artists dressed as animals filmed walking in the countryside, a series of realistic replica interiors fro example a store room, the artists' studio in which every item and piece of furniture is modelled from clay and cast in rubber, in a variety of solid colours, producing a simultaneously convincing and perplexing version of reality. 
Fischli and Weiss made a number of photographic works in which they created art works with food, for example, Still Life with Cold Meats shows an arrangement of sliced sausage, frankfurters and gherkins. Photographic work is always part of their practise, most usually presented as a slide projection or light box. Indeed much of Fischli and Weiss's video work is very static and closer to photography. 
A slide projection featuring hundreds of images of flowers, shown grouped into seasons, each slide dissolving into the next so that a complex double image is created, takes about an hour to view. It has a mesmeric effect. An illuminated table, about 15meters long and one metre wide is used to display hundreds of images of land and city scapes, each one about 6 cm wide. At first off putting this work also draws the viewer in as they try to make connections between the images and inadvertently construct their own narrative.
Video work is often similarly open-ended, as in one installation of about 12 monitors placed in a room, all showing different images of landscape, but not simultaneously so that some screens were blue while others are playing. The viewer is directed to move about the room by the monitors' apparently random showings.
Fischli and Weiss's best known static video work is Pussy, a single shot film of a black and white cat drinking milk from a saucer. This was made to be projected on a huge LED screen above Times Square in New York, where it achieved a monumental quality.
Perhaps their best known video is The Way Things Go, a film of the progression of a series of inanimate objects caused to move by a  cause and effect chain of events. Using simple science, physics and common sense, and using wood, metal, a variety of vessels, balls, weights, fire, steam, salt, batteries, and ice, the artists made a series of structures, balances, ramps, slopes, water channels, tubes, pipes and levers that are set in motion be each other over a period of about half and hour. Basic principles of physics using steam for expansion, melting, burning, balancing, rocking, tipping and velocity for example are used to propel water down a slope into a bucket which tips filling a tank with something floating on the surface which when it reaches a ball cock pushes a weight down on a platform which sets a ball rolling and so on.. This is filmed in an empty warehouse space. 
Fischli and Weiss often make questions part of their work, as if they are involved in some kind of research. The question with which they are most often associated is Are animals People? At the Venice biennale in 2003 they presented a work in which they simply projected a series of questions on a wall in enormous letters, one of which was Will Happiness Find Me?
!Flanagan Barry.
British sculptor working from the 1960s onwards, first mainly making semi-abstract stone sculptures until the 1980s when he began to make figurative work and took the hare as his motif. Flanagan achieved great success with a series of sculptures featuring the figure of a hare roughly modelled in clay, its legs exaggerated in length, cast in bronze, often painted in ultramarine, in a running leaping pose, set upon a plinth made of a crisscrossed rectangle of lengths of planed wood. This hare and plinth combination was repeated in many different materials.
!Flavin Dan.
American artist working in the 1970s and 80s, Flavin took fluorescent tubes and the coloured light they produce as his medium. He created sculptures by leaning the tubes against the wall sometimes two or three, sometimes a whole row, sometimes placed casually at different angles, sometimes measured and at the same angle. Some tubes might be placed on the floor. The transformers and cables are very visible and are part of the work, something which is commonplace now but was not when Flavin made these works. Examples of works are a row of cold white tubes leaning at a slight angle against the wall, a formation of cold blue coloured tubes mounted on the wall in a cruciform, which shines out its light giving the impression of something celestial or perhaps extraterrestrial. Flavin became skilled at using the colours of the light as sculptural form.
!Fleury Sylvie.
French artist working from the 1980s onwards. Fleury is interested in consumerism and in the relation between fashion and desire. One work consisted of  shopping bags from different high fashion shops, placed in a casual arrangement on a plinth. The bags themselves took on a significance beyond their actual existence as carrier of luxury goods, they seemed to be as good as the good they might have contained, to even transcend them, so clearly did their names, logos, colours and designs advertise a particular way of life, style and aspiration. Fleury also made works with shoes.
!Floyer Ceal.
British artist born in the 1960s who has made many works using light as her main medium. However Floyer's earliest works are a pair of brass door fingerplates with the inscriptions pushed and pulled on them, and a red dot of the kind galleries use to indicate that a work has been sold, which in reality was a hole in the wall filled with red pigment. Both poetic interventions into the mundane, which is what characterises Floyer's work. 
A lot of Floyer's early works are slide projections. Light switch, made in the early 1990s, is a slide projection of a photograph of an ordinary household light switch, projected onto a wall where a light switch might be. The projector is not concealed at all. The projection looks exactly like a real light switch prompting a double take, and the viewer, surprisingly, does not readily associate the projector with the image. Floyer achieved full suspension of disbelief by presenting something utterly ordinary in a magical way. Light Under The Door consisted of a projector placed in front of a door, projecting a strip of light the exact dimensions of the space under the door, at this space. The effect was of an incredibly strong light was coming from behind the door, enough to make the viewer want to investigate, when in fact the light source was readily visible at the viewers feet.
In a similar vein, a photograph of an illuminated light bulb was projected on to a switched off light bulb, making it a light source in a new way. A light was projected down through a light bulb, projecting the text on the bulb (brand name, wattage etc) onto the ground below, again prompting a kind of double take since when this work is viewed it seems like something that should happen every time a light bulb is switched on.
Floyer began to work with sound in the late 1990s, presenting a work in which the sound of a slide projector is recorded and pressed onto vinyl, which is played on a turntable on a plinth. The viewer looks for the slide projection, which is not there, and eventually associates what they are hearing with what they are 
This slippage, between what is heard and seen, or what is seen and understood is Floyer's main interest. Around the same time, she made a video of a dog just outside the frame, jumping into the frame regularly leaping for an invisible ball. Once again it takes a certain amount of watching to work out what is happening. An apparently abstract static image suddenly moves and turns out to be a white net curtain against a window which occasionally flaps in the breeze, and when it touches the window becomes more transparent. A piece of screwed up white paper, fluttering in an eddy of wind in an empty cul-de-sac, resembles, when the film is looped, a white butterfly forever fluttering in the same place. 
Floyer's interest in light extended to an interest in all things white, and indeed she has not used colour at all unless the object she wishes to use is coloured. A till receipt is placed on the wall and seems to be nothing more than that, until the viewer realises that every object on the list is white in colour (milk, flour, lard etc.) 
In 2003 Floyer made a work for the Tate gallery in London, She installed a bell which rang a regular intervals, a bit like the bell that summons an audience into an auditorium. This created a sense of expectation, and a feeling in the viewer that they ought to obey the abstract summons causing them to look for a direction and finally to look at their towards where they perceive the sound to be emanating from. This act of looking is what the artist wanted to achieve. Another sound work is a recording of a drip in a bucket, played on a cassette player placed in a bucket, causing the viewer to look to the ceiling or if not to think about the sound they are hearing as being generated by a drip coming from the ceiling which they know is not there. Floyer's project has been to reveal the beauty in the nearly intangible.
!Fontana Luciano.
Italian artist working in the1950s and 60s, Fontana's paintings are abstract, plain flat coloured canvases with slits cut in them and sometimes textural detail added, such as painted gravel. He uses a range of colours from dark reds and blues to bright copper green. Sometimes his paintings are made on egg shaped canvases.
!Ford Laura.
British artist born in 1960s who made a number of installation works in the 1980s concerned with fairy tales and the imagination, including one performance featuring a person in a bear suit. In the 1990s Ford made a large installation of actual size stuffed toy children, made in chintz fabrics of different colours, in a range of poses.
!Forg Gunter.
German artist born in the 1950s who makes paintings, photographs and bronze sculpture. Forg typically places a large (2 meter high) black and white photograph in a frame butted up against an abstract painting of a similar size, consisting of very broad straight single brushstrokes and rectangular areas of colour in orangey red, green and other colours. His photographs are very often of women (head and shoulders) who look serious. Forg's bronze sculptures are the same size as the paintings and photographs, in fact they could be described as sculptures of paintings. The bronzes are about 30cm deep 2 metre height rectangular panels which stand, in portrait format, on bases which slope straight down from and are one piece with the panels. The surface of the bronze is covered with impasto marks presumably made in clay with a palette knife, as if to indicate that this might be a cast of a painting. These monolithic objects are placed around the gallery space in relation to the works on the wall.
!Fortnum Rebecca.
Births artist born in the 1960s whose abstract paintings made in the 1980s and 90s have been described as feminist abstraction. Fortnum often painted a cut or slit as a metaphor for the female body, the interior, a metaphor employed by many women artists in the 1980s and early 90s (which can also be seen in the work of Cathy de Monchaux). This arose from readings of Lacan, a French psychoanalyst whose ideas about 'lack' in the female psyche and about language and femininity were taken up by feminist artists and theorists. Fortnum was also interested in a general discussion about the female body as a 'text' (in the sense that the term used in literary theory) and the 'writing out' of performing of that text being essential to the representation of femininity. Fortnum's paintings, symbolise this lack, and the writing out of the female, by the painted slit or cut, (never an actual cut) and by text she writes on the canvas, sometimes unreadable writing like marks, sometimes actual words. 
Fortnum's paintings are very large, up to 3 metres high, and she predominantly used dark reds and browns. The paintings are a fairly flat colour with brush marks, except where she has chosen to paint the slit, which is made with very thick paint squeezed and brushed. Writing is done with a very small brush in a flowing hand, usually a single line of text running horizontally across the painting.
!Fra Angelico.
Medieval Italian painter of religious subjects.
!Fragonard.
French late eighteenth century painter who is known for his paintings of women and children in the countryside or in a garden. Fragonard's many portraits depict an idyllic life, as in Girl on a Swing and his many paintings of society women in beautiful dresses, children. Fragonard's paintings are full of trees, flowers and grass in the background, and the detail of the subjects clothes is given great attention. 
!Francois Michel.
Belgian artist born 1950s who makes large scale photographic installations. 
!Frankland John.
British artist born around 1960 who makes sculpture and installation. Frankland's early works were a series of site specific installations in which he used plastic sheeting, stretched over battens, to line the walls of a space, creating a second skin inner wall that appeared to be very solid and shiny but was in fact only a thin layer of cheap plastic. He did this in black plastic in a cellar, in silver grey plastic on a freestanding form that looked like a giant metal wall, and in gold metal along a wall in a gallery. IN this last work, Lift, he arranged the battening and supports so that there was the illusion of minimal gold metal lift doors set into a gold wall, with a hemispherical convex form pushed out from the back of the plastic to represent the button for calling the lift the only detail.
Following this Frankland made a work in which he painted a section of tree trunk in bright yellow high gloss paint, using so many layers that the detail of the truck is smoothed out so that it appears at first sight to be a model of a tree trunk. The trunk is mounted on the wall at chest height defying its tremendous weight. There is a  contradiction between the possible lightness of the trunk if it is a fibreglass cast, and the reality of the weight of the wood.
Frankland later made a site specific work for a sculpture park which consisted of his selection of a one ton boulder from a quarry placed in a site of his choice.
!Freidrich Casper David.
German nineteenth century romantic painter many of whose works were painted from nature on an island in the Baltic sea off the coast of Germany. and chiefly known for his interpretation of the Romantic sublime, meaning a rendering of nature so beautiful as to seem almost like heaven. This deeply subjective approach was of great interest to many critics and writers struggling with the idea of authorship, subjectivity and the purpose of art after Kant. Ideas also dealt with by Mary Shelley in Frankenstein and by English poets such as Wordsworth and Shelley.
Friedrich's paintings usually less than 200 by 100 cm in size. Nearly always featured a solitary figure in a landscape, for example a moonlit night with a ruined folly on a hilltop peak and a figure on a closer peak the canvas rendered in blues and greys, or a figure in a rain and windswept landscape with barren trees, or a figure in a moonlit garden with a balustrade, a classical statue and a broken urn. He painted in cool reduced palette. Moonlit scenes are rendered in shades of blue. Freidrich's idealism and his pitting of man against the elements made him seem reactionary at times.
!Freud Lucien.
British figurative painter born in the 1930s, Freud began his career precociously in his teens and was still painting in 2003. He paints portraits, usually of models, sometimes of friends, family, and sometimes of well known people, although rarely accepts commissions. His models are almost always nude, or semi-clothed and painted in accurate detail, but with a psychological perspective. They are often lying down. The predominant colours of the paintings are consistent: skin tones, and the greys and browns of his studio, the background to all his works since he never paints anywhere else. A painting may take up to 20 or so sittings, and some sitters are not able to continue. He has often painted a favourite model repeatedly. 
Some of Freud's paintings might be of two people lying together, or there may be a dog, often a greyhound. He favours gaunt loose skinned physiques but has also painted larger people. He has painted many self portraits. 
Freud's subjects include his lovers, his models (sometimes the tow are the same), his daughters, artist Leigh Bowery, the Queen of England, and the fashion model Kate Moss.
!Frink Elizabeth.
British artist born in the 1930s who from the 1960s onwards has made heads, bronze heads, busts and equestrian statues. The surface of the sculptures is usually roughly modelled. Frink is particularly known for her distinctive thin figures riding a horse bare back whose tiny heads have strangely flat features, which can be seen in many public sites in London. 
!Fritsch Katarina.
Artist working in the late 1990s and early 2000s.
!Frize Bernard.
German, artist who makes process paintings, using multiple clear and bright colours of thin paint in several distinct and apparently continuous brushstrokes, wet into wet, to create a simple repeated pattern. The means of execution of the painting are puzzling for it appears that it would be impossible for the brush strokes to be juxtaposed in the way that they are.
!Frost Terry.
British artist who was a later member of the St. Ives group in the 1950s and 60s. Frost made abstract pattern painting, painting irregular squashed circular shapes in a repeated pattern, in black and white with red, dark green, blue and other colours. Sometimes the paintings would consist of a part of a large solid black circle on one side of the canvas, and a part of a red circle on the opposite side. Frosts paintings were very in tune with the fashions and design of the 1950s and 60s, with their clean lines, minimal forms and bright colours with black and white.
!Gabo Naum.
Eastern European artist who often lived and worked in Britain, Gabo made sculptures and maquettes for proposed sculptures from wood, metal, Perspex and acrylic, wire and thread. Gabo's formalist abstract structures have a strong architectural element in their reliance on apparent tension and cantilevering for their structural strength, and clearly influenced by Russian constructivism and Suprematism, but they also relate to the sculptural forms of Barbara Hepworth, in which voids and solid form are often given tension by thread or wires across them in parallel lines. Most of his work is very small, around 40 cm high. 
Gabo's use of very new materials and his architectural forms made him unique in his time, and Gabo developed a body of work which is singular in its devotion to an aesthetic and to material. He also made preparatory drawings and sketches. 
!Gainsborough Sir Thomas.
eighteenth century British artist who is best known for a painting titled Mrs and Mrs (name) showing a couple and their children and dog under a tree with a house in the background. The man wears breeches, a tail coat and a hat, and is leaning on a shotgun, the woman has on a fine low necked dress which is light blue and white with a straw hat. They are dressed in their country clothes, but there is no pretence - this is a painting of the couples wealth and social status, showing them confidently engaging the viewer from the rural beauty of their large estate. Gainsborough in fact had painted the best known portrait of the new English bourgeoisie, the landed gentry. As such this is a very important painting.  
!Gallaccio Anya.
British artist born in the 1960s who has made a number of installations since the 1990s. One of her first involved paining the walls of a gallery with chocolate. The colour was reminiscent of a dirty protest, the smell in contrast overpoweringly sweet. She also made a curtain to hang in a gallery window from dark orange gerbera flower heads strung on threads the height of the window.  
!Gaston Vidya.
Artist working in partnership with another artist who has made work in the 1990s and 2000s that consist of woollen pom poms placed on Perspex boxes. The pom poms are made in the way that children make them, by threading wool around tow circles of card with a hole in the middle and then cutting between the circles. They are made in a range of sizes and colours, usually muted, from about 60 cm in diameter to 2 cm. The plinths are various sized too. 
The artists have made other kinds of work.
!Gauguin Paul.
French painter working in the 1870s and 80s who made figurative paintings. His early works were landscapes and portraits. Gauguin was an opinionated member of the Parisian art world. He was a very close friend of Van Gogh and they lived together in Arles in the south of France. Gauguin supported Van Gogh and encouraged him, helping him through periods of madness including the ear dismembering incident. Towards the end of his life Gauguin moved to Tahiti and painted the works with which he is most readily associated (although they are not necessarily seen as his best), showing Tahitian people, often women, in native dress, with the interiors of traditional homes and beach scenes. The colours he uses are very strong bold and sun drenched, with the brown skin colour of his subjects predominating. 
In Tahiti Gauguin surrendered to his life long addiction to alcohol and women and he died there.
!Genovese Anna.
British sculptor born in the 1970s who make small scale models of large environments or tracts of land, for example deserts. A desert complete with an oases might be modelled on a domestic scale. Genovese also makes fibreglass 'puddles'.
!Gensken Isa.
German artist who makes sculptural objects and collages. The objects might be concrete forms reminiscent of building materials placed together to form rudimentary architectural structures of about 3 metres in height. Or long wooden spear like forms 4 metres tapered at both ends. Other works include collections of metal cooking utensils, such as sieves, whisks, knives, colanders, graters and peelers, strung together with chains and hung in clusters on the wall. Presented in this was these banal domestic objects seem somehow sexual and perverse. Gensken also made a series of 30cm square collages of nudes cut up and re-joined to make strange human forms. 
In 2002 Gensken made a series of small sculptures that look like maquettes for skyscrapers, made form found materials such as fruit crates, cellophane, printed matter, cardboard and glass, and roughly formed with copious amounts of glue oozing from the joins. This work is about the re-building of Berlin, the changing from a faded modernist architecture to the new skyscrapers. 
!Gentileschi Artemisia.
an Italian artist who was Perhaps the best known woman painter of the classical period. Her best known painting is a portrait of a woman.
!Gericault Theodore.
French artist who made history paintings, most significantly The Raft of the Medusa. This is a very large (2 metre high) and shows a shipwreck scene with the survivors clinging to a raft in a stormy sea. The painting shows a real event which had happened in the recent past. In this sense this painting was a news picture, conveying very vividly the terror of the survivors.
!Gerz Jochen and partner.
German artist who worked with his wife. The Gerzs realised several public sculptures most notably two holocaust memorials. The Sinking Monument is a lead covered square column which is gradually sinking in into the ground on which it stands, where it will eventually over a number of years fill a hole exactly its size and disappear for ever. The soft lead surface invites graffiti, as it is so easy to scratch into. In a reversal of the customary civic practise of removing any graffiti or making it difficult to carry out, the Gerz's column asks the viewer to add to it. The idea is that much of the graffiti will be concerned with the holocaust and that people will engrave the names of those they lost. The monument will take these names underground and they will remain there for ever, a subterranean plinth to a memorial plaque on the surface. 
The other memorial is sited in front of the Reichstag in Berlin. The Gerzs and a number of student volunteers researched into a number of Jewish cemeteries known to have been destroyed and built over. Lists of the names of those buried in these places were obtained. At night they removed cobblestones from the street in front of the building and carved the name of one of the deceased on the bottom. The stones would then be replaced. Eventually all of those whose graves had been lost were symbolically reburied in a permanent and subversive act.
!Gerz Ori.
artist working in the 1990s and 2000s who took a panoramic photo of a London football stadium and presented it on 25 metre gallery wall.
!Ghiberti Lorenzo de.
Italian renaissance painter
!Giacommetti Alberto.
Italian artist working at the turn of the century. Giacommetti's bronze sculptures are extremely elongated usually standing figures, sometimes single figures, sometimes pairs or groups. They are not more than human height and often much smaller. The legs and arms are no more than rods of metal, the bodies agglomerations of bronze that seen to serve merely to join the limbs, not much more than 30 cm wide, the heads small. The sculptures are modelled roughly. 
Giacommetti's sculptures often imply movement, the figures seem to be about to walk. They are solitary and isolated, reduced to essences of the human form. Giacommetti made a large number of working drawings. 
!Gilbert and George.
British artists born in the 1950s who have lived and worked together in a longstanding partnership. They began to work together in their twenties, when they moved into a house in London's East End and began their life as art existence rigidly regulated by both banal and eccentric habits. They became as well known for their lifestyle, and their deadpan personae as for their work. In the early years of their careers Gilbert and George rarely spoke about their work or lives together unless in a very cryptic manner. George, who has a strong eastern European accent, did all the talking, Gilbert spoke only in the most stilted manner in an upper class accent. They parried all interviewers questions by assuming an impenetrably eccentric manner. It was never clear whether or not they were having the last laugh. 
Gilbert and George lived together in a sparse house, wore tweed suits, hats and overcoats, ate in the same café every day of the year, and were never seen without each other.
Their early work was performance. They held many soirees with invited performers, poetry readings, all conducted with an anachronistic formality, including the letterpress invitation cards. They became well known for their work 'underneath the Arches', made in about 1975, for which they became 'singing sculptures'. They stood, their clothes and bodies entirely covered in silver paint, on a plinth underneath the arches of One of the bridges over the Thames and sang the music hall song of the same name continuously. 
In the 1980s Gilbert and George began to make the collaborative photographic work for which they are now known. They began to make photo collages by making self portraits in which they appeared, full length, usually one on each side of the image. The kind of symmetry found in religious diptychs seemed to be a reference point. 
Initially these were small black and white works, the subject matter images of London, flowers and plants, often repeated in a mirror image. Later they began to photograph young men and boys that they had met around London. The compositions began to have a scatological and sexual theme and were often directly abusive. Swear words began to appear. The artists themselves usually featured, the other men and boys often seemed to be submissive.
By the 1990s, Gilbert and George were making works that set out to shock, using images of human faeces and naked men, including themselves. 
The images are printed in square sections which are placed together in a grid to make a large image often 3 metres square. Each section is has a thin frame usually red, and there is no pretence at being one large image.
Eventually what had been shocking about the works became its trademark. The Gilbert and George photo collage became an instantly recognisable trademark.
!Gillick Liam.
British artist who born in around 1960 who works in a variety of media. Text is integral to Gillick's work both visually and because he has written a number of books which he also designs, for example Erasmus is Late (which has a yellow cover with the title punched through in ticker tape lettering), and published texts which are part of his practise. In both texts and visual work he explores the legacy of and iconography of modernism, in particular its architectural and graphic forms, and urban and social space. There is a strongly liberal political aspect to Gillick's work. He often uses quotation relating to politically significant historical events (such as the English civil war) in his work, an approach which ultimately gives Gillick's work a didactic quality. 
For Gillick the design of printed material is of paramount importance, equal to the construction of his sculptural works.
For example Gillick's 'Conversation Platforms' are panels made from squares of apparently random coloured Perspex sheets set in an aluminium framework to make a grid. The panel is then hung above head height and the viewer will be standing under it. These works have expanded into more complex and architectural structures, featuring sections of wall as well as platforms, and even a huge suspended ceiling, always using different colours of Perspex. 
Gillick has also made a large number of works on paper often consisting of works on paper, many of them proposals for the graphic design projects or architectural designs for the facades of buildings. In addition to his ideas for transforming public spaces he makes proposals for social events, some of which are carried out, as for example when he scheduled a programme of yoga classes to take place in a gallery under and around his Perspex platforms.
Gillick's approach is ideological; part social scientist, part philosophical theorist, part artist, part liberal political, he presents a vision of the world which has clean lines, perfectly considered typographic style, well designed building, where history can be reconsidered and there is a lot to read. 
!Giorgione.
Italian artist who painted The Flagellation of St Jerome, depicting the middle aged saint, with a thin sagging body, long grey hair and a beard, alone in penitent pose in his room, naked save for a red cloth around his loins. Jerome is surrounded by symbolic objects and is in the act of self flagellation with a long leather whip.
!Glass Kerstin.
British artist born in the 1970s who paints images of female models from magazines, juxtaposed in the manner of a collage, with a text title running across the painting. the women are painted realistically, and are fashionable dressed and made up often in punk and Goth fashions. The paintings have echoes of 80s design. 
!Gluzberg, Margarita.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes very realistic detailed pencil drawings of women, about 2 metres high. 
!Gober Robert. 
American artist born in the 1960s, Gober's work consider the human body and landscape. He has made a series of works using wax casts of his body parts, for example legs and hands. These are realistically finished down to the last hair, and installed in odd ways, for example a leg and foot may project from wall at right angles to it. His has made many Installations featuring body parts, for example a room papered with the artist's own design of wall paper, a forest of trees with sinister things going on among them and leg sculptures sticking out of wall. Another theme is that of 'orifices'. Gober will often make circular openings with metal facings and plugholes, highly finished but ambiguous in terms of position and function. It is as if the external world is seen as a metaphor fro the human body, and the orifices in the world might lead to a mysterious interior which provokes both curiosity and repulsion. 
Everything Gober makes is made very well with attention to detail and exactly specified materials. In the early 2000s he began to make huge scale installations, for example a large gallery space which is empty apart from a small grill in the floor. Looking through this grille it is possible to discern an apparently real river bed and pool stretching under the floor, creating the impression that the whole of the gallery floor is covering a piece of nature. Somewhere there is a figure of Christ.
Gober also made a piece of work featuring a bridal dress.
!Godfrey Isaacs Laura.
British artist born around 1960 who made wall reliefs that lead eventually to polyurethane foam sculptures reminiscent of the work of Lydia Benglis. The foam in Godfrey Isaacs sculptures is brightly coloured and the forms often jokily allude to the female body. Godfrey Isaacs ceased working as an artist in the late 1990s and opened her own home as a gallery and venue.
!Golden, Pamela.
British artist who makes paintings.
!Goldin Nan.
American artist born in the 1950s, during the 1980s and early 1990s Goldin photographed her own life obsessively. Every detail of the lives of her friends in New York was recorded as she spent time with them. People are photographed sleeping, eating, sober, drunk, fixing up, having sex, getting dressed, in the bath, on the toilet, kissing, cooking, talking, singly and in groups, meeting the camera or looking away. Most of them are artists, musicians, actors and their friends. There are straight and gay couples, single parents, families, children. 
There is a sense of a window on private lives but never a sense of intrusion. A subject might have a black eye, or be menstruating. The subjects are always relaxed in the presence of the camera.
The resulting hundreds of images were exhibited in groups, usually printed fairly small, approximately 50cm high. The groups of images became larger, they were installed in grid formation. Ultimately they were shown as a continuously running slide projection of hundreds of images. 
Goldin's work became a testament to and document of the lives of a generation. This is particularly poignant because many of her friends were gay and this was the generation that was most drastically affected by deaths from the AIDS related disease. Thus this body of work is also memorial. However it is not in any sense melancholic, rather it is experienced as a record of the intimate lives of one generation of mostly young New Yorkers. 
The success of Goldin's work led to her being sought after for commissioned projects, which she resisted, preferring to continue to record her life and the lives of others. 
!Goldstein Jack.
American artist born in the 1950s who has made a number of 16 mm films and videos. Goldstein's work often has a formal simplicity that belies his choice of medium. He may chose to film something that is barely there, that hardly moves. He uses animation on top of the film, for example in a film of a willow pattern plate, which show simply the plate seen face on, in a static shot, one of the birds in the pattern comes to life and flys off the plate. In another a figure of a person is outlined in tiny red lights and moves, the lights shining blurrily.
!Goldsworthy Andrew.
British artist born in the 1950s who makes land art - site specific sculptures placed in the landscape. Goldsworthy began in the 1980s to make small sculptures from leaves and twigs, which he would photograph in the landscape. The photograph is the document of the work. Sometimes he arranged leaves, for example grading the different colours of fallen autumn leaves and arranging them in a pattern. Constructing sculptures and floating them on water. But he also used growing plants, staking the branches of willow trees to from a living structure, weaving twigs into circles. He then began to use snow to make sculptural forms. 
Goldsworthy's work always relates the natural materials and the environment in which they are found to the final form that the artist creates for them. It also makes associations with the history of ritual and nature and the resulting artefacts.
Gradually Goldsworthy's work became larger scale. He travelled widely in search of new landscapes to work with for example to Alaska. 
!Golub Leon.
American artist born in the 1930s or 40s who makes abstract paintings.
!Gontarski Steven.
American artist born in the 1970s who makes figurative sculptures. Gontarski began in the late 90s to make life-size figures made from fabric sewn tighter in the style of a stuffed toy on top of an armature. The figures were distorted human forms made from shiny plastics and vinyl, elongated and featureless, their limbs often joining and intertwined. The combinations of fabric alluded to garments, indeed sometimes a part of a garment might be incorporated into the sculpture. The lumpen feet might have a stripe alluding to but looking nothing like a trainer. Gontarski's soft sculptures are dancers, made from a combination of formal sculptural forms of example Henry Moore with club wear and fashion. Along with the sculptures he made drawings on acetate, and later on Perspex sheet. These were simple outlines in fibre tip marker featuring people that he had meet and odd lines from their conversation.
In 2000 Gontarski began to make similar figures from cast fibreglass, finished in one colour of a high gloss lacquer often pearlescent or metallic. The figures began to take on a molten morphology, like a mixture of Rodin, Boccioni and the molten baddy in the film Terminator 2. the figures twist and dance, and are placed on plinths made of the same material and of a part with the sculpture. The plinths are sprayed with graffiti, done not by the artist but by graffiti artists that the artist has met. 
Gontarski has also made a series of busts in a similar way. These refer to the generic bronze bust which one might see anywhere. There might be a hole right through the face in Gontarski's smoothed out and rendered version. One in a virgin Mary figure painted in white.
!Gonzales Torres Felix. 
American artist born around 1960 who made sculptures and installations. Gonzales Torres died in the late 1990s. During his lifetime his father died as did many of his friends from AIDS related illness. His work is preoccupied with death. 
Gonzales Torres used a variety of media, usually with minimal intervention on his part. The work he did was in choosing his material, decisions about quantity and [placement. For example he presented a simple stack of his own weight in sheets of newsprint. The weight of his dead father in ivory coloured sweets, is laid out in an amorphous shape on the floor, the sweets are the same colour as bone and the visitor is invited to take one away. A way of giving out the body of his father in an obvious parallel with the Transubstantiation.
