This is one of a series of candy works, using different colours of sweets arranged in different abstract shaped heaps on the floor according to the subject of the work (given in the title) always to be taken away so that the work changes through interaction. Another series of works are bead curtains, floor to ceiling, strung across the width of a gallery to bisect or divide it creating a new space, partially transparent, the beads are of certain specified colours. One work included a performative element. A Male dancer naked except for silver shorts and boots dance on a podium surrounded by lights. When the dancer wasn't there the podium remained as the work, a plinth for an absent sculpture, the dancer, symbolic of death, the final curtain and the performance of life.
Gonzales Torres also made a series of light bulb works, strings of standard light bulbs to be strung in specific locations, in a tree, a stairwell for example. and he made Photographic works the most well known of which is an empty bed with white sheets each of the pillows bearing the impressions left by a head. Another is a photo graph of flowers growing in bare soil, which is titled The Grave of Gertrude Stein. Gonzales Torres' work deals with death, the fleeting nature of life, and the idea of the memorial, by using the simplest simple sculptural forms. 
!Gordon Douglas.
British artist born in the 1960s who works in a range of media, including photography, video and text. Gordon is fascinated with the visual representation of psychological states in particular in film.
Gordon made an installation for which he covered the walls with a list of All the people he knows their names applied in self adhesive lettering in columns. Gordon often uses this type of lettering. A small work appeared in a book consisting of a text about looking which ends by stating that by the time the reader has read the text, they will have been reading for 3 minutes. Gordon makes a comparison between the time taken to read and the time taken to look at images.
Gordon has made a number of video and film works. He often uses back projection onto freestanding or hanging screens in the gallery. One work shows a film from the thirties of two psychoanalysts talking to and subsequently restraining a hysterical female patient, finally pinning her down. The film is presented played forwards and backwards, resulting in very different interpretations on the viewers part. This deconstruction of found film led eventually to Gordon's best known work, 24 Hour Psycho. Gordon takes the feature film of the same name and projects it on the gallery wall, slowed down so that it takes 24 hours to play from start to finish. The gallery remains open during this time. The Resulting film provokes all kinds of new readings, and the extreme slow motion renders the famous shower scene entirely different. 
Gordon has made many works on paper including a number of photographs of film stars with the eyes cut out or replaced with plain paper, and a number of photographs of apparent actions such as his black finger tattoo, a photograph of his finger which is entirely black. 
!Gormley Antony.
British artist born in the 1950s who became very well known in Britain when he made a large scale public commission called The Angel of the North. This 30 meter high sculpture is a standing figure with no facial features or detail. Its outstretched arms have become aeroplane wings, the arms span about 40 meters. The sculpture which is made from steel, and is sited by the motorway and railway line from London to Scotland. It can be seen for miles around, and is probably the most visible and best known piece of public art in Britain.
Gormley made lead casts of parts of his body fro a number of years in the 1980s and 90s. He then began to cast his whole body and make sculptures from beaten moulded and welded lead sheet. The resulting figures are life size and have no detail or features, and are seamed where the lead sheets meet as if they are made of stitched together pieces of fabric. Some are prone some standing. Gormley has conducted a most thorough investigation into the male human form, their visual anonymity allowing the figures to stand for every person. 
Gormley became known to a much wider public when he made the participatory work Field in the early 1990s. Field consists of hundreds of 40cm high clay figures, roughly alike, placed shoulder to shoulder in a mass on the floor of the gallery. Field was partly inspired by the terracotta army of 7th century Chinese sculptures found in a burial ground.
Wherever field is exhibited it is created anew by local people, each person making their own figure from local clay. 
!Goya Francisco.
Spanish painter considered one of the greatest painters in history, Goya is particularly well known several works including The Disasters of War, The Execution of Maximilian (1814) and a series of works known as The Black Paintings. His paintings are mostly smallish, around 150 cm high at most.
Goya was a political painter whose intention was to reveal to a wider public the cruelty of war and the harshness of life for ordinary people. He was also an innovator in terms of his use of paint and colour. The Execution of Maximilian (an emperor) shows a blindfold man on the right of the canvas facing a firing squad on the right side. The scene takes place in front of a hill with some buildings behind it. Smoke is coming from the soldiers rifles. The painting depicts the actual moment of execution and was made very soon after the event. Thus it is news reportage, depicting the deposition and execution of a ruling monarch.
The Disasters of War are a series of around 30 etchings (about 40 cm high) showing scenes of carnage from the battlefield. Soldiers lie dismembered and disembowelled on the ground, they hang from trees, decapitated or genitally mutilated. Goya intended to show the gross cruelty of war and because he chose to use etching his work reached a wide audience who might otherwise have been able to try to ignore the black paintings show the life of ordinary Spanish people eking out a subsistence living on the land, living in one room. The paintings are dark and inky, there is no light. They show the harshness and ugliness of life. There is one in the series that shows a dog in quicksand, an ochre sand hill filling most of the canvas with the dogs head sticking out of it.
!Graham Dan.
American artist working in from the 1970s onwards, Graham makes architectural sculptures, usually site specific, from glass and mirrors. He creates simple structures from plate glass that invite the viewer to enter them. The reflections or the surrounding landscape and of the structure itself cause a sense of disorientation, in particular in terms of which parts of the sculpture are transparent and which are reflective. In short a confusion about what is a real image and the nature of that reality. The structures are extremely simple and formal, usually human height, often no more that a series of vertical planes placed together at angles and ramps. They are usually places in the open air as the light of the sky plays an important role in the reflectivity that is part of the work.
!Graham Rodney.
Canadian artist born in the 1950s who since the 1980s has made works in film, video, installation, photography and sound. Graham made a video showing himself as Robinson Crusoe lying beneath a palm tree sleeping until  coconut drops onto his head from the tree. He sits ups, rubs his eyes, tries to focus, feels dizzy and lies down only to have the same thing happen again, and in fact the film is a loop. This work portrays the endless loop of time lived out by Crusoe while stranded. Another film shows a house burning down. 
Graham made a sound work in which he took a segment of a piece of classical music which is not usually played when that pieces is performed, analysed it and added it to make a playable loop. He then arranged for an orchestra to play it and it was recorded.
Graham took a photograph of a tree in winter, and presented it upside down tree. The branches of the tree become the roots, and the roots the branches. On the theme of the inverted or retinal  image Gordon also installed a pinhole camera in a horse drawn carriage, giving the viewer the opportunity to enter the carriage and view the scene outside through the camera upside down. 
!Green Gregory.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes installation and sculpture. In the early 1990s Green made an installation in a small room fro which he constructed an imaginary IRA bomb factory from scratch by reading books and information freely available to the public. The process of bomb making is shown taking place in a small kitchen, the materials needed strewn everywhere, empty cups and ashtrays, food and food wrappers, plates the evidence of long hours of work taking place. The drain gin board contains canisters of weed killer and bags of sugar, the materials used to make explosives, and there are five litre plastic bottles with liquid in that is undergoing some process of transformation. A table is crammed with electrical equipment and tools, mostly ordinary domestic materials such as light switches, plugs, fuses, wire, insulating tape, cable connectors. There is a bomb makers manual open on the table. The verisimilitude is such that the viewer cannot avoid the feeling that they are trespassing in a place where an illegal activity is taking place. The viewer feels jumpy, as if they might be discovered by the perpetrators, who they feel might be dangerous. The effect of the installation is to show who close to daily life, how enmeshed in the everyday is the potential to kill.
Green also made several bombs complete but lacking explosive and detonators. These attracted the attention of the tabloid press and in some cases the gallery that exhibited was forced to remove them. One was a suitcase bomb, one was even a nuclear device, complete except for the plutonium.
Subsequently Green set up a pirate radio station and broadcast illegally to a free state that he had created.
!Green Renee.
American artist born in the 1960s, Green is known for her large scale interactive installations which have a socio-political genesis. In the late 1990s Green made an installation that filled a gallery with a maze like partition board structure consisting of a series of spaces, the walls painted different colours, sometimes plain, sometimes striped. Viewers walked through the structure, choosing to stop where they wished, their participation in what Green had presented invited. Videos are presented on monitors on the floor with bean bags and headphones strewn about. Written participation in a survey in invited. Photographs are on the walls. One space has sitting room furniture. One has a record collection and a record player. Overall the viewer enters a microcosmic world that represents daily life and is asked to consider and participate in activities that illustrate how identity is constructed and how it might be possible to think in new ways about its construction. 
!Greuze.
French painter, died.
!Grimonprez Johan.
Belgian artist who made a video work in the late 1990s D-I-A-L History. This projected film consists of vast amount of excerpted footage from newsreels, film and television that concerns terrorism, specifically the hijacking of an aeroplane. Grimonprez has edited this footage to make a short film that is a history of hijacking and the imagery that we have of such events. 
!Gris Juan.
French painter who was probably the first painter to use collage, an idea which was picked up by his contemporaries Picasso and Braque. Gris made a number of small paintings with collage and went to make Cubist paintings.
!Grosz George.
German artist who painted the decadence of the Weimar republic at the beginning of the twentieth century and the aftermath of war.
!Group Material. 
Toronto based group of artists/activists, producing collaborative works, solo works, publishing, running a gallery, and commissioning projects and books. Including among their number the painter Jochen Klein.
!Grunefeld Thomas.
German artist born in the 1950s known for his stuffed animal sculptures in which different parts of animals are combined to form one hybrid creature that could never exist. Grunefeld uses highly skilled taxidermists to renders these creatures objectively completely convincing as if they were the result of some kind of perverse genetic engineering project. The animals for example a rosters feet on a furry mammals body with the tail of a lizard, could have no imaginable purpose, thus they seem like contemporary fantastic beasts. 
!Grunewald Matthias. 
German medieval artist working around 1450 who is best known for a crucifixion scene. Grunewald is noted for the gritty realism of his paintings, extraordinary for the time, and for his use of colour. Grunewald did not shrink from depicting the horrors of medieval life, in a realistic way. 
!Gunning Lucy.
British artist born in the 1970s who made a video titled Climbing Round my Room (mid 1990s). Gunning negotiated the perimeter of her room without touching the floor, which involved climbing to the top of a wardrobe and balancing precariously on various items of furniture and using door handles and steps. The resulting video presented a re-articulation of an ordinary domestic space. 
!Gursky Andreas.
Swiss photographer who takes pictures of banal scenes in which the minute is contrasted with the gigantic, creating a formal image which becomes almost abstract in some cases. Gursky heightens this effect by manipulating the image in the darkroom, often altering the perspective so that the image reads as a flat picture plane rather than a representation of perspective. There is usually a repetitive pattern created by the presence in the images of a mass of something whether it be people, windows, etc. These effects are subtle and subversive.
Examples of Gursky's work from the 1990s include a container park at the port of Ferrara, where the bright colours of the containers form a patchwork pattern on the background of red soil and glowing pink light. The floor of a Siemens factory is a mainly white image with the black lines for the production line furniture and the bright colours for the electrical cable supplies creating a pattern, The Japanese stock exchange is a pattern in yellow and black added to by the white of the cascading tickets and the striped jackets of the trader. An expanse of plain carpet seems to be vast, skiers on slope are sticks of colour in a white space, a race course in Japan can be read a horizontal bands of colour, the display case in a Prada shoe shop is alive with glowing jewelled colour, a shopping centre is presented as bands of brass and greenery as the escalator travel down through the main atrium, a vast apartment block is a pattern of squares, a canal and dyke are horizontal stripes 
!Gussin Graham.
British artist born 1960s who makes drawings and sometimes installations. Around 1999 Gussin made a sound sensitive floor that made sounds in response to the viewers footsteps, producing a re-articulation of the viewers action in looking. Gussin made some drawings of the sound prouduced in this way using a computer programme that represented the sound waves as a perspective drawing.
!Guston Philip.
American painter born in the 1930s who made abstract expressionist work at the beginning of his career in the 1950s and early 60s. Guston had a crisis of belief in abstraction and began to paint figuratively. His began to be successful from this point, but was only really recognised much later, in the 1980s. Guston developed a distinctive personal iconography that included cigarette butts, ashtrays, horseshoes, shoes, and Klu Klux Klansmen's pointed hats, painted in a cartoon like style with heavy black outlines. The objects in particular can be seen as self portraits. Paintings include a pile of shoes, old and battered boots with the odd horseshoe, an ashtray overflowing with giant very fat cigarette butts, and a series of paintings of figures wearing Klansmen's hats the figures often facing each other as trying to converse. Guston typically used either a monochrome palette of grey brown black and white (for example in a painting of a pile of horseshoes), or a bright palette. His bright colours are blue, red and a fairly bright light pink, the pink often used as a background. Some of the Klansmen paintings are black on white. 
Guston depicts a lonely world not without a bleak humour, filled long hours in the studio smoking and trying to communicate with the rest of the world.
!Gygi Fabrice.
Swiss artist born in the 1960s who make installations. These consist of structures made from metal wood rubber and plastic, which apparently have practical function but in fact are difficult to decipher. The structures or groups of structures might fill a room. They have metal frames made from some kind of metal pole, put together using clamps and off the shelf heavy duty fixings. There might be a screen like structure, a bench like one, a platform. The frames might be filled in with plastic or rubber sheeting, hemmed and edged with large eyelets and then laced to the frame. A bench might have an upholstered top. The elements reference the gym, the club and seem to be an arena that implies sex and possibly violence. Yet they are benign mysterious objects that invite human interaction.

!Haacke Hans.
American artist who began working in the 1960s and became one of the best known of the 70s conceptual artists in America and Europe. His practise was political in the sense that his subject matter was the multinational corporation and advertising, which he believed to be the means by which corporations controlled their customers and the financial markets. 
Haacke made a number of installations consisting of text panels with photographs detailing the activities of multinational companies such as William Morris, often subverting advertising design used by the company.
Haacke's earlier sculptural work includes a piece called Condensation Cube (1965), a Plexiglas cube about 50cm square wit naturally occurring and accumulating droplets of condensation inside it.
!Hale Matthew.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes performance videos and assembled sculptures from household objects that hint at a performative action. Hale was one of the founders of City Racing an artist-run gallery in London in the 1990s.
!Hals Franz.
Dutch Eighteenth century painter notably of portraits, Hals painting The Laughing Cavalier, a portrait of a man in a large hat with a feather is instantly recognisable. 
!Hambling Maggie.
British artist who began painting portraits in the 1960s
!Hamilton Finlay Richard.
Scottish artist who since the 1970s has made and continues to make site specific outdoor installations. Hamilton Finlay carves text into stone panels and blocks, inscribed in simple classical fonts, single words and phrases that relate to landscape in which they are placed. His intention is to integrate words with landscape. These installations are sometimes small scale but may also be installed over a large area of land, such as in a park. The installation will often be permanent. Hamilton Finlay's work is probably best known as photographic documentation.
!Hamilton Richard.
British artist who was part of a group of pop artists in the 1960s. Hamilton made paintings and collages. His paintings are figurative and usually have a realistically painted element, perhaps a figure, with an abstract more painterly element, often blurry and watery, painted on un-primed or thinly primed canvas. Like many of the pop artists, Hamilton used many if the techniques of graphic art for example air brushing and photographic reproduction, in combination with oil painting. For example a painting of the IRA hunger striker Bobby Sands during the period of his dirty protest made in the 1970s show Sands in his prison cell, long haired and bearded, with the wall behind him smeared with his own excrement. Hamilton used an air brush to paint the excrement as blurry brown squiggles. Hamilton's collages are made as sketches for painting but sometimes presented as works in their own right such as the best known one, and arguably Hamilton's best known work, A Plan for Everyday Living which shows a montage of a modern domestic interior, an apartment with cantilevered stairs descending into a double height living area with picture windows, a body builder dressed only in a frilly apron vacuuming on the stairs. 
!Hammons David.
Artist whose work investigates cultural and ethnic identity.
!Hanson Duane.
American artist who began making his very well known life casts of people in the 1970s. Hanson's fibreglass casts of himself and others are sculptures equivalent of the photo realist painting. The casts are painted, have real hair added and are dressed to look as if they are real. They do indeed prompt a double take in the viewer. Hanson chose very ordinary subjects, for example a police officer, a museum attendant, a pair of overweight and badly dressed American tourists, a motorbike accident victim, prostrate with his bike, a cleaning woman. They are uncanny in their ability to fool the viewer.
!Hapaska Siobhan.
Irish artist born in 1965 who makes sculptures and installation. An early work was 8 identical life casts of a clothed young man, painted in buff colour, bearing a coffin. Made in 1990s this work seemed like a comment on the number of young men who were dying from AIDS related illnesses at that time. Also a vaguely heart shaped wall mounted 'speaker' constructed from teak veneer and purple felt, with a heartbeat sound playing from it. St Christopher Legless was included in a room sized installation featuring a fibreglass body cast or lifelike sculpture of the saint in rags and long black beard, sitting against the wall with amputated or cut off legs, an irony for this patron saint of travellers, or a cruel blow dealt to him deliberately. Echoes of kneecapping punishment in the conflict in northern Ireland. There was also a fibreglass sculpture of a motorbike, whose contours and mechanics had been smoothed out and filled in so that the whole bike turned into a kind of futuristic vehicle, that did indeed echo the sculptures of Boccioni but also somehow of Rodin. Sprayed a pearlescent white and highly polished. Also a large bedlike structure, a futuristic sleeping platform made from shiny white lacquered fibreglass, the platform with ergonomic curved base, with a slit running all round the inset mattress area, from which water was pumped and ran over the sides of the base in a constant water curtain. The mattress of shaggy fur, with a harness and headphones suggesting that one could strap oneself in and be transported, not literally but mentally, by the comfort and the sound and the trickling water. The bed like the motorbike, seemed to come from science fiction.
!Haring Keith.
American artist, now dead, who began as a graffiti artist in the early 1980s in New York. Haring developed a distinctive graphic style, using bold lines to outline his figures, animals and symbols, and filling them in with plain flat colour. Once he became known Haring began to make his work on canvas and made a lot of printed works, books and ultimately merchandise. Today Haring's work is very recognisable. He used a simple lexicon of symbols and figures, always outlining them in black and filling the background in with small marks. Hearts, hands, and figures, much simplified, often appear. The figures usually in active poses, rarely have features. One of his best known images is of a red heart on a yellow background, the heart surrounded by lines as if to indicate that it is beating. He most often used the colours primary yellow and red, and black.
Haring made a lot of political posters about AIDS, and his instantly recognisable graphic style was particularly suited to campaigning on various issues. 
!Harris Dereck.
British painter born in the 1960s who makes paintings of details of sportsmen and women, much enlarged, from photographs. 
!Harris Louise.
British artist born in the 1960s who made installations and sculpture in the 1990s from wool and other textile materials.
!Harris Mark.
British artist born around 1960 who makes conceptual work.
!Harrison David.
British artist born in the 1950s who makes autobiographical sculpture, paintings and drawings. Harrison's work has a naïve quality, the paintings are small, around 60 cm high, and sometimes awkwardly painted. The drawings are made in pencil. In his early work in the 1980s Harrison concentrated almost exclusively on painting cats with a highly stylised 50s appearance. Later Harrison began to describe his own life and lives like his more literally in terms of encounters with people, animals and landscape. For example, several paintings feature a caravan in a rural setting, and include the name of a purported guest to the caravan in the title, for example British footballer Paul Gascoigne. In some of the caravan paintings a mysterious white rabbit hovers, ghost like, as if it represents the spirit of something. 
Harrison's sculptures are assemblages of objects to resemble animals, in the spirit of Picasso. Forks, table legs and any kind of discarded objects are given new roles as the legs of a heron, a birds beak. The sculptures junk like appearance belies their accurate resemblance to the animal they represent. 
!Harvey Marcus.
Marcus Harvey is a British painter born in the 1970s who is interested in using paint and its application to produce a hidden image, or conversely to produce an image by an unusual means of application of paint. Harvey made a series of works in the 1990s which on first inspection appear to be abstract paintings with lots of impasto and brushstrokes. In fact superimposed are outlines of women taken from pornographic images painted in thin black lines which merge into the abstraction. Once recognised, however the images are very clear. 
In 1999 Harvey made a painting of Myra Hindley a convicted child murderer still serving her sentence at the time which became notorious. Harvey made the 10meter high portrait, taken from the police mug shot made famous by the constant press interest in the story, from the handprints of children. The handprints in varying shades from white to dark grey form a kind of pixel effect, creating and image that only appears in full detail from distance, much like the effect of a billboard poster
!Hasewaga Jun.
Korean artist working in Britain, born in the 1970s. Hasewaga makes paintings of people which are done on board, in shiny gloss paint with solid outlines, and then cut out.
!Hatoum Mona.
British artist born in the 1950s in a middle eastern country, who makes installation and video work. Hatoum's work investigates the extremes of human nature and cruelty, alluding to the repressive practises of certain regimes in the middle east and their effect on the life of women in particular. An early video work shows the veiled face of a woman with the shadows of Arabic text falling across her face. At a certain point a knife is drawn across her eyes as if to cut them open.
A later installation consists of a long totally dark corridor constructed within a gallery, at the end of which is dimly visible a red glow. The curious viewer will walk along the corridor and the glow becomes brighter, revealing itself to be several vertical bars of red light. Only at close proximity does it become apparent that the light source is electric heater elements. The effect of this is palpable, the viewer has the sense that they might burn themselves, that the red hot bars have somehow lured them towards them.
Another installation that uses a similar economy of means consists of three banks of wire mesh lockers, arranged to create a 'room'. Hanging from the ceiling is an unshaded electric light bulb which slowly descends and rises into and out of the locker space. The shadows cast by the wire gridded construction of the lockers grow large and shrink in the most sinister fashion. In the late 1990s Hatoum moved on from the almost theatrical effects created by these installations to create more video work. In a video installation called Deep Throat displayed in a wooden kiosk, Hatoum showed a film made by a medical camera travelling through the various orifices of a woman's body. Like all her work the visceral impact of this work leads to secondary reflections on imprisonment and torture.
!Head Tim.
British artist born around 1950, since the late 1970s Head has made work in a variety of media. He has often focussed on generic and graphic imagery which he has abstracted and reconstructed in various ways, both as sculptural form, painting and works on paper. In the 2000s Head began to work digital animations, using the pixels that usually constitute the image of video and manipulating their colours to create abstract colour fields of constantly changing coloured squares. These are projected very large, and make immediate visual connection with pointillism and with the dot matrix printing process made familiar by advertising hoardings. Head has created a moving abstract work from pixels, literally the stuff that images are now made from. 
!Heilman Mary.
Abstract painter born in the 1940s, Heilman makes small paintings of carefully considered abstract shapes, lines and forms exploring endless possibilities of form and colour.
!Hemsworth Gerard.
British painter (born 1940s) who has taken generic graphic forms and visual jokes as his subject matter. Hemsworth's paintings are made with entirely flat smooth colour and hard edged lines. Typically he will choose one or several diagrammatic representations, such as a cloud, a rabbit or a vase for example, simplify this to the minimum number of lines required and paint it on the canvas in black. 
!Hepworth Barbara.
British sculptor who began working in the 1940s and made the work for which she is best known in the 50s and 60s. Hepworth spent most of her working life in the artists colony of St. Ives in Cornwall, south west England, along with Ben Nicholson and others. She was also a contemporary of Henry Moore.
Hepworth worked in stone, wood and metal, as well as making working drawings. Her sculptures were smooth often oval forms, which often had holes (or voids) through them. The voids might be spanned by string or wires strung across in parallel lines or fanning out like spokes, as if to create some kind of tension in the object. She favoured black or white marble, granite or wood, highly polishes, or bronze which would be polished to a high gold shine. Many of her sculptures were worked out in plaster.
The sculptures ranged from small table sized ones, for example three perfectly smooth elliptical forms on a shallow plinth, all made of white marble, or an elongated bronze shape standing on its end, to medium sized plinth based works such as the stringed elliptical voids, to large outdoor sculptures that sat on the ground, on stone boulders, or bronze plinths. Hepworth tended to make the plinths for her work from the same material as the sculpture itself.
Hepworth made several large scale public commissions and her work can be seen in London on the façade of John Lewis department store, in various public parks for example on Hampstead Heath and in Battersea Park, and most famously towering over the piazza of the United Nations building in New York. In most cases these works featured an oval shape often with strings, either in stone or bronze. For the UN building she made a large oval flat shape with a small hole through it, reminiscent of a standing stone standing on its end next to an ornamental water feature.
In fact the standing stone and other stone age monuments and monoliths were part of her inspiration, surrounded as she was by them in Cornwall. Hepworth wanted her sculptures to sit in the landscape in the same way. 
!Hesse Eva.
American artist born in the 1940s died in 1969. Hesse's prodigious output seen as an important counterpoint to the prevailing, new and radical minimal sculpture of the time, and as a way point for women artists who were in the minority, particularly in this field. Hesse combined current sculptural preoccupations with unorthodox, and traditionally feminine materials and methods. Her achievement was recognised very early and she was included in several seminal exhibitions including one curated by Lucy Lippard which defined the new work. 
Hesse began her career around 1960 as a painter, training under ??. She made figurative paintings and self portraits, gradually turning to abstraction, until she had a crisis of confidence in her ability to paint. Hesse ceased to make work until she and her husband travelled to Dusseldorf where a German industrialist collector had offered them a studio residency in a disused factory that he owned. There she found an abundance of materials and machine parts lying around, and she began to draw these parts and experiment. Working in the factory Hesse produced a number of odd abstract drawings based on the machine parts, and began to make wall based three dimensional paintings, modelling the surface of the work and adding string following the shapes, as well as projecting elements, the whole painted. She then began to make sculptures with the materials she found on the factory floor, and her first successful ones were cubes of steel mesh sheets, open on one side. Hesse pushed equal lengths of  rubber tubing through the holes in the mesh in the manner of rug tufting, to create a furry effect on the interior of the box. This painstaking task produced 2 sculptures (one with black tubing, one with transparent tubing) which combined the (traditionally female) techniques of handcraft with those (traditionally male) of industrial fabrication used to make minimal sculpture. Hesse continued to make wall reliefs from modelling materials, string and paint including Rosy, a breast like form on a buff coloured round with a spiral of string painted in graduated shades from pink to red. Hesse's interest in concentric circles was worked in these reliefs and in her Dot graph paper drawings, delicate pencil or pen drawings in which rectangles are filled with row of tiny small circles or rows of concentric circles coloured in with pencil. 
The string forms resulted in a wall work consisting of a row of about 25 hemispheres 30cm in diameter covered with string spiralling round, placed in a horizontal line on a long strip about 40cm wide, at picture height on the wall. From the centre of each hemisphere cords project and extend to the floor where the ends lie. The whole is painted grey.
Hang up (1967) is a seminal work by Hesse. It consists of a 2m by 1.5m empty frame on the wall, bound with cloth bandage and painted light grey. Form the frame projects a heavy gauge wire also wrapped with cloth bandage attached on the bottom right and top left corners and looping out about 2 m form the wall and back again. The viewer may stand inside the loop, in which case they are seen to be lassoed by the work. This work is an abstract but concrete representation of the nature of looking at paintings.
String was one of Hesse's favoured materials along with latex and fibreglass. She made a string mess consisting of length of cord dipped in latex and strung from the ceiling to form an abject enlarged untidy web. She took net shopping bag and stuffed them with scrunched up plastic sheeting, hanging them in clusters which are strangely anthropomorphic, on the wall. She photographed herself lying on a bed covered with a mass of string like spaghetti. 
Hesse also used rubber tubing which she filled with plaster to create sausage like forms. Her work often made allusion to the male and the female body. 
The preoccupation with tubular forms and repetition characterises Hesse's later works, Accession and Accretion. Accession consists of two black 50cm square panels mounted on the wall with a space between them with a mass of silver wires running between them. Accretion is a row of 1.5m long fibreglass tubes, slightly crumpled, leaning against wall placed at regular intervals.
Similar works are a casual arrangement of caved in fibreglass cylinders about 70cm high and 50 cm wide standing on their ends on the floor, and rows of latex spheres on a sheet.
Hesse died prematurely of a brain tumour.
!Higgs Matthew.
British curator and artist born in the early 60s who makes works on paper. Higgs's dry witty conceptual work re-presents book covers, pages from books and titles, making plays on words and puns. He will often make a text work in which a book, or a title is transformed by minimal intervention into a reference to the visual. One series of works involved obliterating all the words on a page except the words 'There is a painting on the wall' and placing this framed page next to a painting by another artist. 
Higgs also made an artists publication series called Imprint 93 (from 1993 onwards) for which he invited  artist to make a small pamphlet. This activity bridges his practise as an artists and his work as a curator.
!Hiller Susan.
British artist born in the 1950s, Hiller makes installations usually using video often projected, works on paper and often three dimensional objects and structures. She is interested myth, religion, ritual, literature, grand narrative, and psychoanalysis. Belshazzar's Feast made in the late 80s takes the biblical reference of its title as a starting point for one work. A sitting room 'set' with sofas, coffee table, lamps and sideboard contains a television where the fireplace would be. On the television is a film of showing a burning fire, as if to illustrate that TV is the hearth around which we gather. On the wall is automatic writing (a reference to the writing on the wall by the unseen hand that takes place at Belshazzar's Feast) done by Hiller.
Another work features giant shadow puppets of Punch and Judy and other folkloric characters, made into a film projected to cover all four walls of a gallery space. The silhouetted characters are looming and distorted. 
Hiller made a site specific work at the Freud Museum in London (the home of Sigmund Freud). She presented a series of cardboard boxes containing text and objects, and also made photographs of Freud's ethnographic collection.
!Hilliard John.
British artist who makes photographs of landscape and architecture, presented on wall mounted light boxes. 
!Hiorns, Roger.
!Hirschorn Thomas.
German artist born around 1960 who makes large scale environments and installations. Hirschorn's work evolves from a life as art philosophy that leads him to create environments and make related publications. He uses found imagery, and all manner of materials in his work, building large architectural structures in a homemade way, using cardboard, silver foil, plastic bags, and any number of other found items and materials. 
!Hirst Damien.
British artist born in the early 1960s who the best known contemporary artists in Britain when he exhibited a shark preserved in formaldehyde in a glass tank titled The Impossibility of Death in the Mind of Someone Living. 
Hirst is considered to have been the impetus fro a generation of British artists known as the Young British Artists (abbreviated to YBAs) because he curated an exhibition in an empty warehouse title Freeze. This featured his contemporaries (they were all still students) at a time when it was nearly impossible fro young artist to exhibit their work in London galleries. The term YBAs went on to become the collective name for all of Hirst's generation, including Jake and Dinos Chapman, Tracy Emin, and Sarah Lucas. 
Hirst's work was purchased by a London Collector and advertising executive Charles Saatchi, who financed the shark and subsequent formaldehyde works. Saatchi had the only gallery in London at the time big enough to exhibit these works. 
Hirst made a number of formaldehyde works including a cow and her calf sliced right through into two vertical halves, exhibited in a tow pairs of tanks so that the viewer could walk throng the centre of the cow and a sheep. He also made a work consisting of a glass wall case filled with rows of fish of different species preserved by taxidermy, all facing the same way (Fish Swimming in the Same Direction)
Hirst has made many paintings which fall into three series, the spin paintings (done on a circular canvas spinning horizontally which has multicoloured gloss paint thrown at it), the spot paintings (multicoloured gloss paint spots on white arranged in rows) and the butterfly paintings. The first butterfly paintings were canvases covered with gloss paint in plain colours in the surface of which adhered many different types of butterfly. Most recently Hirst has been covering the canvas entirely with the wings of butterflies, arranged in geometric patterns. Hirst's paintings are made by assistants to his instructions and produced in large numbers. The spot paintings have become Hirst's logo, and these signature spots adorned first the restaurant that Hirst opening with Charles Saatchi in London, The Pharmacy, then a Thames riverboat which takes visitors between the two Tate galleries, and finally an unmanned space vehicle which has been sent to Mars.
Life and death are the major preoccupations of Hirst's work made evident by his use of  formaldehyde, insects, and medical drugs. 
Following his work with butterflies, Hirst used flies; a cows head was placed inside a 3m high glass tank, and maggots were allowed to breed in it. These turned into flies, which could fly around inside the tank and an interconnected one in which there was a desk and a chair. The flies eventually having lived their entire lives in the sealed tank. 
Medical drugs are used by Hirst to represent both life and death and he has made several works using bottles, cartons, boxes, tubes, and other containers of drugs. These are arranged in glass wall cases their predominantly white packaging with blocks of a huge variety of colours giving a particular visual effect when seen in the context of a gallery. Hirst made a room sized installation titled Pharmacy which contained these case on all the walls as well as some of the oversized red green and blue coloured glass decanters that are seen in pharmacists windows. 
Hirst also made a feature film with many of his friends as actors, shot on 35mm with high production values. In 2003 Hirst continues to make formaldehyde works, for example glass tanks containing animals heads with knives plunged into them, or with blood spattered on the wall around them.
Damien Hirst's work is often lampooned by the national press in cartoons.
!Hitchens Ivon.
British painter working  from the 1940s onwards, died in the 1980s. Hitchens painted landscapes and vegetation and flowers with loose flowing brushstrokes leaving parts of the canvas unpainted. He favoured subdued earth colours, purples, blues and greens. Hitchens often used an unconventional panorama format canvas.
!Hobbypop.
Group of German and British artists who work collaboratively. Hobbypop are based in London. Their paintings are often installed both on the wall and propped at angles in the centre of a gallery space. The group also initiates events including guided walks, and theatrical performances.
!Hoberman Niki.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes large (up to 3m high) paintings of children. These are done from photographs taken by the artist of children she meets. They are painted as if there is a strange often green or blueish light cast upon them. The children have exaggerated features and seem to exist in some other world. There is something sinister yet saccharine about the children. Hoberman applies paint smoothly and thinly, soften out all brushstrokes. 
!Hockney David.
One of the best known of all British twentieth artists. Hockney was born in the 1940s and began his career in the 1960s, being successful while he was still at college. His paintings are figurative, at this early stage he often added text as in a work titled We Two Boys Together Swinging with the title in the painting in white letters.
 Hockney made a painting called The Bigger Splash which is instantly familiar. It shows an empty swimming pool and diving board with a villa in the background. Hockney uses acrylic paint and the pools is rendered in flat aquamarine. There is a splash in the water as if someone had just dived in, painted in white paint, perhaps one of the best representations of a splash in the history of painting. Hockney went to live in Los Angeles and the swimming pool became his favourite subject. He became very good at painting water and also painted in water colour on tiled sheets of watercolour paper. He continues to paint in watercolour in 2003, perversely making large scale full length portraits in this apparently unsuitable medium. 
Drawings constitute a major part of Hockney's oeuvre. He used a single confident line, pencil or pen, and also made a number of lithographs and etchings. His drawings are restrained and monochrome in comparison to his often bright paintings. He drew his friends and lovers incessantly, and his mother, as well as still lives, flowers, landscapes, and animals, in particular cats and dogs. In fact Hockney's work is extremely traditional in terms of his subject matter, it was the means of execution that attracted attention to him.
Another well known painting by Hockney is Mrs and Mrs Clarke at Home, a double portrait of Ossie and Celia Clarke, fashion designer friends of Hockney's, seated on opposites of a bay window in their London home, a cat next to them.
Hockney also designed a number of stage productions fro opera companies, notably the Magic Flute and The Rakes Progress. He painted backdrops and flys, as well as designing costumes. 
!Hodgkin Howard.
British painter born in the 1940s who makes abstract paintings. Hodgkin uses very bright jewel like colours, applied in large often round repeated splodges, the paint continuing right off the edge of the canvas onto and round the frame. Hodgkin's paintings are never larger than2 metres high. He sometimes uses round and oval shaped canvases. Hodgkin has made work for public commission including the massively enlarged photomural of brushstrokes that fills the windows of the circular Imax cinema in London. 
!Hofer Candida.
Photographer born around 1960 who has photographed zoos, libraries and various other institutions. Her images often show glamorous ornate surroundings yet have a cool restrained atmosphere. 
!Hogarth William.
British artist and satirist, Hogarth documented eighteenth century London life in his highly detailed drawings and engravings. His series of engravings of Bedlam the notorious London hospital for the insane are famous for their accurate depiction of a horrific place few had seen. Hogarth's portrayal of English daily life is invaluable for historians. 
!Hokusai.
Japanese eighteenth century artist who is best known for his painting The Wave which shows a towering wave with a foamy crest about to crash down on the surrounding sea. Hokusai worked in traditional style but his subject matter was innovative. He used pen ink and pigment on paper.
!Holbein.
English artist working during the reign of Elizabeth the first. Like all artists of the time Holbein largely painted to commission, recording the members of and activities of the royal Court. He made several distinctive standing portraits of the Queen including one in which she is wearing a white embroidered dress decorated with pearls, of her father Henry VIII, and many other people, usually between 50cm and one metre high. He also painted Sir Thomas More. The Ambassadors is a very well known Holbein. This painting shows two bearded ambassadors dressed in doublet and hose with cloaks edged with ermine and hats, standing either side of a sideboard and table. On the furniture are casually arranges various items, tropes, that represent elements of learning and knowledge which are prerequisite to public life. Some of these are obvious, like the globe and compasses, the bible, others more obscure to us today. In the centre of the painting is a curious trompe l'oeil feature. Invisible to the viewer standing in front of the painting but for a smear like element, viewed from a specified angle a skull can be seen. It is speculated that Holbein merely wanted to display his virtuosity, or that the painting was intended to be hung on a stairway where it would be reliably encountered at the requisite angle. 
!Holzer Jenny.
American artist born in the 1950s who works in a variety of media using text. During the 1980s Holzer made a series of works featuring popular sayings slightly altered that she titled Truisms. These political statements subverted commonly used truisms to make points about consumerism, subjectivity and art. The Truisms featured on T shirts, Till receipts in shops, park benches, digital display boards about Times Square and Piccadilly Circus, LED displays in public places, posters and leaflets. Holzer employed the various methods of public display and dissemination of information for the presentation of her work. Holzer was a contemporary of Barbara Kruger.  
!Home Stewart. 
British artist and writer has written about punk, art and performance, including writing and photographing a performer known as Cosey Fanni Tutti. Home has also made work on paper and installation, such as the Art Bed, an ordinary single bed with a bedspread on it on which the viewer is invited to recline. Homes' subject is art itself. 
!Hopkins, Georgie.
British artist born in the late 1960s. 
!Hopper Edward.
American artist who worked from the 1940s to the 1970s. Now dead. Hopper painted American life, mostly but not all urban scenes. He was particularly good at painting night scenes. His painting showing a bar at night is very well known. The bar is long and low, green on the outside with mostly black on the canvas, but glowing with bright yellow light from its interior. Inside is a woman waitress and a few solitary male customers. Another painting shows a petrol station at night, another a truck on a lonely road stopped by a payphone, caught in the headlights of an oncoming car. Hopper seems to depict a melancholy loneliness that he found in modern life. His paintings are extremely popular. They are painted in a realistic yet painterly way.
!Horn Rebecca.
Artist born in the 1950s who makes sculptures that are largely concerned with the human body. Horn's work is often presented as installations and she sometimes makes kinetic work. An example of this is apiece called The Conductor which is a white non-sighted persons stick suspended in space over a metal plate. An invisible magnetic field causes the stick to dance spasmodically, making a tapping and scraping sound when it touches the metal plate, as if it were wielded by an invisible person. This is quite threatening for the viewer. A piano hangs from ceiling, its insides exploding out periodically, catching the viewer unaware as the lid springs open and all the strings sound and the hammers slide out.
Another work consists of a large number of hoses strewn over a whole exhibition space, all leading to something which is operated by an air bladder. The hoses are full of compressed air and the whole operation seems to be random. 
A work which is visually similar to this is a dressmakers dummy holding a dress made from transparent tubing which is full of red liquid, which is pumped around the dress as if the tubes were an arterial system. 
Horn has also made a dress, again displayed on a dummy, from large pieces of meat sewn together. 
Much of Horn's work involves the construction of strange garments, such as the gloves with 50 cm long pointed finger extensions. She often makes work which impedes and transforms the function of the body, or places its inner workings on the outside. Her work has a fairy tale atmosphere that is quite frightening. 
!Horn Roni.
American artist working from the 1980s onwards. 
!Houshiary Shiraz.
British artist who makes sculptures, often of metal, that make reference to Islamic architecture. The surfaces of the sculptures are often etched with Arabic text and Islamic patterning. 
!Howell Kate.
American video artist born in the 1960s. 
!Howson Peter.
Brigit artist who makes paintings and drawings, who went to several war zones as a war artist.
!Hoyland John.
British painter who makes figurative paintings.
!Huebler Douglas.
Canadian conceptual artist.
!Hughes Des.
British artist born in the 1970s who makes resin cast sculptures of junk. For example Hughes might model old nails in clay and cast them, painting them to look exactly like the real thing.
!Hugonnier Marine.
French artist who made a film over several months spent in Afghanistan.
!Hume Gary.
British artist who has represented Britain at the Venice Biennale. Hume is one a group of artists known as Young Births Artists or YBAs who emerged in the early 90s. Hume began his career making paintings from hospital doors which he painted all over with white or cream coloured gloss paint. In fact gloss household paint has become Hume's trademark and he never uses anything else. Hume went on to make figurative paintings mostly portraits in which he vastly simplified the elements of a face, so that it consisted of one or two areas of colour and no features. The paint was poured on, allowed to dry, and then the next colour added. The edge of the dry part acts as a barrier to the next colour. 
Hume painted the model Kate Moss, a virgin Mary and other images taken from famous paintings, and a baby. He featured plants, and vague interiors, but all in his vastly simplified blocks of shiny colour. The paintings nearly resist interpretation which is Hume's intention.  
Hume made a video in which he portrays himself as King Canute. Around 2000 he began to make sculptures, bronze snowmen, very smoothed out shapes painted in shiny pale colours. 
!Huttner Per.
Swedish artist who make socially inclusive work in which he often invites people to participate. 
!Huwys, Bethan.
Welsh artist who makes very small scale sculptures from natural materials. 
!Huyghe Pierre.
Swiss artist who makes photographs and videos. A photograph of his shows a stone frozen in mid air over a motorway. The stone looks like a boulder implying impending tragedy as it will surely cause a terrible accident, until the viewer realises that it is a tiny pebble that has been photographed as it drops from a bridge over the speeding cars. 

!Ichige Yasu.
Japanese artist living in London, Ichige makes videos usually featuring himself. In one he places a car directly in front of a wall and puts his foot on the accelerator, driving into the wall without moving until the tyres catch fire and the engine gives up. Another shows Ichige enter an amusement arcade dressed in full racing driver gear. He sits in a seat in front of a racing game and plays the game, winning the race without dropping any points. We see the game form his point of view. 
!Immendorf Jorges.
German artist whose career began in the 1970s, Immendorf achieved success in the 1980s with his figurative expressionist German twentieth century history paintings often featuring the eagle of the third Reich, nightclub and café scenes and musicians. Immendorf's earlier work had been performative. He invented a concept called Lidl, which consisted of an imaginary town and once trailed a brick with the world Lidl on it in and out of shops. Immendorf got arrested by the police a few times for these activities.
!Ingres.
French nineteenth century painter who made a painting of a seated nude which is known all over the world. Ingres showed the woman seated on a bed with her back to the viewer, a white sheet draped round her hips, and a red patterned scarf wound round her head, the face just visible in profile. It has been remarked that that drawing of the figure is anatomically inaccurate and that if the woman were to stand up her body would be very out of proportion. 
!Innes Callum.
Born in the 60s Innes 90s works a series of views of London, mainly rooftops and other banal cityscapes painted entirely in white relying on brush marks and texture to render the image. 
!Inventory.
British artist group who make socially engaged and political work and publish a journal, Inventory. Inventory's work usually consist of installations, usually some kind of stand or kiosk like structure, then a variety of photographic and text works. They take the position of proselytisers for a new way of life, highlight the dystopian elements in everyday life and imagining collective actions. An example is a photo collage of London  in 2002 showing the city as if it were about to collapse. Inventory have spray painted anti-American slogans on the walls of a museum in which they were invited to show. 
!Irvin Bert.
British painter born in the 1930s who made large abstract paintings usually by pouring very brightly coloured paint onto a white ground, sometimes only a few stripes of red yellow, green and blue paint across the bottom corners of a canvas, for example
!Iserman Jim.
American artist who makes multicoloured tapestry quilts and cubes. The tapestries hand stitched in repeated geometric patterns.
!Islam Runa.
twentieth century artist who makes films that are concerned with ethnicity.
!Jackson Vanessa.
British artist born around 1950 who makes abstract paintings.
!Jankowski Christian.
German artist born in the 1970s who makes video, installation and sound work. In Bank, Jankowski hired a security guards to keep people out of a empty disused bank, the result of which was that people wanted to enter a space they ordinarily would have ignored. In another work Jankoswki recorded a board meeting at a national art museum, and then got children to speak the transcript. Because the children had no understanding of the issues discussed they gave the transcript an entirely new and rather odd emphasis. Jankowski often uses actors. In another work he asks actors to record the reading of the extended email correspondence between himself and his girlfriend when they were apart for several months. Again he was interested in the different inflexion and emphases given to the transcript. Jankoswki is interested in what happens if slight subversions are made to everyday materials and locations. 
!Jeffries Neil.
British artist who has made folded and bent sheet metal sculptures from the 1980s onwards. These are figurative, including people vehicles and have a toy like, mad inventor quality. The sculptures are painted.
!Joffe Chantal.
British artist born in the 1960s, Joffe paints children and women in loose expressive style. Her paintings are usually very small, no more that 20 or 30 cm high, made on board with oil paint. She uses photographs as her source. Joffe often exhibits groups of paintings together arranged in a line or on a grid pattern. 

!John Gwen.
British nineteenth century painter, John painted mostly portraits, using a sombre palette. 
!Johns Jasper.
1960s on. American flag paintings are the work he is best known for. In 1960s Johns painted the American flag actual size. This was the first time anyone had used the flag as an image and johns made it into an abstract painting, the flag as abstraction going right to edge of canvas, rather that a painting of a flag. At the time the flag was being burned in protest at Vietnam war? He went on to make a proliferation of flag paintings drawings and lithographs initially red white and blue but then he began changing the colours culminating in a series of white on white flags and even an edition of bronze flag table sculptures. Johns also painted the numbers one to ten and the alphabet, using stencils to draw the shapes.
!Jonas Joan.
American performance artist and musician 70s also worked with Judson church dancers Yvonne Rainer and Merce Cunningham a performance with a cello. 
!Jones Alison.
British figurative painter who began working in the 80s and 90s. at the beginning of 2000 Jones began to make site specific installations by painting directly on walls using projections of shadows (for example of people who might inhabit the room - someone on their hands and knees vomiting, or a cleaner going about their work, painted in flat black silhouette) and by wallpapering architecturally complex sections of the interiors of buildings using multicoloured striped wallpaper.  
!Jones Allen.
British artist born in the 1940s, Jones achieved notoriety in the 1960s and 70s for his sculptures of women as furniture. Feminists in particular found his work hard to accept and he fell from popularity during the 1980s. Jones continues to make his work, the images of some of his sculptures are known all over the world. 
Jones' sculptures are made by taking body casts of women. These are cast in fibreglass, spray painted realistically and other materials added. The sculptures are very beautifully made. A characteristic work is a coffee table - A glass top is placed on the back of a kneeling woman who is wearing a basque, stockings and suspenders and extremely high heels. The clothes and shoes are real. Other furniture includes a similar table top placed on the shins of a woman lying on her back with her legs bent at hip and knee, shins parallel to the floor and bound with cords to form a 'z' shaped support. 
Jones explored the idea of woman as support creating stools (a woman crouched over and bound with an specially made upholstered leather seat strapped on to her back, for example) and other kinds of furniture as well as paintings. The women are rarely depicted without sex shop lingerie, vertiginous heels and a lot of makeup. They always have perfect figures, in short they are images from pornography. The sadistic quality and implications of Jones' work did not endear him to generations of women in the 70s and 80s although his work was enduringly popular. Jones himself refuted any criticism and rarely acceded to the demands to justify his work.  
!Jones Gareth.
British sculptor, formerly a critic and curator, Jones makes singular and highly crafted objects, a red yellow and blue cube with one side missing, a paint handkerchief made by painting several layers of white paint onto a handkerchief and peeling off the paint, a peacock feather stuck into a wall, works using unspoiled cassette tape, stainless steel minimal box floor pieces, a series of plinths made of different materials, some with words on, commenting on the display of art and turning the plinth in art object, a cape made of raffle tickets, works using LP covers, drawing pins, tape, and architectural model-like constructions. Often a homage to a musician, for example David bowie.
!Jones Sarah.
British artist who makes hard edged abstract pattern paintings that take the facades of modern buildings as her subject. She minimises the detail in these and produces brightly coloured grids which have a perspective to them, the grid is in white. The high gloss paint is meticulously neatly applied. 
!Jones Zebedee.
British painter born in the 1970s who makes abstract paintings by putting a vast quantity of very thick oil paint in a single on a canvas (usually under one metre high) and spreading it across the canvas leaving the brush marks readily visible. His paintings are really no more than thick layers of paint and as such they draw attention to the physicality of painting.
!Jorn Asger.
Dutch artist and member of the Situationists. Jorn made collages with torn posters, for example a sixteenth century portrait of a woman in a blue brocade dress has the face defaced by the additions of some paint and a collaged element. 
!Joy Clair.
British painter born in around 1960 who painted skies with clouds in the 1980s. Joys work has subsequently become pattern based.
Judd Ben
British artist born around 1970 who takes photographs that allude to glamour photography, using amateur techniques such as Vaseline on the camera lens. In 2002 he began making short video films that show groups of people involved in photo shoots and film making watching the actors and models, who are never shown. Judd's work thus becomes a study of the viewers, of vicarious pleasures, revealing these men in their absorption.
!Judd Donald.
American artist born in the 1940s Donald Judd became one of the most significant minimal sculptors in the world during the 1960s and 70s. He makes steel boxes, shelf life structures, and repeated units, fabricated with a high attention to detail and immaculately spray painted in a variety of colours. Some sculptures are made of polished metal such as brass, so that they are extremely shiny The structures and units are ambiguous, both suggesting function but obviously non-functional. They are either mounted on the wall, in a horizontal line or one above the other, sometimes in ascending sizes, or placed with precision on the floor. He used metal folding machines with virtuosity, and the I Beam is a favourite material.
Judd brought the qualities of steel fabrication and the modular construction of modernism to his sculptures, simple works that often had a monumental quality.
Judd set up a studio and a foundation in Marfa, Texas, where he has a number of assistants working. He is extremely prolific. 
!Julien Isaac.
British artist who makes films, usually presented in two or threes screen forma. Julien uses 35 mm film and projects his films in especially exhibition spaces. He is interested in masculine identity and sexual and ethnic specificity. His films have a still, dream like quality, with high production values. 

!Kabakov Ilya and his wife.
Russian artist who has made a large number of installations working in partnership with his wife. His work comments on Russian (both Soviet and post-soviet) social order, and on the position of the individual in a totalitarian society. The Kabakov's work is often optimistic and engenders a feeling of the possibility of escape in the viewer. It often has a didactic quality.
A work made in the late 1980s consists of a bedroom constructed inside a gallery space, the structural walls entirely freestanding so that the viewer is presented with a wooden box. Through the open door the viewer can see into the room which is full of books and comic books and magazines, a bed and some rudimentary furniture. A life size realistic dummy of a aircraft pilot who has apparently ejected from a plane has parachuted through the ceiling, bring the ceiling down and spreading rubble over the tiny room. The pilot is suspended, his parachute harness caught in the roof.
The Kabakov's also made a very large scale installation based on Vladimir Tatlin's tower, a spiral construction. This installation consists of the tower, and several interlinked spaces with doors and windows, the whole roughly constructed of unpainted light coloured wood. Viewers enter the structure and negotiate the spaces, eventually climbing to the top of the tower on its helter skelter like steps. At the top of the tower and in the rooms are various objects, images and texts to contemplate, often placed on school desks. There is quite a lot to read. The installation is a kind of maze. 
!Kahlo Frida.
Mexican artist born in 1920s who made figurative paintings, largely self portraits. Kahlo depicted her somewhat tragic life in her paintings, which are graphc and detailed. She shows herself in many guises, with her distinguishing eyebrows which meet in the middle, and her long braided hair and native Mexican dress. She details her gynaecological problems and back problems stemming from a trolley bus accident as a young girl (which eventually took her life prematurely in the 1950s) in her paintings. In particular a number of miscarriages (she shows the foetus outside her womb, still attached by the umbilical cord, for example) and also the restraining corset which she wore to support her spine in her last years. Kahlo's discomfort and anger at her body's inability to fulfil its biological destiny, coupled with a feminist fury at the obstacles faced by a woman trying to succeed as a serious painter at that time, also feed into her work. Kahlo's paintings are disturbing, often horrific but she adds to this her bright colours, her love of pattern and beauty. 
Kahlo lived with the Mexican muralist Diego Rivera for part of her life. 
!Kalf.
seventeenth century painter. Kalf mainly painted still life, one example is a painting of a suit of armour cast onto a chair by a table covered with gold goblets and plates, the whole an array of luxurious things, painted with great facility to create a glow from the candle light reflected in the gold and bronze items. 
!Kandinsky Wassily.
Turn of the century Russian artist who is known for making some the earliest abstract expressionist painting. Kandinsky used bright colours and black on a white ground to make gestural flowing marks. He was interested in the relation of music to sound and some of his paintings are attempts to 'paint' music. Kandinsky was part of Die Brucke, the German artists group founded by Ernst Kirchner. He made some stage backdrops for experimental orchestral works.
!Kane Alan.
British artist who worked with Jeremy Deller on a work called the Folk Archive, a collection of British folk and craft objects.
!Kapoor Anish.
British artist who began in the late 1970s to make works using pigment. Kapoor was first known for his varying shaped piles of very bright and densely coloured pure pigment. These were arranged on the floor of the gallery. Kapoor then began to make spherical and other shaped structures that he placed on the floor and attached to the wall, the interior of which were coated with pigment. The intense matt quality of the dry powder sucked in light creating oddly disorientating effects and tricks of the eye, something like the effects created by the light works of James Turrell. He also made some work in which large boulders of rock were drilled with cavities filled with pigment. Kapoor made an extremely large installation for a London museum which consisted of a 50 metre high 200 metre long trumpet like structure that tapered into a tube running the length of the museums entrance hall and curved into a second funnel. The work was made with a steel frame attached to which is a dark red rubber skin stretched to a smooth tautness.
!Kaprow Allan.
American artist working between the 1960s and 80s, Kaprow is best known for his Happenings in New York in the late 1960s. These were site specific installations in which a performance would happen witnessed by an invited audience. The Happenings were one of the earliest examples of artists making live art in galleries or gallery like spaces rather than in a theatre or studio space. One happening took place in a loft in which a structure of stud wall frames covered with transparent plastic sheeting created a box like space in which the performers could be seen but were obscured. Kaprow wrote a seminal book about the Happenings which was influential for artists making time based work. 
!Katz Alex.
American painter who has made large figurative paintings since the early 1970s. Katz's subjects are landscape and his family. He paints with flat acrylic paint, making each work in a day. The paint is applied simply and with no visible brushstrokes. Katz might paint a row of autumnal trees, trees in blossom, or he might paint his wife (repeatedly), his children, or groups of friends. All are shown in casual poses, yet the figures are presented at one remove, locked in time as if in a photograph. And indeed Katz in painting them from photographs and he is interested in highlighting that conjunction of distance and intimacy.   
!Kawara On.
Japanese artist who began working in the 1960s, Kawara known for a number of conceptual projects and endurance works. I Met I Went is a year of postcards written and mailed to friends and acquatinaces by Kawara every day for a year. The work with which Kawara is usually associated is a still accumulating work called the Date Paintigns, one of which he has made every day since the 1960s. Each identical 50cm high painting has the days date in white numerals on a black canvas.
!Kay Emma.
British artist who makes figurative paintings and portraits.
!Keene John.
British painter chosen to record the Gulf war for Britain. Keene later travelled to Afghanistan to record the war there, having become very interested in the depiction of armed conflict.
!Keifer Anselm|.
Keifer is a German expressionist painter working from the 1970s onwards. Keifer has made paintings and sculpture, both on a large scale, tending towards the monumental. He is often considered to be most important painters of the 1980s because he evolved a contemporary purpose for expressionistic painting and because of the operatic scale of his work. 
Keifer's paintings generally depict bare depopulated interiors, for example one that shows an attic like space, which is entirely composed of painted wooden planks. He uses a restrained palette of earth colours, black and white with dark reds and blues, and his work often has a atmosphere of darkness and foreboding. 
Keifer's sculpture is often presented as an installation, for example a row of hospital beds, the sheets actually made of lead sheet, draped over the beds, in the centre of which are shallow depressions some of which are filled with water, as if to denote the transformation or passing of the occupant of the bed
!Keinholz Ed.
Artist working in from the 1960s to 80s, Keinholz created several installations including the Beanery, a tiny kiosk set up as bar which the viewer could enter. The space inside was disorientating. Keinholz's installations usually featured life-size figures cast from plaster, unpainted, and real furniture, fittings and fabric. 
!Keita Seydou.
An African artist who photographed the extraordinary qualities he found in ordinary people. The photographs are black and white.
!Kelley Mike.
US artist making sculptures and drawing, Kelley has a particular interest in history, politics and the state. Often uses unwanted and second hand materials, car boot and charity shop purchases. Series of formal sculptural works using soft toys, for example a large wall relief made off found soft toys sewn onto a found blanket, which uses abject objects to makes reference to abstract expressionist painting, and highlights craft and homemade qualities, drawing a comparison between different values assigned to cultural production. Similar floor pieces - two soft toy dogs face each other across a small blanket, somehow an encounter that seems serious and eloquent. A number of different sized toys under a large grey blanket entitled Lumpenprole a jokey reference to the greyness and poverty of ordinary working lives and a pun on the German - a lumpy grey blanket is just what the work appears to be.
A large collection of soft toys fills a room neatly laid out on trestle tables, all accompanied by measuring sticks, as if in some kind of statistical survey, post mortem, or the aftermath of a mass disaster like an air crash. The soft toys used to represent human kind in its lowliness and vulnerability. Kelley manages to use these materials in such a way as to produce a formal sculptural works that have a seriousness that belies their existence as discarded and undervalued objects. 
In fact Kelley eschews conventional art materials on the whole. A series of felt banners made in a homespun way, around 6feet by 4 feet, in which he replicates flyers found on the street usually made by grass root organisations bearing populist messages, or even just advertisements. 
An installation called Plato's cave which has large panels with drawings of American leaders like Lincoln accompanied by substantial text all round the wall. Drawing with marker pen and use of handwritten text also common in Kelley's work. 
!Kelly Ellsworth.
US sculptor working born in 1930s best known for his 1960s - 80s work, wall mounted steel shapes, distorted rhomboid, parallelogram, triangular, immaculately spray painted in matt primary colours and black and white, and butted up together one colour and shape against another to create abstract works which are both sculpture and painting and have a contemplative lyrical presence. 
!Kelly Mary.
US artist who made works dealing with feminist issues from the 1970s onwards. Kelly's seminal work is the Post Partum Document, an extremely influential work made in 1973 which documents the birth and emergence into language of her son Kelly. The work takes the form of about thirty framed panels, each containing an object and a text. The object might be a soiled nappy (which helped to gather tabloid attention at the time), an item of clothing. The text details the child's weight and length, age in weeks, and describes his development with notes on his linguistic development. This is a work which documents the birth of her child and her care for his life as a creative activity, making the point that the birth of a child, as revealed here through the banal objects and detail, is a profoundly creative act. This startlingly simple and obvious statement caused a great deal of criticism and publicity, the Post Partum Document was declared not to be art, to be offensive, to be cruel. Questions about the artists motherhood were raised, and from all this it can be deduced that the work made its point very successfully. Motherhood is a taboo, it is work which is not to be considered work, not on a par with artistic achievement and not to be revealed in all its messy detail. 
The works form was important. it was predominantly a text work and this was another of Kelly's points - that text, or language, is how the subject is constituted and that we are not subjects until we are speaking subjects. The emergence of a child into language is therefore the most important moment in our existence and this is made possible by the mother (or primary carer, as we should add today). Kelly drew on the works of Lacan the French literary theorist who developed the theory of the other and the difference between  the male and female subject. and of psychoanalytical writers like Freud, Klein. Lacan's theories on the constitution of the subject were particularly influential for a whole generation of artists in the 1980s, mostly but not all women.
Kelly's later work developed into institutional critique always wall and text based. She made a number of highly crafted metal medallions or wall plaques.
!Kelly Michael UK.
British artist born in the 1940s died 1990s. Kelly made wood and metal abstract sculpture.
!Kerbel Janice.
Canadian artist working in Britain, born in 1969. Kerbels' work is conceptual and usually research based, and is carried out in variety of media, united by her interest in desire and its link to deception. Kerbel's research often involves a degree of controlled risk, and elements of detective work. The place of desire in Kerbels work is elucidated by her invention of utopian places and ideal conditions. 
Kerbels earliest works from the early 1990s concern subterfuge and malicious intent. A table is laden with jam made from poisonous plants. Snippings is a series of minutes snippings taken from the clothes of strangers, who remain unaware of this affront. The snippings are mounted and titled with the date and the place on the garment and type of garment from which the snipping is taken.
