Trust Me (Remington) is typed on a blue sheet of letter paper, folded once, with a ring stain from a coffee mug on it. The letter s has jumped and is higher than the rest as if it had been typed on a manual typewriter in the days when the author of a letter could be traced by mechanical faults in the way a typewriter functioned, such as a raised letter. Accompanying this is a disc containing a digital typewriter style font with a raised s that Kerbel has created.
Kerbel's interest in deception led her to invent four Magic Tricks fro which she designed the apparatus as if it were flat pack furniture. The tricks included levitation table and disappearance box. Like much of Kerbels work the plans and drawings are the work, which is never actually realised. This is intrinsic for the success of the work as it hinges on Kerbel's interest in the desire to do unacceptable or impossible things. 
Places to Bury a Body illustrates this. A series of photo pHs show very ordinary places such as a front garden, where there is no obvious hiding space but the acknowledgment that a hole could be dug. Or a cliff top over which a body could be thrown. Bank Job (1999) consists of two cork board panels onto which are pinned detailed plans for a bank robbery from an existing bank in the City of London. There are reconnaissance photographs, detailed floor plans, a map of the getaway route, and an exhaustive time line of the robbery, listing all the participants, entrances and exits, times and vehicles involved. Bank Job is a work which presents the inconceivable as something which anyone could carry out.
A number of Kerbel's works are concerned with how to cheat. Home Fittings is a series of floor plans, made for existing rooms and buildings, on which Kerbel indicates where a person might walk in order to avoid any creaking floorboards or flooring, and where a person might stand in relation to doors to avoid casting a shadow that might give away their presence. These places are indicated in the space by the insertion of thin brass rods flush with the floor. Marked Decks are prints showing the backs of packs of playing cards, the patterns on which have been subtly altered to allow a person in the know to deduce the suit and number of the card. A letter typed on blue paper seems to allude to a failed tryst, an unfulfilled desire. A number of Kerbel's works deal with this explicitly, for example Bird Island (2000) a web project and book. Kerbel invented an island paradise based on a real uninhabited Caribbean island, and designed a website that invited the purchase of timeshare in the island. The topography, flora, fauna and the style of the buildings on the island are explained and illustrated in detail. The site persuade the viewer to buy into a dream which doesn't exist. Any applicants would receive a brochure. Kerbel even invented a bird, the Exuma Emerald, which she later illustrated by commissioning a drawing from a bird illustrator and the creation of its potential song from a bird song expert, presented as cd. Further to this Kerbel made a number of drawings of fantasy islands in which she played god, taking control of the climate and thus the flora and fauna. In 2003 Kerbel made a series of drawings of hypothetical planters for specified but unnamed buildings. The planters and the plants they contained are designed to grow the most suitable plants for the buildings climactic conditions in the best possible way, thus the plant lists take account of light quality and direction, temperature and humidity, the planters make the most of available light and maximise the planting relevant to the size of the space. The floor plans indication the light source and type, the scale and disposition of the planters, the function of the building and the climactic conditions There is a respiration garden for a Gym, for example, planters for low light in a Victorian house, a shelf garden for a student room, a hanging garden for a launderette and one for a bathroom both making use of high humidity, and circular garden for a revolving restaurant. 
!Kertesz Andresz.
Eastern European photographer working in the early twentieth century, many of Kertsz's images are instantly familiar, including one of the Arc de Triomphe in Paris, seen from a bird's eye view, with snow on the road surface, the tracks of the few vehicles on the streets at that date inscribing black parabolas around it on the white ground.
!Kilimnik Karen.
American artist born around 1960 who is primarily a painter, although she has begun to make photographic work in the 2000s. Kilimnik's practise is project based. She makes work around a particular theme and usually presents it as an installation. One such project dealt with the fantasy life of teenage girls, and featured a gallery with purple painted walls, drawings on the walls with lots of text which has a diaristic quality, paintings and autumn leaves strewn on the floor. Another had a Russian theme, and has paintings that have qualities of Russian icons, with Russian themes, and snow scenes, objects and stuffed animals and drifts of artificial snow on the floor. Kilimnik's paintings are rarely more than 60 cm high.
Kilimnik has many pets and animals are a constant in her work, in particular a pet cockatoo, a crow called Ralph, and dogs and cats. Ralph's portrait shows him both as he is and covered in ectoplasm (Ralph in Ectoplasm).
Another installation is based on a detailed study of ballet, in particular Giselle, and features gallery walls painted dark red, the paintings and drawings in black frames depicting scenes from the ballet, imagined related scenes, and a sculpture on a plinth. Some of the paintings have black velvet ribbon bows on the frame and silk flowers.
Kilimnik's work hovers close to kitschness and sweetness which is held in check by her skill as a painter and the darkness of some of her subject matter.
In 2001 Kilimnik made a series of photographs of central park in New York showing the park in winter in low light conditions. By careful framing, the light conditions, and aided by the appearance of a horse drawn carriage used to convey tourists on sightseeing tours, Kilimnik showed the park as if it had been photographed in the nineteenth century. These were shown alongside a number of landscapes and cityscapes that are also painted in an anachronistic style. Kilimnik's work is distinctive for its combination of rich reference to the past and the history of art with an autobiographical and contemporary approach to painting.
!King Philip.
King is a British artist who made large steel sculptures between the 1960s and 80s. King made a number of public commissions many of which can still be seen, for example there is one in Fulham in London, a red painted tubular steel structure a bit like a climbing frame. King also made the designs for the decoration of the walls of Embankment underground station in London.
!King Scott.
King is a British artist born in the 1960s and publisher of an magazine called Crash!. Crash! is a broadsheet sized magazine written and designed by King and another person, which features anti-establishment satire and polemic mixed with cultural criticism and appreciation in the form of poster style text, graphics and images. It is witty and uncompromising, and is a hybrid of magazine and art work which is implicitly critical of the magazine publishing industry. King's solo work draws on these approaches. He has made collages from magazine images and a large number of text works often in the form of numbered lists. In the 2000s King's texts became more autobiographical and often self deprecating. They are designed and typeset in Kings very visual style. One work is entitled 'Why I Never Became A Punk' and consists of a list of the reasons for this.
!Kirchner Ernst Ludwig.
Kirchner was a German expressionist painter. His paintings are brightly coloured, with angular stylised figures, and lots of brushstrokes. Kirchner was the founder of an artists group called die Brucke, (The Bridge) which published a magazine of critical texts called the Blaue Reiter (Blue Rider).
!Kirk, Joanna.
!Kitson Linda. 
British painter and war artist.
!Klee Paul.
French painter working at the beginning of the twentieth century. Klee's work is delicate and colourful. He made a large number of small paintings and an enormous range of works on paper, both drawing and printmaking. Klee used thin drawn lines to make geometric patterns, often repeated triangles or squares, with dots, which would be coloured and often resemble patchwork. Sometimes these remained as abstract patterns, at other times Klee would draw figures, trees, buildings or animals. 
!Klega Michael.
German artist born in the 1960s, Klega makes wall drawings form unspoiled cassette tape wound round map pins, sculptures from plastic water bottles. He utilises everyday materials like adhesive tape to make his minimal but often dryly humorous works.
!Klein Jochen.
German artist born in the 1960s died in the late 1990s. Klein's water colour and collage paintings have an oneric atmosphere, as if the protagonists were lost in time. The collage, usually an image from a magazine often a person, is often the starting point for a painting. Klein extended the collaged image by painting out from it. He used thin washes of colour on a white ground, often leaving areas deliberately unfinished, to make translucent and light filled images. He uses Klein also made works on paper including a pop up book made from collaged images featuring a pop up penis as its centrefold. 
!Klein Yves.
French artist who was working from the late 1940s to the 60s. Klein's subject was the condition of art, and he made several works which gained him notoriety, in particular the Blue Paintings. These were made as a performance in a gallery in Paris. Klein invited female models to act a human brushes. He applied ultramarine blue paint to their naked bodies and then directed them to position themselves and move their bodies in certain ways, making body prints onto paper he had placed on the floor and walls. Klein's idea was to use the nude to paint the nude. The resulting body prints were exhibited, but the photographic documentation of the event became better known. Later on he made some fire and smoke paintings for which he set fire to canvases and marked them with smoke. 
ultramarine blue was Klein's trademark colour. All his painted work and sculpture was made using it. He made a large number of paintings in the 1950s and early 60s using natural sponges stuck to canvas and the whole painted blue. He also made simple blue canvases. Similarly he made sculptures with the sponges, often creating tableaux of his flower or seaweed like creations.
Klein worked briefly in ceramics, making small sculptures that drew on porcelain table ornaments; Klein made his own lumpen thrown together and glazed versions that nevertheless made their sources clear. 
The other strand to Klein's work is more conceptual. He made many works with found objects and works which were events and documentations of actions. He made a work with a light bulb and several works with furniture.  
The best known of these works are the Leap into the Void, a staged photograph in which Klein is seen apparently leaping off a high wall in a street and falling through the air. There is a house on the left and a lamppost behind him. The photograph is about 40 cm high, portrait format. Paris Air was a sealed glass vial containing nothing but air, to which Klein accorded a status as an art work and a monetary value. In a performance work Klein sold gold leaf which he had authenticated to collectors and then invited them to throw the work they had just bought into the river Seine. He also printed his own bonds, and sold these as works of art. The work which summed up Klein's interests most completely was The Void. This work was a staged event. Klein rented an empty gallery. He sent out invitations to the opening of his exhibition, provided champagnes and hired a commissionaire to stand on the door. No one was allowed to enter the gallery without an invitation. When they did so they found an empty gallery - the void of the title.  
!Klimt Gustav.
An Austrian artist working at the turn of century based in Vienna, klimt painted highly decorative images of women with particular attention to the reproduction of the patterns on textiles and clothes the models are wearing. He also painted landscapes, giving the same attention to the 'pattern' of leaves and flowers, such as in his series The Four Seasons, paintings of woodlands and gardens in seasonal colours. Klimt had a very distinctive style of drawing the figure, giving his almost exclusively female subjects a kind of languid beauty. They are often draped in decadent poses or staring straight at the viewer giving an impression of forthrightness, and unabashed hedonism. One example is a painting of a standing woman, her head almost resting on one shoulder, wearing an extraordinarily ornate dress and cloak which has a pattern of multicoloured and gold squares. In fact Klimt's trademark was his use of tooled and flat gold leaf applied to the patterns on fabric in his paintings. He also tended to delineate the shape of the body by the outline of the clothes, so that the figure often appeared to be a solid block. These attributes give Klimt's work a graphic effect. Another example is a painting of four women who are lying together on a bed. Klimt's paintings often show women together in intimacy, lying together in an embrace. In this painting the four are intertwined and seen in a kind of false perspective as if from above. It is hardly possible to tell which limbs belong to which head. The fabrics of their clothes are mixed together too so that the composition is a swirl of heads, limbs and pattern, creating a kind of round shape within the frame of the canvas. 
!Kline Franz.
American artist who made abstract expressionist paintings from the 1950s onwards. Kline's paintings usually feature large black gestural marks made with a big brush, on a the white or even un-primed ground of enormous canvases. Much of the ground is left untouched but it becomes an active part of the surface of the painting.
!Knall Ulli.
Swiss artist living in Britain who makes ceramic sculptures. Knall has made a series of small busts of characters from television programmes such as Star Trek, and other invented characters, as well as portraits of her friends. These are modelled in a simple almost naïve style. Other works include small fountains. Most of Knall work is on a domestic scale, ranging between 30 to 80 cm high. The sculptures are painted and glazed carefully in realistic colours. 
!Knur Karen.
British artist born around 1960 whose conceptual text and photographic image work is concerned with the interrogation of the institution.
!Koetther Jutta.
Koetther is a German artist born in the 1960s who makes large messy paintings and drawings with text added. These are apparently autobiographical and personal but remain mysterious. The paintings are large and scruffy, often appearing to have been made on the floor over a period of time.
!Kokoschka Oscar.
German artist who painted before and after the first world war, figurative works.
!Kollwitz Kathe. 
Austrian artist contemporaneous drawings of the holocaust mainly in graphite and charcoal, often of women.
!Koons Geoff.
American artist, formerly a banker, born in the late 1950s who became known in the 1980s for his photographic works based on advertisements. Koons invented magazine advertisements based on existing ones and had them professionally photographed featuring himself as the model. He then placed these ads in art magazines in effect advertising himself. This strategy was intended to show that an artist and a career could be invented and promoted. 
Having achieved this Koons began to make sculptural works. He first made a marble bust of himself, which he had carved by the best stone carver he could find. It was whiter marble with a slight sparkle to it. 
Koons was initially one of number of new York artists making work that became known as Neo Geo. His first works were objects in glass cases, fro example a baseball, apparently suspended in mid air inside a vitrine on a plinth, and a number of pristine vacuum cleaners in a large case (probably a pun on the idea of the airless vacuum). These early works relied on the purchase and representation of readily available objects, with a nod Duchamp and later influencing Damien Hirst.
Koons then began to design sculptures based on trinkets and souvenirs enlarged to many times their original size and presented on plinths. These works of art called attention to the status of the work of art, its value, the skill of its making and its authorship, because Koons rarely made anything himself. Instead he ordered his works to be made to his detailed plans by the best crafts people he could find in the medium that interested him.
The first was a serried of porcelain sculptures for example one of Michael Jackson reclining with a poodle, some large flowers taken from a children's model set, and one of an enlarged children's Cabbage Patch Kid doll. The sculptures were made by the best porcelain artists (in eastern Europe), hand painted, gilded. They were extremely kitsch but very striking due to their size (about one metre high usually). Koons was interested in how an object of little value other than sentimental value which may however have cost the owner quite a lot (the nature of souvenirs) might be transformed into an art object of immense value despite its bad taste, lack of traditional fine art values and lack of the artists hand in its making. 
Koons made several groups of works in a similar way, for example cast metal which was highly chromed (a train set which is a decanter glasses and coaster holder, a balloon rabbit), and polychromed wood vases of flowers, extremely detailed pieces of virtuoso carving made in Germany by master carvers.  
He later made a number of extremely large site specific works, the flower sculptures, for which he again borrowed the talents of others. The first of these was a 20 metre high Scottie dog covered with flowers the colours of which in some way functioned like the dots on a billboard poster.
In the mid 1990s Koons married la Cicciolina and Italian porn actress and made a series of photographic works which were styled as stills from a porn film, using professional stylists and photographers once again. The images had a degree of shock value since some featured clearly visible penetration. Koons and Cicciolina stared out at the viewer. The images were odd hybrids of pornography and portrait.
After a much publicised divorce from la Cicciolina Koons began in the late 1990s to make 2 or 3 metre high paintings of enlarged parts of things, such as balloons, mixtures of toys, household objects and natural things, so enlarged and partially valise as to be hard to interpret. These are painting by a studio full of assistants to Koons's designs. 
!Kounellis Janis.
Greek artist who was very successful in the 1980s when he made a number of large installations. Kounellis used the image of the horse quite a lot, as well as metal constructions and soot or smoke-like pigment. His subject matter was to do with the relation of cultural history to the modern world. 
!Kovats Tania.
British artists who uses model making materials to construct landscapes on shelves mounted on the wall, grassy hills and valleys with trees, rivers and lakes, microcosmic three dimensional landscapes.
!Krasner Lee.
Krasner was an abstract painter who married Jackson Pollock and found herself nursing him through alcoholism to his death. She established a foundation in their joint names which through its grant making activities contributes to the careers of many artists. Krasner is now dead.
!Kruger Barbara.
Kruger made a seminal body of work in the 1980s using photographic collage and text. Her work is feminist and political. Using skills developed in her paid job in graphic design Kruger began to juxtapose generic images with generic phrases and slogans that she slightly altered to make a political point. She appropriated styles from Russian constructivist art and socialist graphics. For example, a female hand with long immaculately manicured nails in black and white with the slogan in bold Helvetica black 'protect me from what I want' diagonally across the image. A closed eye appropriated from a surrealist photograph is captioned 'I am your almost nothing'. One of her most copied slogans is 'I shop therefore I am'. Kruger's skill was in the juxtaposition of slogan and image producing a powerful effect. She rarely used colour but when she did it was almost always red. Her work was produced as postcards and achieved wide dissemination making her one of the best known artists of her generation.
!Krystufek Elke.
Austrian artist born in 1970s whose subject is herself and her own body portrayed in videos, drawings and paintings of herself in her daily life. However Krystufek often appears naked, usually made up with the red lipstick which is characteristic of the videos and the drawings, sometimes in an abject state and her work is also a portrait of neurosis. Her emotional state is usually unexplained and never indulgent. A video self portrait, for example is shot with a hand-held (thus never more than arms length away) as she spins around dancing. Her own apartment is often the setting for the work. 
!Kuitca.
Painter
Kupka Frantisek.
Painter
!Laderman Ukeles Mierle.
American artist who made a collaborative work with the New York Sanitation Department in the 1980s. Laderman Ukeles staged a performance in which she shook the hand of every sanitation worked in New York, an action that highlighted and reversed the desired invisibility of waste disposal, unwillingness to consider the quantities and marginalisation of those that carry out a daily task without which life would become untenable. Another part of this project was the design and construction of a refuse collection cart with mirrored sides. This cart was used on the daily round, and as it travelled the city streets the people that created the refuse that it held were reflected clearly in its sides.
!Laib Wolfgang.
artist working from the 1990s onwards, Laib makes installations. He made a floor work by sifting bright yellow pollen over a large rectangle shape in an even layer, the colour startlingly bright. The effect of this work lies in the contrast between the minimal quality of the work, the quantity of the material and the viewers knowledge of the organ and potential of the material and the work required to obtain it.
!Lambie Jim.
British artist working from the 1990s onwards who made a number of floor installations in which the floor of a gallery or museum is entirely covered in stripes made of plastic tape in different colours and widths, the stripes following the floor plan of the room, running round the edge of the room and moving inwards towards the centre of the room concentrically until the centre is reached. Sometimes Lambie might use black and whiter tape, or metallics. Lambie also made wall sculptures from ordinary domestic objects such as record player turntables, mattresses, mirrors which are covered in glitter. 

!Landers Sean.
American artist born in the 1960s who made a number of very large text paintings in the 1990s. the text on these paintings is essentially a diary of his life as an artist. In these works Landy tries a new way of carrying out the traditional task of the artist to make life into art. The canvas of one 3 meter high painting rolls onto the floor at the bottom, reaching toward the viewer implying a physical inclusion in the work, and in the viewing and reading space. 
Landers went on to make a series of very large paintings after famous artists such as Picasso, copying works and adding to them, so that they are instantly recognisable but recognisable different.
!Landseer Edwin.
British nineteenth century portrait painter who also painted dogs. 
!Landy Michael. 
Landy is a British artist who was born in the 1960s. He began career making obsessively detailed cartoon-like drawings in black pen featuring a multitude of images and text. These drawings created an alternative world of social and political comment and from them emerged a large scale installation, Scrapheap Services in the late 1990s. Scrapheap Services is a fictitious company which carries out the collection and disposal of unwanted members of society. What Landy showed was a collection of the trolleys, uniforms, printed literature, signage, bins and waste bags, all bearing the companies logo and livery. The paper literature was stern all over the floor as if it were itself rubbish. He also made a flower stall. Landy's next large scale work further developed his interest in human waste and the value of inanimate objects, sentimentality and materiality. This involved the professional construction of a large waste disposal plant consisting of a conveyor belt for sorting, various grading machines and a compressing, shredding and grinding machines, reducing everything to a pulp. Landy passed every single item had every owned through this machine: the contents of his flat, his car, his clothes. The item he found most difficult to destroy was his deceased fathers coat. The machinery was installed in a temporarily empty department store ground floor and open to the public every day. The list of items destroyed remains as the document of the work.
In 2003 Landy made a number of botanical drawings of weeds species and varieties that might be found growing in the streets and waste areas of London.
!Lane Abigail.
Born in eh 1960s Lane has made a number of installations. For one of these she papered a gallery space a wall paper printed with bloody hand prints. In the centre of the room a female figure constructed from a collection of clothes, shoes and a wig hung from a rope. 
!Langlands and bell.
Two artists who work together producing works that investigate architecture and the built environment and its relating to the lives of people. In the late 1990s they made a work which consisted of a series of white chairs with models of buildings set into seats visible through a glass top to the seat. They made a series of works about the panopticon plan for prison buildings. 
!Lanyon Peter.
St Ives painter one of a group of artist who went to live in St. Ives in Cornwall in the 1930s to avoid London during the blitz and then through the  40s and 50s. chiefly for the quality of the light. St Ives became a centre for artists including Barbara Hepworth and Patrick Heron. Lanyon painted semi-abstract landscapes. Used a lot o green brown and white.
!Lautrec Henri de Toulouse.
Toulouse Lautrec was a graphic artist who was friends with many of the artist of fin de siecle Paris. He is best known for his posters for the Folies Bergere in particular for one showing a row of can can dancers. He was a superb draughtsman. Often portrayed in films set in the period and known suffer from a medical condition that reduced his growth. 
!Leahy Matthew.
British artist born in the 1970s died in 1999. Leahy made landscape paintings in particular snow capped mountains.
!Leapman David.
British artist who began working in the mid 1980s. His paintings used repeating motifs on a plain ground, the lines themselves removed from the surface of the canvas revealing that under the plain flat colour is very bright multicoloured painting. He later began to use fluorescent colours. 
!Leckey Mark.
British artist born 1976 makes video and sound installations. Leckey's work comes out of club and street culture, but has a serious intent. His video Fiorucci made me hardcore shows intercuts footage of the British northern soul scene with hardcore house clubs, edited to make a compelling testament to transcendence through dance and music. Other works come out of his experience as a dj.
!Lee Rosa.
one of generation of British women artist born in the early 1960s who developed a self defined 'feminist abstraction' that owed much to Brigit riley. Their work defined itself by its relation to 80s and 90s feminist critique of abstract painting, in particular that claiming the painting as the body, and painting an act of 'writing the body'. Lees work employed repeated decorative elements such as bows and ribbons on a brushstroke ground. Rebecca Fortnum, Vanessa?? Therese Oulton.
!Leger Fernand.
French painter 1920s distinctive style of painting figures as if their bodies are composed of tubular elements, in shaded greys on a whiter ground with occasional additions of primary colours. 
!Lempicka Tamara de.
French artist working in the 1920s and 30s, Lempicka made a number of paintings and small sculptures usually showing herself or other women in a highly stylised graphic style. Lempicka is distinctive in her own work for her straight bobbed hair with low fringe and fashionable clothes. 
!Levine Sherrie.
American conceptual artist who became extremely successful in the 1980s and influenced a generation of younger artists. Levine's primary interest is in the status of the work of art and of the reproduction. In this interest makes evident her interest in the work of Marcel Duchamp. Levine made a number of chequer board works painted on wood, a number of paintings in which she simply reproduced a cartoon character in outline (such as Charlie Brown), a number of black and white photographs by famous photographers, and of a marble torso sculpture which she signed, declaring the work to be then authenticated by her and therefore made by her.
She made some works using wooden ply in which she utilises the oval insert of ply that fills the hole where there was a knot as part of the image, she made some works using gold leaf which dealt with another interest of hers in the perceived versus the intrinsic value of a work of art. Levine's work is almost exclusively small, no bigger than 80cm high. Levine's work essentially borrows the work of other artists, Levin either remaking it (as she did in a number of drawings and watercolours after certain artists, which are then signed and authenticated as by her), or elevating another art from to the status of work of art. Levine took the unacceptable: fakery, plagiarism and false authentication and made it the subject of her work. 
!Lewandowska, Marysia and Cummings, Neil.
British artists working together who make theoretical conceptual investigations into economies, culture and knowledge, resulting in a socio-political project based practise which may be presented in many different forms. They made a project in London with the Bank of England which was the result of a period of research. They presented documentation of their research and made it possible for museum visitors to interact with their work.
!Lewis Mark.
British artist born in the 1950s who works in 35 mm film and video. Lewis's work since the 1980s has stemmed from his interest in and knowledge of the history of cinema. He uses the structure and visual language to construct short looped films presented as installations and longer more narrative scripted films using actors.
Lewis presents everyday scenes and situations through the lens of cinematic production, prompting the viewer to consider the inconsequential as a moment of grandeur, and to question what they are seeing. 
A short looped film made in the late 1990s shot from inside a café A woman is talking to a man outside on the pavement right in front of the window. But there is something odd about their conversation. The man doesn't seem to be listening to the woman. Eventually a car passes and disappears mid screen. Then other traffic does the same, coming from both directions, as do pedestrians. Now the viewer begins to think that they are watching a split screen, a convention occasionally used in cinema to show a telephone conversation or to show two things happening simultaneously, and that in fact the couple are not talking to each other but to others. 
Finally it becomes clear that in fact the dividing on the screen is in fact the edge of a mirror on the wall of the café, which exactly reflect the street outside. The woman is in fact reflected in this mirror and is talking to someone else out of view. The man is seen through the window and is on his own, waiting. 
A film made in a city housing estate shows a concrete walkway with tower blocks rising above it, the vast empty space punctuated and enlivened by children playing games. 
Another film shows a derelict building of which only the façade and floors. The camera moves towards the building extremely slowly. As it moves the viewer becomes aware of the trajectory of the camera, something that is largely unnoticed usually. It is moving in and down from a great height and the shot is continues. The camera reaches the building and enters a window. On a table in the empty roofless space is a child's spinning top. This short looped film places the viewer in the position of the camera and makes the process of filming evident and the subject of the work. The camera is finally understood to be on a very high crane which is also moving on a track, usually only associated with big budget film making. 
Another work shows A section of an black and white silent film showing an elevated cast iron railway upside down. A landscape work shows an iced over lake in a snowy landscape with a solitary skater on the ice.
Lewis has made narrative films using actors, including one shot in a garden in California which uses porn actors, one shot in a Californian desert hotel, Peeping Tom (a homage to the feature film of the same name) and The Pitch, which shows Lewis himself standing in the middle of the main concourse of a railway station talking to the camera, oblivious to the people around him. By the end of his monologue it becomes apparent that he is pitching the idea for the very film that the viewer is watching directly to the viewer.
!Lewisjohn Cedar.
American or Canadian artist who makes abstract pattern paintings and related drawings which reflect his work as a dj.
!Lewitt Sol.
American conceptual artist who made installation and drawings. Lewitt is best known for his wall drawings made in pencil directly onto the wall which is painted either black or white. Lewitt devises a complex geometric pattern of lines, parallel, radiating or on a grid, which is drawn in coloured pencil, forming a barely perceptible drawing which nevertheless has a strong effect on the space and adds glowing areas of colour. 
!Lichtenstein Roy.
American artist working from the 1960s onwards, Lichtenstein's work was among the best known of the pop artists. He made paintings using images from printed cartoon magazines. Lichtenstein took single frames or even a detail from a frame and enlarged it, initially to about one metre high but eventually to a much larger size. He then made a painting form the drawing, reproducing exactly what he saw including any text. Initially the paintings were black and white but Lichtenstein soon mover to colour. The painting s nearly always feature a person, a part of a vehicle and a graphic depiction of fire, speed, force, for example. They are part of a larger narrative which we are left to guess at.
Eventually Lichtenstein began to paint the dots made by the printing process, easily visible in the magazine but very obvious when enlarged. These dots are obvious on billboards when they are viewed close up. By making the dots part of the painting Lichtenstein was reproducing a reproduction and the means of its reproduction. He was actually painting the printing process. He used the cyan magenta and yellow process colours on a white ground, with black. From a distance his paintings appeared to be made in flat colours, from close up the dots were the main component. 
Certain of Lichtenstein's works became iconographic, for example one of a fighter pilot in a cockpit of a plane that has a yellow flash coming from. The text announces the pilots thought at that moment. Another of a glamorous woman crying, again with text revealing her thoughts. His style was and still it copied, plagiarised and borrowed.
!Ligon Glenn.
American artist born in the 1960s who made work about black American identity. A lot of Ligon's work took slavery and its legacy as a starting point. He made installations and added text to them and also made photographic works. A canvas punch bag hanging from the ceiling on a rope has a poetic story of a slave prize fighter stencilled on its white surface in black letters, as if it were both a painting and a sack. This object symbolised the body of the slave, captive to be treated as an object, subjected to punches, traded as goods. A boxing ring has text inscribed around it in a similar way. Ligon made a version of the US Flag drawn in black with words where the stars and stripes would be. In his work he drew attention to the objectification of black Americans, particularly black American men.
!Lin Maya. 
Lin, an American artist, was chosen by competition to design the Vietnam war memorial in Washington. She designed a long highly polished black marble wall, inscribed on which in columns are the names of all those who lost their lives in combat in the Vietnam war. The wall is set into the ground at ground level, and appears to be gradually revealed as the ground in front of it is excavated to slope down. The top of the wall remains level parallel with the ground and the viewer descends passing along the wall which becomes higher in relation to them the further along the wall they walk.
The memorial is extremely popular, it ahs been profoundly successful in conveying the feelings of a generation of the bereaved and indeed of those who did not believe that America should have fought in Vietnam. Perhaps this is due to its simple formal qualities and its gentle interactivity with the viewer. The memorial invites participation and takes the viewer down below ground level, to see the names symbolically buried but rising up to meet the light. Its surface is reflects the viewers, so that a multitude of face appear on its surface. 
!Linke Simon.
British artist born around 1960 who became known in the late 1980s for his post-modern conceptual painting. Linke reproduced advertisements from art magazines on small canvases about 60 cm high, painting the text very accurately but using thick impasto. He chose to reproduce mostly advertisements printed in black and white. The paintings appear from a distance to be exactly what they represent. The texture and application of the paint is what makes them paintings, reducing painting to its fundamentals. These works became iconographic, and were seen to represent a particular moment in conceptual art.
The subject matter of the paintings could be said like much of the art of the time to be reproduction, the condition of reality, the status of the subject. Indeed much critical theory of the time investigated the nature of reality and reproduction under the influence of Baudrillard. Many conceptual artists in the 1980s read the works of Saussure and other writers interested in semiotics. There was a marked emphasis on the constitution of the subject through language, and language and text as a system of signs and signifiers. For Baudrillard and other post-modern theorists, the real world was no more real than the reconstructed world of representation. Linke and other artists like Sherrie Levine made this the subject of their work. There was also a concern for the status of the art object, its aura, which came from readings of the work of Walter Benjamin.
Following this work in the late 1990s Linke began to work with text for its own sake, making the paintings in the same way but not reproducing anything.
!Lippi Fillipo.
medieval Italian artist, painter of religious scenes.
!Lloyd Hilary.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes video films that she terms sculptures. Lloyds films are always presented on a specified size of monitor anything ranging from a portable personal TV with head phones to a large monitor. The monitors are placed on monitor stands of a particular type, in particular relation to each other. Some works may consist of two monitors places back to back. The viewer is never a typically passive receiver, since they have to walk round the monitors and Lloyds work is never shown projected.
Lloyds films have a minimal sculptural quality. She is chiefly interested in people and banal repetitive activities. For example, in Colin, made in the mid 1990s, a young man standing on some derelict land between two buildings proceed to slowly pick up, examine and discard the items that he finds there, pieces of wood and metal and other kinds of refuse. He does this slowly, very deliberately, as if there were some kind of purpose to his actions, which become quite compelling and graceful in repetition.
Colin, like most of Lloyd's work involving people, met the artist in a club and she persuaded him to allow her to film him. She takes pains to allow her subjects to make their own decisions about what they will do as she films, arrived at through social interaction. In this sense she is emphatically not directing the action. 
Other works have featured a man pulling a jumper off over his head played in forward and reverse on back to back monitors so that it becomes a kind of dance. There is one of a young man sitting on the floor cutting images out of magazines endlessly. There is a film in which two teenage girls hold a length of string between them, one has ball and slowly unwinds it the other winds the string back into a ball. The end of the film the girls have transferred the string from one to the other. 
Another film shows a woman in a white trouser suit sitting on a chair one leg resting on the other knee, smoking a cigarette. On the point of extinction of the cigarette the film loop begins again. Most of Lloyd's work is looped, including the works that do not feature people such as one which shows a reflection on the surface of water which is somehow read, the film becoming a shimmering abstract pattern. The films are always static camera single continuous shots, even one in which Lloyd attaches a camera to a skateboard and asks a skateboarder to skate. The resulting film of pavement, ground and then graffiti covered walls as the skateboard goes vertical is accompanied by the sound of the wheels. Similarly a film shot again form a static camera placed on the windowsill of the revolving restaurant in the post office tower show 360dgree panorama of London in the time it takes for the restaurant to revolve - this is quite slow so the image only changes imperceptibly. 
Lloyd made some films in which two djs are in a taxi going from one job to another, and a text and photographic work which features a dj called princess Julia. Later works from the early 2000s show the spray from a fountain against backdrop of trees, ebbing and flowing with the wind and the jet of the water, and a slide projection showing waiters at a café in St. marks square in Venice. Lloyd became interested in groups of people observed rather than single people invited to cooperate.
Lloyds work deals with the still moments in everyday life that can be considered as formal visual and discrete. Andy Warhol is a reference point for her work.
!Locke Hew.
British artist who made Cardboard Palace, a maze that fills a gallery from floor to ceiling made from corrugated cardboard sheets cut into shapes a bit like a Frank Stella wall relief, with light bulbs creating shadows. The cardboard is left brown with only the black felt pen outlines as adornment. The maze leads to a kind of sitting room with a tea table. There is lots of documentation about the British Royal Family pinned to the structures of the maze.
!Lockhart Sharon.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes films and photographic work. Lockhart's work has an anthropological vision, she is interested in how the cultural life of different society groups is portrayed by those who study it, and in how this can be made visual, or even in how the artist can make a similar portrayal with very different results. Lockhart made series of photographs of the inhabitants of dwellings that had been visited and described by anthropologists. The dwellings are shown uninhabited, and the inhabitants shown separately. Lockhart spent some time in Manaus in Brazil making a film set in the town theatre. Teatro Amazonia is a hour long single shot film showing the auditorium of the theatre seen from the stage. The audience in the theatre watching Lockhart's film are put in the position of performers on a stage watching the audience in Manaus. The performance of a piece of contemporary classical music begins, sung by an invisible choir on the stage in the position of the viewers.  The music begins with the tuning up of the orchestra and then takes the form of a single note of orchestral noise building into a continuously mutating wall of sound, then fading. The audience  on the screen watch, listen fidget, get up and go out occasionally. The minute detail of their behaviour becomes interesting. The viewer is able to watch something that would be unwatchable without the suspense created by the voices of the choir. 
The films titles list the audience participants, every single one of them, in groups dictated by the barrio that they live in. So that the image of the audience can be seen representing the people of the city of Manaus, and in fact they do this proportionally. The numbers of people from each barrio representing the relative population of each barrio. This list of names also reveals the family connections of the people of the city.
Lockhart subsequently made a film in a Japanese gymnasium where a group of women are doing physical drill. This is very regimented but balletic in the way that the space seems to be used choreographically. Lockhart films in similar choreographed shots to create a mesmeric document of a strict regime, in which the scrutiny of uniformity yields fascination for minute details of difference

!Long Richard.
British artist born in the 1950s, Long is a land artist and as such his site specific work exists mostly as documentation. Long began by making walks and documenting them with photographs. He would then mount these photographs relate them to sections of large scale survey maps showing the walk and the north south orientation, and print below them the distance walked as proportion of the total distance of the walk. In doing so he was developing ideas put into practice by American land artist Dennis Oppenheim in the 1960s and 70s, and anther artist who walked along coastlines, the walk, he declared, making a drawing of a line, the line being the demarcation between land and water. 
This idea of 'taking a line for a walk' has been explored by several artists. Longs walks were made all over Britain and then in other countries, they were mostly quite short. The photographs never showed a person. In Longs work the land itself, the ground on which we tread and the landscape it forms, is the subject. The artists subjectivity in terms of choice of walk is part of the work too. The viewer can imagine what it would be like to make the walk in terms of the feel of the ground underfoot and the inclines and descents.
Long developed his interest in the ground underfoot into an interest in the materials this comprised of. He brought clay, earth and stone into the gallery in various forms. Never making an actual mark on a landscape, Long began to being elements from the land into the gallery to make formal works. A number of temporary wall paintings made of mud and clay, thrown at the wall or splashed on with handprints, the mess and drips on the surrounding floor and wall allowed to remain. Long might make a circle of white clay splashes on a dark painted wall, or a horizontal band of dark mud on a white wall.
He also made rock floor sculptures, which make reference to the work of Carl Andre and Robert Smithson. A piece of the rock from a particular landscape would be cut into slices and then fragmented into enormous jagged shards, these laid out to fill a solid circle or band on the floor, in a sculptural echo of rock strata and of man made intervention such a roads and paths.
!Longo Robert.
American artist who made both paintings drawings and sculptures in the 1970s and 80s. The sculptures are usually complementary to or even shown as a part of a painted work. For example Longo made a number of works in tow and half metre high panels arranged in threes and fours, the panels showing a man clothed in a suit apparently falling to the ground (perhaps having been shot) although he could almost be dancing, some blank white panels and some cityscapes. The representational panels would often be drawn in pencil and graphite in a very realistic way. The panels are very well made. In some cases there is a bronze cast of a similar male figure placed in front of the wall panels, the best known being of man 'falling', the balance of the sculpture precarious. 
!Lorenzetti.
Italian fifteenth century painter, who painted a large wall painting which can be seen in Siena titled Good and Bad Government. This painting is the width of a room, situated high up near the ceiling. It shows a city on a hill with people going about their lives in productive and unproductive ways, with a man on a horse riding into the image from left.
!Lotto Lorenzo.
Italian renaissance painter and sculptor
!Louis Morris. 
American artist working in the 1960s and 70s who painted very large abstract works, distinctive for the technique Louis developed of pouring paint onto unprimed canvas. The thinned paint pooled and spread creating a soft irretgulatr edged shape, usually a stripe, with an element of chance playing a part in the position and direction fo the colour. Louis would use several colours, usually bright, to create the stripes, but leave the rest of the canvas bare.
!Louis Morris.
American artist who is known for his abstract pour paintings made in the 1960s and 70s. Louis developed a technique for pouring paint onto raw canvas and allowing the paint to pool and spread, soaking into the canvas yet the colours remaining distinct from one another. The resulting works are usually brightly coloured stripes emanating from one side of the canvas, spreading and blending on the edges, the texture of the surface of the canvas always visible.
!Lowe Bridget.
British artist born in the 1960s who works with text. Lowe made a work in which she took the titles of books and typeset them as a continuous text. She also made a work fro the web in which a text is presented to the viewer at a controlled pace.
!Lowe Colin and Thompson Roddy.
British artists who work together, both born in the 1960s. Lowe and Thompson have for a number years been conducting tongue in check correspondence with businesses, public agencies and institutions about art and artists. They make outlandish requests, complaints or suggestions but these are made in absolutely conventional ways, most often in the form of letters which themselves constitute the artist's work. They have also made site specific work including street banners.
!Lowry A S.
British artist painting in the 1940s onwards, now dead. Lowry lived and worked in the North of England and painted scenes of everyday and working class life in the cities of the North. He became known as a kind of peoples painter, a painter of 'matchstick men and matchstick cats and dogs' immortalised in a popular music song. Lowry's showed the grey light of northern winters and the relentless coming and going of daily life, showing buildings and a multitude of tiny people walking past. The people are painted in two or three brushstrokes, suggestions of figures so that Lowry could paint a great number of them on one painting. The paintings are often predominantly grey or white the figures in dark colours with little hints of bright colours. 
!Lucas, Sarah.
Lucas is a British artist born in the 1960s, one of the so-called Frieze generation of artists who were working in London in the early 1990s. She was a close friend of Tracy Emin's at this time and the two artist's opened a shop together. 
Lucas's work shows a preoccupation with sex death and female identity within a framework of humorous abjection. The work with which she became known was a mattress from a double bed placed against a wall, hall of it propped up, half on the floor. Inserted in openings cut in the mattress were two melons at the top on one side and a bucket on the part resting on the floor below them, whilst on the other side a cucumber was inserted at an angle of 45 degrees from the mattress, at the same level as the bucket. This was a crude but very expressive representation of the male and female forms reclining in bed and like a lot of Lucas's work a crude humour is used in a sophisticated way to represent universal sexual desires by formal but unconventional, and by implication not traditionally male sculptural means. 
Another work in similar vein A Kebab and Two Fried Eggs consist of an old wooden table with two fried eggs placed on it at one end and a pitta bread containing doner kebab at the other end. Plainly suggestive of female sexual anatomy, this work also represents the female form lying on a table, suggestive of sexual intercourse in simultaneously humorous and slightly threatening ways. 
Other early works have become iconic, in particular a photographic self portrait, one of a continuing series, of the artist sitting in a chair with feet up on a table at the front of the image nearest the viewer, clad in work boots. This uncompromising image presents Lucas as a female artist in male pose and clothes, a subject to which she returns often in these photographs. She holds a beer can between her knees and pulls the ring tab, for example, and the beer which has been shaken, spurts upwards. She sits on a toilet, her trousers round her ankles. Her work boots which  she is wearing in all the photos, are shown on their own. Lucas often has a cigarette hanging from her lips. These and other images portray the artist as a working man, bringing the viewers own preconceptions to their attention.
Small sculptures, such as a cast of the artists genitalia in a homage to Duchamp (a major influence), Lucas's own work boots with razor blades inserted into the sole at the toes also explore the theme of masculine and feminine identity as well as traditional artist's themes such as the nude body and the self portrait.
A collage of newspaper images from the Sun newspaper featuring vastly overweight women shows a distorted image of femininity.
In later works Lucas began to model forms more literally as in her series of sculptures which are covered entirely in cigarettes stuck on in a mosaic pattern, such a s motorbike and an enlarged garden gnome. Quite apart from the characteristic inappropriateness of the materials the apparent cost of materials is striking. The display of the materials of addiction and over consumption is in tune with the associations with drink in particular beer in the earlier work.
A car is used in a large installation. The car has a mechanism in it that makes it bounce rhythmically, to give the impression that a couple are having sex inside and bouncing on the seat.
The Bunny Girl is another later work, this is a representation of a playboy bunny girl made from stuffed pantyhose draped on a chair to suggest arms and legs, and a pair of ears and a fluffy tail. This utterly abject object conveys both the idea of a bunny girl astride a punters lap, or straddling a chair and the idea of a woman as flesh encased in nylon tights or even of a sex toy, a toy woman. 
!Luke, Jeff.
British artist born around 1965 died 1996. Luke's sculptures are made essentially from rubbish and throwaway materials, yet they have a strongly formal quality. His sculptures are often on the floor, usually installed and involving a large number of parts, reminiscent of American scatter art of the late 1970s. A number of rubber band balls some about 60 cm in diameter, are arranged on a low plinth, as if they were a Henry Moore or Barbara Hepworth carved stone sculpture. Instead the abstract forms, pebble like shapes, are the result of hours of obsessive activity. Luke took a mindless doodle like activity and expanded the scale to make a sculpture. Cosmetics and toiletries such as hair shampoo and conditioner, cleaning fluids, and other household liquids are places in tiny 2 cm high sample tubes, which are numbered to refer to a key telling the viewer what they contain. These are then placed on a shelf in a row, creating a formal work from the great range of colours of the liquids. 
Other works consist of unidentifiable bits of small rubbish (this quality of anonymity, a sense that the object is a part of the unknown larger whole seeming to be the criteria for selection), placed in a careful arrangement on the floor.

!Lum, Ken.
Canadian artist
!Luna, James.
Born around 1950, Luna is a Native American living on a reservation who is known for his socio-political art projects, which highlight the discrimination against Native American Indians. The best known of his works is a performed installation for which Luna had himself tranquillised and placed in a display case in a museum of native American artefacts during museum opening hours. He remained in the case every day for a month, under close supervision.
!Lyotard, Francois.
The Guerilla Girls
Group of anonymous feminist activists who were working in New York in the late 80s and early 90s, the Guerilla Girls were named for their wearing of gorilla masks when they went organised demonstrations. They also used the banana as an emblem, along with the gorilla suit a prop of slapstick humour and a visual pun on both the diet of gorillas and the offering of an opportunity to institutions who fail to represent women adequately to 'slip up' (on the proffered banana skins). The group is thought to have included a number of members who went on to become prominent artists, for example Sue Williams. The Guerilla Girls were very much a product of an era, when feminism was well established and energy could be turned from discriminatory practises in everyday life, work and the home, to those in culture. The group produced a number of seminal posters and postcards for example a list of reasons 'Why There are no Great Women Artists', and a list of statistics about the proportional representation of women artists in art museums. These texts achieved widespread distribution because they were clever in their use of graphics and advertising to get message across, and because the appearance of the gorilla suited members staging demonstrations at institutions and relevant events was always newsworthy.
!John Martin.
English artist working in the late nineteenth century who painted visionary scenes depicting humankind struggling with the natural world, a kind of inversion of the idea of the sublime. An example is a painting of a towering dark black and brown mountain at night in which a huge chasm opened up, a volcanic eruption spewing out flames sparks and lava into the darkness. Dwarfed by this landscape lots of people are struggling and fleeing from the lava flow and fires.
!Macarthy Paul.
American artist who is well known for his performance work, which is always shown as a video documentation, usually displayed as part of an installation containing the props, set and costumes used in the production of the video. The actual one off performance may be done in front of an audience, but they watch it on a monitor in another room, Macarthy always performing in private. 
Macarthy appropriates folk tales and popular icons, subverting them by renacting the tales transplanted into his own bizarre and usually scatological world. He draws a parallel between everyday activities and the making of art through his parody of  fiction.
Macarthy himself usually features as main protagonist/artist. He always uses theatrical costumes and sets, often using masks and props. For example a work titled Heidi and the Grandfather is loosely based on the story of Heidi, but in McCarthy's hands becomes a tale of child abuse and perverse practices involving faecal obsession. In part of the performance Heidi and the grandfather make sausages from fake faeces extracted from Heidi's prosthetic bottom. Bossy Burger is a horror story involving a chef character who wears a mask and makes burgers. The film makes reference to Sweeny Todd and a lot of tomato ketchup is used (a development from an earlier video in which Macarthy smears himself liberally from head to foot with tomato ketchup). 
A film of a Pinocchio character is shown on a monitor in a large box construction with peepholes which are apparently holes for Pinocchio's nose, and there are Pinocchio costumes complete with noses for the viewer to put on. As ever, in this work Macarthy makes obvious and puerile associations with his chosen subject and lets the various elements collide to disconcerting but humourous effect. He takes the Freudian claim that art is at base no more than playing with ones own excreta absolutely literally.  
Mcarthy notorious for his wittiest creation, De Kooning, also his cruellest and funniest parody of art and the art world, featuring mcarthy visiting a blue chip dealer and also dressed as a tube of paint throwing him self mindlessly against the studio walls, chanting a tuneless ditty 'De Kooonign, De Kooning'. He mocks the art worlds pretensions and contrasts them with the still feted work of the supposedly senile de Koonign. This is perhaps the most extreme example of the way Macarthy focuses on breaking taboos. His comic and clownish portrayal of senility, ketchup and fake faeces smeared over his body and surroundings to mimic blood and shit, scenes of child abuse and indecent or murderous practises: all these are grist to Macarthy's comic tragic mill, his interpretation of the darkest side of human nature as a form of slapstick.  
!Mach David.
Brtistih artist born in the 1950s, Mach became known when he made large sculptures from meticulousy stacked magazines. These monumental scale sculptures developed and Mach began to use other materials, fro example a submarine made from scrapped car tyres, a train made from stacked bricks. Constructed with no fixings, the sculptures have a casual appearance that belies the complexity of their construction, needed to prevent them from collapsing. The sculptures gain their intrinsic strength from the way the stacks are formed and interlinked, but they remain potentially un-fixed and are impermanent despite their bulk and weight. 
!Mackenzie Lucy.
Scottish artist born in the late 1970s, Mackenzie's practise is extensive, ranging from painting, drawing, printmaking and writing, to video, installation, dj-ing, and event organising. She has also used her work to promote the activites of charities, such as one that supports women in Eastern Europe who are trafficked as prostitutes. Mackensies ppolymorphous practise is the result of an ideological and socially engaged approach to art as a medium with the power to transform daily life. At the same time her work references early twentieth century art, particulary that of Russia and Germany both iconographically and in the creation fo social events. Her paintings and works on paper often borrow apparently anachronistic historical graphics style, and have featuring athletes, Olympic rings, and graphic and geometric patterns. Or they may be graffiti like, borrowing from classical art and include text. They are painted in faded out colours, drawn roughly or printed on, often on cheap paper wicih in itself is a symbolic act, as cheap paper and printing methods seem entirely  anachronistic in western Europe today, where they are still the norm in many parts of the world.
In some sense Mackenzie's work reflects the state of the body politic, in other senses it is a reflection on the practise of art. She started a club night in her native Glasgow that was and remains a focus for artists and musicians. She also ran a club in Warsaw with Pauline Olowska, a long term collaborator, at which they presented events including music, poetry and readings. 
A video made in 2003 shows Mackenzie and Olowska working togheter in an empty space. Mackenzie is drawing Olowska who seems to be doing nothing but talking on the phone.
Mackenzie also uses text in her large paintings and painted wall sized murals. 
A recent printed work features a year planner poster for 2004, blank, with all the weekends blocked out in yellow higlgighter pen. 
!Mackie Christina.
Scottish artist born in 1960 who makes sculptures and video which are conceptual cool and restrained and have a poetic minimalism. Mackie might place a monitor showing a piece of road with traffic sounds next to a railway sleeper, or use a telephone which the viewer can pick up and listen to a sound. Monitors are often placed on or situated in constructions made of old timbers.
!Maclean Bruce.
Scottish artist Maclean was born in the 1940s and made his best known work in London in the 1970s and 80s. He made paintings, coulurful gestural abstracts and performances, in which he adopted a persona smoking a pipe and wearing a trilby hat.
!Maclennan Alistair.
A Scottish performance artist working in the late 1970s ande 80s, Maclennan made a controversial work at the Institute of Contemporary art in London in 1981 call Still Tills. Maclennan spent a week living in the gallery eating and sleeping surrounded by food, detritus and waste including bodily waste. The artists body and functions as sculpture. He also sorted through and arranged bags of refuse, which as the press reperoted included sanitary protection and nappies. The title may have been a comment on the end of consumerism and shopping, the beginning of a new self-sufficiency and the artist was trying to show the possibilitiy of this by living without purchasing anything for a week. Other works of Maclennans were similarly time- and often endurance-based. 
!Maddox Brown Ford.
One of the British pre-Raphaelite painters, Maddox Brown made less stylised paintings than Rosetti, for example a painting of a goat in a barren landscape light by a lurid setting sun, using orange and purple and green.
!Magill Elisabeth.
British/Scottish painter born in 1960s who made a series of paintings of luggage seen through an airport security x-ray machine. Later work includes paintings of trees.
!Magritte Rene.
Magritte is perhaps the most famous of the Surrealists, and indeed is one of the best known artists in the world. A French painter born in 1850, Magritte's images are instantly recognisable and attributable, and have been much copied. They have influenced adviseritisng imagery for several decades and continue to do so. 
Magritte was interested in puns both visual and textual, for ezxample, 'Ceci n'est pas une pipe' - 'this is not a pipe' is inscribed on a painting of a pipe. Many of his paintings feature both words and objects often the objects are apparently named but the names do not make sense. In this way Magrittes text paintings are both object and subject in the sense that are what they are but the viewer is then told that they are not, leading them to a new consideration of what they are seeing and of what art might do.
Examples of paintings without text are a train emeergin from a fireplace, an apple obscuring most of the front of a bowler hatted man's face, an inverted mermaid: with a fish's head and woman's legs and pelvis, an easel by a window with a painting on it, the painting showing the view through the window, two grey spaceship like objects titled 'Floating Spheres', and a man's face which is also a female torso in which the pubic hair is also the man's beard. These are painted in a highly realistic but flat and stiff style, with a muted palette, smooth, flat and few brushstrokes. The ludicrous juxtapositions of objects and people are nonetheless poetic and leave the viewer hopgin that they might actually happen, that the everyday might mix up and become magical, in other words surreal.
!Maillot Auguste.
French late nineteenth century sculptor working in bronze and stone, who made solid and rounded mostly female figures with distinctively simply formed bodies, even the smaller works have a kind of naïve but monumental quality. 
!Malevitch Kasimir.
Russian suprematist painter who painted the famous 'Black Square' in 1910. This work is often attributed with being the beginning of abstraction. The painting is simply a square canvas painted black all over. Square canvases were very unusual since they did not lend themselves to the traditional subjects of paitnign, landscapes and portraits, by which names the two most common proportions of paintings were known. The square drew attention to the work itself, not the subject of the work, so that the painting as an object, about painting, rather than as a depiction of something became important. Mondrian was soon using the square format. 
Malevitchs paitnign was entirely black which drew attention to the only possible detaisl of his work, the colour fo the black and the brushstrokes, in fact the viewer was directedt to regard the nature of paint and painting in this work. This may have been more than malevich intended, for the work emerged as a result of his systematic attention to consturctivits principles of which it was a logical if daring conclusion. Other works emplyed the geometry, sharp edged straight lines and primary colours of constructivism, a movement whose members used these principles to construc abttract works. Consturcitvism had a great influence on and was influenced by contemporary graphic design and typography, and is still evident in design today. 
!Maloney Martin.
British artist and former curator who pioneered 'bad painting' in the late 90s, making very large scale monumental figurative works often based on other paintings for example poussin, for which he used friends and acquaintances as models in a highly ambitious kitchen sink meets Alez katz style. He also painted still lives and animals, as well as a painting cycle of gay club scenes. Maloney's work is distinguished by the speed at which he works painting wet on wet with large brushes at speed, rarely spending more that 6 hours on a work. The resulting faux clumsiness was highly controversial but Maloney is representative of a generation of artists who began to make work in hitherto scorned media such as textiles, the so called 'dumb' art which combined a knowing intellectualism with great enthusiasm and joy in materials and a freedom from the tyranny of conventional artistic skills.
Maloney curated several seminal exhibitions in his own home, including Lost in Space and Die Yuppie Scum and his 'you can do it if you really want to' attitude to success in the art world, combined with a willingness to combine the classical with the trashy made him very successful in the 1990s. He works chiefly in oils, but also in watercolour, sticky tape and ceramics.
!Man Ray.
A French surrealist sculptor painter and photographer working in paris in the early twentieth century. Man Ray used various photographic methods including placing objects on light sensitve plates and then explosing them to get an image. Man Ray took the well known photograph of Duchamp with a star shaved out of the back of his head smoking a pipe. 
!Manet Edouard.
A French artist who is often described as the father of impressionism, Manet painted in a realistic way but used paint expressively. More than any artist at that point in time he openly used the recently invented photograph in making his work. This had a profound effect, in particular on composition. Taking an interest in the way a photograph cropped a scene and noting the difference for a painter who can choose what to include and where, moving things in and out of the frame, Manet began to compose his canvases in the same way, giving them an immediately new look. Prominent among artist of the mid 19th century in paris Manet was friends with Courbet, Degas, Monet. 
Manet painted two very well known works, Olympia, and Dejeuner Sur L'Herbe, as well as Bonjour Monsieur Courbet and the Bar at the Folies Bergere. This last is often cited by artists as an important inspiration in their own work, it is a very original painting which depicts a bar at the Folies Bergere night club, visited and painted by many of his contemporaries, behind which is a woman serving. On closer inspection it is actually the reflection in the mirror behind the bar, Manet has taken this mirror as the edge of his painting and thus we see the woman's back, some of people at the bar and the bottles which are in front of the mirror. 
Manet's paintings were startlingly new and inventive. His Olympia (based on a historical paintgin of the same subject) was shocking and provoked storms of protests. It was rejected from the paris salon, the official body of recognition for artists, as being pornographic and the public was outraged. Olympia is depicted nude, reclining on a couch, with her maid and a cat behind her. She wears a ribbon choker and earrings and seems to be a prostitute. However what was provoked outrage was look on the woman's face - she stares out of the canvas at the viewer, actively acknowledging their presence and thus making them a subject of the work. This was revolutionary, and brought the whole history of the depiction of the female nude in art into question - here was a woman who meet the viewer's gaze rather than allowing the viewer unchallenged access to her body in passive acquiescence.
Dejeuner Sur L'Herbe, also a reworking of a historical painting, provoked a similar response but soon these two paintings were acknowledged as groundbreaking works. Dejeuner Sur L'Herbe shows a group of young men and women picnicking in the woods, one of the women is naked, reclining casually and staring out at the viewer from the centre of the canvas. The gaze of some of the others is also focused out of the picture plane at the viewer.
Bonjour Monseiur Courbet is painting in which Manet acknowledges his debto to Courbet. He shows himelf walking alond a country lane, greeting the passing Courbet. 
!Mantegna Andrea.
An Italain renaissance painter. Mantegna invented painting technique grisaille.
!Manzanelli Margarita.
An Italian painter born in 1970s who paints young women, often emaciated but fashionably dressed, in the middle of a large canvas with a plain flat background. the women are characterised by their monochrome flat backgrounds, thin bodies and large often bulging eyes.
!Mapplethorp Robert.
A photographer working in New York in the 1980s and early 90s, Mapplethorp became notorious for his photographs of gay men. The men are often naked or semi-clothed, and the images sexually unambiguous such as Man in a Polyester Suit which shows an African-american man in the eponymous suit resting his penis on a stool, and another photograph showing a man urinating into another's mouth, both men dressed in leather. 
Photographs like these led to Mapplethorp being attacked by the Christian right and his exhibitions were denounced as pornography. The fact that museums spent public money on exhibiting Mapplethorp's work led to a full scale row in the United States senate and other artists were dragged into the debate. Senator Jesse Helms led the attack supported by The Association for the American Family and religious organisations. The result was a substantial decrease in the amount of support given to artists by the National Endowment for the Arts, the United States government arts subsidy fund. The exisiting obscenity laws were reassessed there was a call for a revision of the censorship laws in order to make it easier to close an exhibition or require special conditions to be met. 
There were many counter claims, not just by Mapplethorp, of discrimination on the grounds of sexuality. Mapplethorp said of his work that he photographed images of love and trust, and that even the most 'sado masochistic' of his images represented supreme trust, love and vulnerability on the part of the participants. His defenders claimed that if his images were of heterosexual couples there would have been no such outcry.
The case went to court and a ruling was made that the photographs defined as obscene could only be exhibited in a specially constructed screened off area, with a clear indication to the public of the contents of the these works. The case increased the public interest in the work and made Mapplethorp one of the best known photographers in the world.
Mapplethorp's themes were (like many of the gay artists in a period in which so many were lost to AIDS related illnesses), love, sex and death. He made many self portraits the best known of which shows him wearing a leather biker jacket with chains and a leather cap. Antoher shows Mapplethorp as a young man standing on a fire escape with the singer Patti Smith. A photograph taken just before his death, shows a visibly frail Mapplethorp holding a cane topped with a skull and crossbones emblem which owes an allegiance to Warhol's late self portraits. 
Mapplethorp made a series of photographs of flowers, mostly lilies and other exotic flowers, which are also sexual images. These are sometimes reminiscent of Georgia O'Keefe paintings, the photographs are usually black and white but some are colour.
Mapplethorp also photographed the body builder Lisa Lyon, whose physique he considered to be perfect. He made commissioned portraits, for which he was much sought after by the rich and famous.
Robert Mapplethorp died of AIDS related illness in 1994.
!Marclay Christian.
US artist born 1957, Marclay worked as a muscian and DJ before becoming an artist as well. Starting from his interest in music, Marclay makes video, sound and works on paper. A 2003 installation consisted of 6screen projectikon arranged in a horizontal row, composed of film clips. Thses were chosen from a huge range of films, documentaries, news footage, and chosen for a particular sound (pitch and qualtity of sound) as well as for the image. Thus a singing marilyn, marching feet, shouting, piano player, almost any thing is sampled and used. Marclay built the resulting samples into a complex musical score, 15 minutes long which is 'played' by the film clips. The viewer is engaged in a compelling set of responses to whqt they hear and see, having to match sound to image and also to process reponses to the image, many of which are consciously or subconsciously recognised. The urge to find a narrative is continually denied, and the viewer is forced to recognise the sound as the narrative instead.
!Marden Brice.
Amercian painter of minimal abstract works, who made large (at least 2 by 3 often) cancvase painted entirely usually muted colurs very often grey with one or two contrasting coloured stripes often on the edge of the canvas. Marden's work has similar formal qultities to the work of Barnet Newman, although where Newmans work is painterly, Mardern's is hard edged and painted with flat solid colour. Marden used a lot of grey, black and netral colours.
!Maria Walter de la.
