Known for his permanent installation Lightning Field 1987, in Arizona in US, a specific area which has the highest frequency of lightning strikes in the world in which he set out a square mile of lightning conductor poles linked by conductive wires. When struck the field becomes a spectacle of blue light and electrical activity. 
!Marinetti Frederico.
The leading member of the Italian Futurist movement, Marinetti was an eccentric didact who Produced many painting, the best known one depicting a kind of elephant hoover construction. His many published texts include several manifesti. He wrote a Futurist Cookbook which contains feasible recipes including several for pasta, but also some very inedible and often ironic ones inlvoving rose petals for example, with complicated presentation methods. The cookbook is a glimpse into the suprising romanticism of the futuristic movement. Marinetti declared pasta to be the most important (and futurist) of all food. 
Marinetti made several performance works including one in which a set of dining chairs were made to dance on and off the stage. 
!Marker Chris.
Very influential British underground filmmaker working from the 1970s onwards, whose work is often a collage of images and film clips. Marker's films are presented as an installation. For example one work is shown on a tower of monitors placed one on top of the other, the same film playing on each but at a different point, so that the images are never the same.
!Marshall Maria.
A British artist born around 1968. Marshall has made a number of video films featuring her own children, including one of her son smoking. 
!Marshall Mike.
A Britisih artist born in 1970s, Marshall has made a number of films  of banal everyday scenes, including a film of a water sprinkler in a garden. The viewer watches the green leaves of the hedge and the waxing and waning of the spray droplets as they sparkle in the sun. Marshall brings a kind of everyday sublime to the viewer's attention, producing moments of abstract beauty and contemplation from unexpected sources. 
!Martin Agnes.
A painter born about 1930 who made the work for which she is best known in the 1950s and 60s. Martin's minimal abstract paintings are usually based on a grid, mainly white with pencil thin lines of bright colours, which are barely perceptible from a distance. The paintings are cool, calm and full of light, and rigourous in their exectuion.
!Martin Simon.
Brisths artist born in 1968 whose highly finished conceptual painting and sculpture is concerned with the nature of art. He has made a photorealist painting of a bright red tree frog, a painted fibreglass repkcia of a carved and painted wooden bird in relief, that is based on an original wood carving, and a laquered fibreglass sculpture of a toy monkey's head, enlarged to about 4 feet in diameter and sprayed in off white gloss paint. His work is laborious and labour intensive, the results of his labour producing luxurious objects which have no clear purpose, making the viewer think about them as art objects. He has also made other conceptual work which is opposite in its choicie of more ephemeral materials such as cardboard boxes. 
!Martini Simone.
Italiain renaissance painter.
!Matisse Henri.
A French painter and sculptor, Matisse is considered one of the greatest artist in the world. His paintings include the famous Dance, an 8 metre by 3 metre panoramic painting of dancing female nudes, renowned for its semi abstraction. The pink figures become rhythmic arabesques on the swirling blue ground. Matisse later made The figures into paper cut-outs, a form in which he was prolific in later life, using ultramarine paper collaged onto a white ground. 
Along with the Snail, a large 4 by 3 metre collage made of a spiral of squares of different brightly coloured pieces of paper within a yellow collaged frame, these cut-out became perhaps Matisse's best known work, their graphic simplicity lending itself well to printed reproduction. The blue dancers, their heavy limbs creating a calligraphic pattern, are one fo the most reconisable works by a twentieth century artist.
Matisse also did a large number of drawings and had an assured hand, creating very simple drawings often using one continous line. However it is for his figurative painting that he is most greatly respected, his use of drawing within a painting, the way he allowed a drawn edge to define the pictorial space and function both as part of an object or structure and part of the composition, and similarly the way in which he left areas of the canvas unpainted, allowing the white of the ground to function as part of the image. 
Matisse painted restful, joyous and domestic subjects in an entirely unsentimental and in fact highly intellectual way. The composition was daring, as in the Piano Lesson, a predominantly green and red painting with large blocks of colour, entirely figurative but which nevertheless has an abstract sensibility. It is probably the use of colour which singles him out, for example in the woman with a green face, a head and shoulders portrait in which the shadow of the woman's hat over her face is painted in a swoop of emerald green. In this way Matisse was translating what his eye say literally into paint, the substance of paint.
Anothre of his great paintings depicts a goldfish in a bowl on a table by a French window opening onto a tiny balcony. The window and room are boldly outlined in black, the goldfish bowl is painted minimally yet very convincingly. The Red Studio is a particularly daring work, showing the furniture and objects in his studio outlined in white (made simply by leaving the canvas white where he wanted the line to be) and some colour, with minimal perspsective, but chiefly this is a red square of canvas with objects floating on it. 
He also made a number of bronze casts, the best known of whcih is the triptych The Back, an abstracted human torso viewed from the back, carved in relief as semi cylindrical shapes to represent the two sides of the back and the buttocks, leaving a space in the middle to represent the division caused by the spine. The sculptures were commissioned for a specific interior and were thus made as three bronze wall reliefs. 
!Matta Clark Gordon.
American artist born in 1940s working from 1960s onwards. Matta Clark is best known for his work House, a suburban clapboard house which the artist cut in two vertically down the centre. The two halves lean outwards and away from each other. Matta Clark made a number of other works  whih involved cutting through buildings, for example a project at the opening of the Pompidou Centre in Paris he cut several circular and elliptical holes through a nearby building that was about to be redeveloped. Although major in terms of their execution, Matta Clarks interventions are visually extremely simple.  
Maugham Karl
Painter of intensely detailes flower beds, every flower given individual attention, on large canvases. Born 1960s, works in London
!May Nicholas.
A british painter born 1962, May made a serious of large paintings in which he poured large pools of paint, often house paint, while the canvas was on the floor, and tipped them to manouever the paint and allowing them to dry. He ofrten used different colours together, pouring one into the other, wet on wet, including metallic colours and powders. The resulting slick of paint would form the painting, its surfrace very enticing. May made a number of these during the 1990s, after which he ceased to make art. 
!Mazcuga Goshka.
Polish artist living and working in London born 1970, Mazcuga makes environments in which she curates exhibitions. She may create a cave within a gallery from crumpled brown paper, or a snowy mountain scape from fabric, and fill this space with the work of artists that she has invited to participate. Her work as an artist is the exhibition environment that she has created. 
!McComb Len.
British artist born around 1940. Len McComb mainly makes large scale pencil drawings, for example a sequence of cliff faces on the Anglesey coastline drawn on a number of pieces of paper tiled together to make a wall-sized drawing. 
!McCracken, John.
Briths artist who makes minimal abstract sculptures. Plank and cube shaped objects made to a high gloss laquereed finish in a soldi colours. The planks (about 3 metres long, 50 cm wide and 12 cm deep) typically lean against the gallery wall. Other sculptures are hung from the wall.
!McQueen Steve.
Britihs artist born 1960s who works in film and Video. Mcqueens films have a wide range of subjects but share basically sculptural concerns. Bear, made for his post graduate show in 1993, shows two men wrestling naked, shot from below in slight slow motion. Antoher shows a tightrobpe walker shot from below, so that we focus on the soles of the feet in special shoes, wobbing on the wire. In both these films by choosing an unusual viewpoint McQueen chages our perception fo these spectator acitiviites and shows the effort requrtied to carry them out. In 2001 a film shows McQueen standing before a wooden barn, which subseaqently falls apart, the roof collapsing and the walls falling outwards. As it falls, it seems that McQueen will be squashed by the front of the barn but in fact he is standing at the exact spot over wich the window falls, leaving him standin with the window opening around him, unscathed. In 2003 he made two more abmbitious works The Deep which he filmed in a mine in south Africa, and the Leap which showed young boys jumping off a clff. The Deep takes us down to the coal face in the lift and films at eh coal face, using available light and ambient sound. The result is often exteremely and hard to see, with no illumination save the miners head lamps and creates the impression of a kind of hell. The film sulminates in a sequence shot in the miners shower and clocking out hall where each mans temperature is taken. 
As well as seeking new ways to look at things and investignatng the visual perceptions associated with sound, McQueens wrok deals with race and the representaion of race in art cinema and photography atlhoguth he does not claim this for his work. The Deep does this explicityly, but Bear and the Falling Barn are more sublte. The barn is based on part of a Charlie Chaplin film in which the same thing happens. 
!1Meese Jonathan.
german artist born around 1970 meese made a number of works in which he covered the walls and ceiling of the exhibition space with a vast quantitiy of found material, photographs, drawings, images from magazines, in fact any visual material. Discernable themes might emerge. The effect was that of a teenage bedroom vastly expanded, with adult subjectg matter. Sometimes the material might be sent by post to the gallery by the artist, and the gallery left to mount it on the wall as they wished. 
!Mellor Dawn.
British artist born 1960s paints women usually dressed up, generic women or famous women. For a long time Mellors paintings were very small and displayed in groups, subsequently she began to make bigger works. 
!Merz Mario.
Italian painter born in 1940s made scultures and wall based works always incorporating some illuminated neon tubing. For example and igloo like structure with neon on floor. Paintings with neon arrows.
!Merz Tania.
British painter born 1960s
!Messerschmitt.
German artist 1890s who made a series of small exquisitely modelled  heads cast in bronze, about 10cms across, a male head with a number of different expressions, often the features are contorted. The heads are titled by the mood or emotion the face is displaying. Messerschmitt influendce by contemporary developments in psychologly, and other therapeutic and diagnostic methods fo the day.
!Michaux Henri.
French artist (dead) who makes small obsessive drawings and written works, dense scribblings, extremely intricate patterns and landscapes, letters to anonymous recipients written in the smallest of hands, sometimes blanked out after writing. Michaux mainly used pen and ink, often brown ink. His work was made under the influence of mescalin, which Michaux believed allowed the user make use of otherwise inaccessible parts of the imagination. Michaux's work occupies an odd space in the history of art since it could be compared to outsider art (for example the art of the insane) yet Michaux's altered state of consciousness was self induced.
!Michelangelo.
Italian artist working in Florence in the renaissance period, Michaelangelo is universally known as one of the greatest artists of all time. In fact his name is often used to invoke the idea of the highest achievement in art. Michelangelo is most famous for his white marble sculture David, an oversized nude with which he claimed to have represented perfoction in the male form. Ncihelagnleo is equally well known for the roof of the Sistine Chapel, Michelangelos was a very skilled draughtmena and created very lifelike figures, and he used colour in a very realistic way. The Sistine chapel ceiling was an enourmous undertaking, it is a fresco showing the life of Christ and the ascnesion into heaven. Images from the ceilign are known all over the world, often used by advertisers, and have become almost generic. One part of the ceilin in particular is arpticluarly popular, the image of Chirst reaching out his finger to god, who does the same, and their fingers nearly touch. This is an image of great tension and portent, and it seems as if an electrical charge passes from one to the other throught their fingers. In the late 1990s the ceiling was cleaned, provoking a great deal of controversy since for centuries the fresco had been darkenedn and the colours muted. The fresco had been known to visitors and to those looking at reprocutions as an antique, parts of which were even obsdured from view by centuries of dirt and the breath of millions of visitors. The cleaned ceilnig was very brightly coloured even garish. 
Mihcelangleos also made a marble sucultre of the Pieta.
!Millais John Everett.
British painter working in the late nineteenth century.
!Miller Alain.
Bristih artist born around 1960 who paints highly realistic images (for example a heart or a brain) which from a part of a larger abstract composition.
!Milroy Lisa.
Britsih artist born around 1960 how makes figurative painting. Milroy's work is essentially still life but there is an element of process painting in her work. She often paitns multiple versions fo the same type of object, set out in rows that cover the whole picture plane, for example shoes or blue and white porcelain plates, with the effect of standardising everything she chooses to paint both in terms fo the genre of thing, and in terms of the relation of one type to another. So that car tyres might be treated in the same way as jewellery. 
The objects in Milroys paintings are painted in consistent way, with simple flowing brushctokes on a flat colour ground, in realistic colours. They look very real viewed from a distance, close to the canvas the paint and brushstrokes are what the eye focuses on.  
!Mir Aleksandra.
Polish artist born in the 1960s who works in New York. Mirs work comprises socially engaged installation and events, combined with the consrtuctcion fo large site specific or temporary scultupres. 
Mir often chooses to respond to a location or a situation. For example she sited a military tank paitned pink on a disused lot in London, where local children play. The tank was photographed with women and girls dressed in pink draped over it in an ironic take on the female military mascot. 
Mir has made a work based on the Mir space station, and most recently has made an almost life size inflatable jumbo jet which can be sited anywhere. 
!Miro Juan.
Spanish painter working in the early twentieth century. Miro was one of the early abstract painters and his paintings are instantly recognisavle. Miro uses amorphouse shapes reminiscent of microscopic life forms. These are very often black, sometimes red and blue, and verge on the figurative. The ground of the paintgins is raw linen or very thinly prepared. Miro's found inspiration in cartoon narratives, in particular the American Krazy Kat, and in banal objects juxtaposed in strange ways. In this he was closer to the surrealists. Miro made many collages using images from catalogues such as knives, tools and machinery. These were often mixed with elements from cartoon strips and then abstracted to produce his paintings. 
!Miss Mary.
American artist bron in the late 1940s who made a number of earthwork sculptures, for example a cube shaped void sunk into the ground its top exactly level with the surface, entered on ramps from ground level. She subverted minimal sculptural forms being made by her contemporaries by making them absolyutley concrete but invisible. 
!Miyajima Tatsuo.
Japenese artist working from the late 1980s onwards who has made a large number of works using LED displays. A series of works use small LED units placed on the floor or on the wall, showing a very high number which is contstalnly changing. This number is in fact the date and time diplayed in fractions fo a second. 
!Modigliani Ernesto.
Italian artist working in late nineteenth and early twentieth century whose name is always associated with a certain type of portrait. Typcially a Modigliani protrati will show a seated woman with an elongated body, and a stylised face. Modiglanis work is quite, contemplative and painted in a sombre palette.
!Moholy Nagy Lazlo.
Hungarian artist who was a contemporary of the surrealists. Moholy Nagy made a number of pioneering semi-abstract films and photographs using architectural form, light and shade. 
!Mondrian Piet.
Dutch landscape painter painting his local countryside, trees and canals. Began to abstract these scenes and simplify his palette also experimenting with canvas shape, for example made a series of abstract works on oval canvases bases on trees in a snowy landscape conveyed as geometric grey and brown marks on a white ground. Working in the early 1900s he made a series of works on small square canvases painted in red, yellow, blue, white and black. These works became among the most famous abstract paintings in the world. He divided the canvas into irregular blocks and used black lines to bisect them and outline them. These works are largely untitled.
Later Mondrian began to paint patterns using smaller units and areas, for example 'Who's Afraid of Red, Yellow and Blue?' 1910 and one titled Subway which are a homage to the architecture and the new jazz scene of New York. Mondrian's abstract geometric compositions owe something to contemporary Russian constructivists but are significant for their abstraction of landscape into pure geometric form.
!Monet Claude.
French painter born in the 1840s, Monet is the best known of the Impressionists, and indeed made the painting which gave the movement its name: Impression:Sunrise, a view of the houses fo parliament and the fiver thames in London. This is a small paitning of the river at sunrise, the water reflecting the oranges and pinks of the sun which is painted as a small orange circle. This painting was remarkable for its vagueness in terms fo detail, and for the consideration fo the nature of the painted surface, so that the sun could ve read both as a sun and as a painted circle. The English painter whlistler, who was not a fan, claimed the aptingin to be a 'naïve daubing'. 
Along with Matisse and Picasso, on whom he was influential, Monet considered one of the greatest artists in the world nad the three together to be the founders of modernism and 20th century painting. Alongside the advent of photography and its apparent challenge to the painter to paint what the eye could see as axactly as possible, Monet championed a different approach. He was trying to paint an impression of what he saw, as if with eyes half shut, and it is possible that he got this idea through seeing out of focus iphotographic mages. After all the idea of a deliberately blurred image was a radically new one at the time and Monets dead changed the aims of painters everywhere. Monet took from Cezanne the idea that an artist might paint what was in front of him but paint less that he actullly saw, aiming to reproduce the essence of the view. To this end he developed a very distincdtiv eparinting style breaking the subject he paiting into patches of colour, applied without covering the ground of the canvas, using the uncovered white ground to create a shimmering effect. He used colour to paint light as well as solid forms.
Monet made several important groups of works. The Water liliy paintings made at his country home in Giverny, the façade of Rouen cathedral, and the haystacks.  
The water lilies series are largely 3 m by 4 m semi abstract canvases which depict a pond or lake with with water lilies on it using shades of emerald, ultramarine and violet, with bright pink and red highlights on the usually white lilies. The way the paint is applied to construct the image is almost of as much importance as the image itself.
A series of paintings of the façade of the cathedral at Rouen was a detailed exercise in and demonstration of impressionist technique. In these works the surface of the painting is encrusted with multicouloured paint which is visible as a façade only at a distance. There are an immense variety of paintings which represent and exaggeratd different light conditions. Monet was unique in his preference for continual repetition of views of the same subject. He also painted hay stacks series, notable for their pale blue and lilac shadows, a houses of parliament and Thames series,
Although best known for these series of large scale paintings, Monet made a large number of one off works preceding these. 
Impressionism did not impress the paris salon, leading to the formation of the Salon des Refusés, a place for all those artists rejected by the Salon to exhibit. It soon became recognised that these rejected artists were changing painting for ever and that they were the avant garde
!Moore Henry.
British artist born at the beginning of the twentieth century. Moore began as a figurative sculptor, but later became a pioneer of semi-abstract sculptural form in Britian, and made many public commissions. Like his peer Barbara Hepworth, Moore's sculptures often have holes through them and hollows; he too was interested in representing the 'void' sculpturally. He worked in stone, bronze, and clay. Unlike his contemporaries, however Moore did not make smooth surfaced works. His roughly modelled sculptures are like giant bones, and always refer clearly to the human form. For example a reclining nude (a favourite form for Moore) might be abstracted to from a series of voids and hollows, as if it were a piece of driftwood that had been worn away. A standing figure might have a large space where the belly would have been. Heads are featureless and the proportions of the body adjexted to suit the form that Moore was trying to achieve.
Moore's sculptures are often sited in rural landscapes and parks, and indeed it was the way in which the human form could look as if it were part of and came from the landscape, and the landscape seen through the sculpture, that interested Moore. A late example of this is an arch (around 1960) made for a lakeside site in Hyde Park in London which seems to be constructed of three monumnetla bones, two standing on their ends, the other balancing across the two.
Moore made a large number of drawings both as preparatory sketches and in their own right. His drawings are realistic and anatomically accurate. As well as drawging the figure, he made a notable series of impressions of people sheltering from bombs in an underground station during world war two.
!Morandi Giorgio.
Italian early twentieth century artist who mostly painted still lives, depicting simple domestic objects such as jugs, bottles and vases on plain coloured grounds.
!Morison Paul.
British artist born in the early 1960s who began his career in the late 1990s painting trees, flowers and vegetation in silhouette. Morison's paintings are nearly all made in balck on white, showing meticulously drawn plants and trees of varying scale made into a composition, which are paitnged by hand absolutely flat and hard edged in black acrylic paint on white. He has occaisionally made negative versions in white on black. Morisons paintings are usually around 2 metres high, and he has also made round ones. His wall paintings often span 20 metres.
!Morisot Berthe.
French artist working in the late nineteenth century. Morisot is best known for her portraits and drawings, as well as for being one fo the few women artists working consistently in this period.
!Morris Robert.
American Sculptor working from the 1960s to the 1980s who was known for his use of fabricated steel and other metals. Morris made abstract minimal sculptures which were nearly always cube box shapes, about 1.5 meters high, open at the top. His series of copper plated boxes shown as a group are particularly well known, as well as a similar series made of mirrored glass. The copper boxes emit a glowing pink reflected light from their interiors. These are contradictiory works in terms of their representation of a heavy solid for by means of emptiness and light.
Morris also made sculpture with heavy weight felt, including scatter works. He performed in a live work made with Carolee Schneemna in the 1970s title Olympia, in wihc Schneeman posed as the woman in Manets eponymous painting, and Morris dressed in a white robe and wearing leather work gloves, carried large plywood boards around, arranging them as a backdrop to Schneeman. Both performers wore white makeup. The piece was about the 'work' of art, about the male artist and the female model and about the translation from two to three dimensions, the boards representing 'planes'. 
!Morris Sarah.
American artist born in the 1960s who paints process based geometric pattern works based on the facades of modernist buildings. Morris photographs these mostly glass and metal framed buildings whose main feature is windows, and reduces the resulting grid, distorted due to the perspective of the original photograph, to its basic elements in a compouter programme. The grid is painted in one colour, often white, and the 'windows' in a number of colours, usually very bright, to create a grid pattern with a nod to Mondrain and to Bridget Riley, although it is more complex and colourful. Morris uses high gloss paint very precisely applied. The paintings (and similar screen print panel seires) are about 3 m high and 4 m wide at their largest. 
Morris also makes films showing the kind of architecture and city that inspires her work, for example a film shot in Miami.
!Mortimer Justin.
Britsh artist born in the 1960s who paints portraits and has painted the Queen. His paintings are realistic but one part of the image is often blurred or expressionistically painted.
!Mossman Anna.
Britsih artist born about 1960 who takes photographs in which she represents sensory experiences or thought processes. In a photograph of a rose the rose is out of focus as if the viewer had leaned close enough to smell the roses perfume. A series of photographs show people who are out of focus to the most extreme degree. For these photographs Mossman asked her subjects to stay in a room alone with the camera which was set to take a very long exposure. The sitters were to tell the camera a secret. The resulting image is blurred due the movement of the sitter during the telling of the secret, the blurring varying from person to person.
!Motherwell Robert.
Amercian abstract expressionist painter who made very large paintings often using a lot of black paint with gestural brushstrokes made with large brushes and other areas left bare.
!Mueck Ron. 
Bristihs sculptor born in the 1950s who after a career in film industry prosthetics began to make sculpture. Dead Dad brought him to public attention, a life size model of apparetly of his own dead father, made with such detail that it appeared to be real. Mueck's bear's comparison with that of Duane Hanson in the 1960s but the difference is that Mueck experiments with scale and his figures are often naked.
Mueck makes his sculptures from fibreglass which is painted and human hair applied strand by strand. He has also made a baby, a 6 meter high pregnant woman, a school girl (clothed), a post Partum woman lying on her back with her child placed on her stomach, the umbilicus emerging from her vagina still attached to the baby and a man in a boat.
Mueck's sculptures are so realistic as to cease to provoke amazment after a while, since there is no doubt of their perfection. Instead Mueck wants the viewer to confront the human state which each work represents - death, pregnancy, childbirth, childhood - by presenting sometihgn as close to life as possible. 
!Mueller Otto.
German artist working in the early twentieth century.
!Munch Edvard.
Nrowgian artist working in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries who painted one fo the most famous modern paintings fo all time, The Scream. This iconic painting is used extensively in advertising and the media today and has been stolen several times.
Munck had a very distinctive painting style, which ahs the appearnanc of a woodcut at times, outlining his figures and following the outline with the colours of the backgroung on some occasisions. He used strong and often acid colours, but on other occasions used soft colours and monochrome. 
In The Scream, Munch suggested both the soujnd of the scream and the emtoion that engendered it. The figure of a screaming person dressed in black, shocked eyes, hands to open mouth, is seen on a jetty, surrounded by raditating lines of bright colours that seem to represent the soucnd of the scream. The Scream was painted around the same time that Freud's theories of hysteria and the unconsciouss were becoming known. This had an influence on many artists, including Munch.
Subjects were either rural Norwegian lanscape or people, and his landscapes were nearly always poplulated. There is often a dreamlike qulatiy to Munch's work, as if nothing were quite real. The sun is often a stange colour, shadows are distorted, and elements are coloured unnaturally. The people in Munchs work always seem to be or to feel that they are alone. 
A painting of a lake has a setting sun, the refelction of which is a thckly applied vertical yellow stripe. In a lithograph called The Singer of a woman dressed in balck in a room with a piano the only colour is in the woman's bright red hair.
!Muniz Vik.
Brazilian artist who makes unconventional paintings. Muniz's paintings are usually made from newpaper or other photographs which he collects obbsessively, for example he made a series of works in which he used treacle to copy welll known images such as Jackson Polliock making a painting, which try to be the most basic form of illusion in terms of making a recognisable image.
!Munoz Juan.
Spanish artist born in the 1950s who died in 2001. Munoz, who was Spain's best known artist of the end of the twentieth centyr, made scuolture and installation. One of Munoz's last works was a very large scale installation at Tate Modern in London. Several 'elevators' were constructed which ascend and descen through a false floor. Viewers can see the elevators from the basement level and from above, and watch them ascending in space, in a way that they would never usually be seen. The movement of the elevators echoes that of the pistons of a huge slow engine. 
!Muntdean and Rosenblum.
Artist partners working in the late 90s and early 2000s in London who make collavborative paintings depciign usually young people in social siturations conversing. Their speech is written on the painting. Muntdean and Rosenblum's style is strongly graphic, with flat colours, and balck outlines and white backgrounds are characteristic.
!Murdoch Sadie.
Bristih artist born in mid 1960s who began making large paitings in the early 1990s based on modernist architecture, specificaolly interiors, which she drew out on a white grounhd, usually in black and grey, partially painted using very thin paint, and then partially destroyued by pouring thin paint over the whole. In the 2000s Murdoch began to make architectural models from card and paper which she again partially destroyed. Later models are more abstract and remain intact. Murdochs inetest in the modernist project in art and architecture has led to explore both abstract expressionism and architecture particularly the work of Le Corbusier. 
!Murikame Takahashi.
Takahashi is a Japanese painter who makes Highly decorative intricate and stylised floral paintings made in a flat style which make refernec to traditional Japanese patined or woven screens, but are also distintctively modern even futuristic. Murray Elizabeth

!Musgrave David.
British artist born in the 1960sbwho makes installations, Musgrafves work is essentially a sculptural representation of paint and the painted surface. He cuts out various thicjknesses of plastic and other sheet materials, transpartent and opaque, to look as if they are poured pools of colour and places them on the floor, overlaying them. The cutouts are complex shapes, as if liquid has been splashed or dropped, creating multicouloured floor pieces. Musgrave has also made a number of wall reliefs which are enlarged brushstrikes, modelled and cast and painted in high gloss. The mostly incidental shape and texture of the paint, the blob, the stroke is made into the a veryt deliberate ovbject. 
!Muybridge Eadweard.
An American photographer working at the turn fo the century who Pioneered the use of sequential exposrures to photograh a specific action, for example to show the components of the movement of a naked walking man and of a galloping horse. Muybridge's work had a distinctive influence on artists of the turn of the century. In the days before moving pictures, no one had foreseen that movement could be represented photgrpahically, reduced to a sequence of still images, or been ableto study movement. Muybridge's images became instally recognisable.

!Nash Paul.
Bristih artist who was painting during and after the first world war. Nash showed barren landscapes of the trenches and no-mans land in a slightly surrealist light, favou rtgin greens and browns and his charactierist geometric and skeletal shapes. An atmostphere of death pervades his paintings. 
!Nauman Bruce.
Amercian artist who began his career in the 1960s making performance, installation, sculpure and video work. In fact Nauman made some of the earliest video art, in the days when the technology was new and only TV broadcast equipment was available. This was bulky and heavy, and editing equipment was not readlkyl available. Nauman was also one fo the first artists to consider the TV monitor as an art object in itself, as seen in his seminal corridor piece. This consits of two 4 metre high freestanding white walls, placed nect ot each other with a gap in between. The walls are at a slight angle so that the gap between them narrows gradually until it is the width of a monitor which shows only white interference. The viewer approaches the work from the wider end, the light form the monitor creates a hypnotic effect so that the viewer feels compelled to enter the corridor and walk as far as they can before they no longer fit the space. In some ways the piece represents the familiar tunnel with a white light at the end that is often described by those who have had a near death experience. 
IN the 60s and 70s, Naumjan made several performed works, that are ususaly seen as video documents. The most well known is Taking a Line for a Walk, in which Nauman is seen walkind around a square marked on the floor. Another work, a develplmetn of this piece, is a 4 meter high white box round which the viewet can walk. A CCTV monitor is placed at each corner, on which the viewer sees themselves passing the previsou monitor. The film playing on the CCTV monitor is played back at slight delay, so that the viewer always sees themselves following them, but at the same time, since they are walking round a square, the implication is that they ought to be able to catch up with themselves. 
Nauman made other video works, for example of himself dressed as a clown laughting uncontrollably for 30 minutes. The way the video is presented is always important, in this case for example the monitor is in a wooden cubicle. In the 1980s he began to make more static sculptural work, and began a series in which he cast dogs in rubber and displayed them in various ways, for esxample hanging from the ceiling in a carousel like structure. 
In the 1990s Nauman made a large scale video installation for which he filmed himself headn and shoulders, talking to himself, and presented a number of these films, on 2metre hight flat screen monitors upside down.
!Nelson, Mike.
Bristih artist born in the 1960s who makes installations that are like mazes. Nelson constructs a series of rooms and corridors within a gfallery space, which the viewer enters trhogh a door in the gallery. Inside they are free to wander in the interior space htat nelson has created but there is only one way out. The first of these installations created a series of rooms that seemdto have been recently abandoned. Some were like offices in a factory, others empty rooms, all had a feeling of recent presence but alos of possible sinister events. There are clues in the form of items pinned to the wall, furniture, but ery few objects. The environment seems timless and the viewer has the disorientating experience of not being able to tell what period, country or type of building they are in. the spaces are extremely real. At a central point in the maze it seems that the viewer has reached the point at which they started but this is in fact a replica of that room and the determined participant carries on to discover several more rooms. There are hints of the psychological pressures that are sometimes brought to bear on prisoners.  
Another example is a maze Nelson created specifically for the Tate gallery in London, in which the viewer enters what seems to be a seires of back rooms at the tate, storage for works of art, wrapping and packing areas, etc. It is very difficult to believe that this is an installation that has been constructed in the galleyr and that the viewer has not entered behind the scenes by mistake, but for the peculiar absence of life and activity.

!Newman Barnet.
American painter who made abstract paintings from the late 40s to the 60s. Newmans minimal paintings are known for 'the zip', a line of a contrasting colour running vertically down the canvas. Newman invariably used one or two soldi colours in blocks or bands, often dark blue, black and maroon, but also goldrne ochre colours and aquarmarine, and the zip (red on a dark brown ground for example) animated these, setting up an optical resonance. He spent his whole career painting these and like Rothko was held up as a spiritual painter. One group of paitnigns on a single theme fills a whole room, they are white on white paintings with elements fo black. 
!Newton Helmut.
German phorograhpser who made balck and white or occaisionally coloured photographs of women, naked or clothed, but always objectified as glamourous, made up and fashionable. Newton alwys had his models wearing very high heels. Newtons images fell out of favour for a time in the 1970s when feminist critics claimed back the female form, but he continued to make work. Newtons women often seemed like objects for male pleasure but in fact they have a strange autonomy, despite their nakedness and are not like soft porn. Sometimes several women might be photographed together as in a well known image of several models standing in a group staring at the camera. 
!Nicholson Ben.
British artist who was part of the st Ives group, a group of artist who colonised the small Cornish town in the southwest of England. Nicholason was a contemporary of Barbara Hepworth and he was married to the artist Winifred Nicholson. Nicholsons pioneered a cross between contsrtuctivism and geometric abstraction, and echoes of the forms used by Hepworth can also be seen in his work. His paintings often had raised areas made by cutting out shapes in ply wood. He used white and grey a lot adding lines and smaller areas of brighter colours. He also made representational works, most of which featured the Cornish land and seascapes. 
!Nils Norman.
British artist who makes installations, works on paper, models and written texts. Norman's work presents ecololgical and sustainable models for human existence, in particluar urban exitistence. Norman draws on 1960s and 70s models for resistence, tailoring them to the new century and a new era of globalisioation and anti-capilalism. In particular he focuses on the the modernist myth of creating quatliyt of life for every one through good pracites in design and the built envrinoment. s
His investigations of how to put simple ideas for more socially responsible living into practise take various forms. He has made a number of architectural models that demonstrate for example acts fo urban resistance or reclamation such as the conversion of the flat roof of an apartment building in New York into a vegeitalvl plot to enable the residents to be self sufficient. He has also designed and built a sutstaninalbe bus called Geocruiser (2001). The bus runs on converted waste fat from the catering industrut and has solar panels enabling it to actually return energy to the national grid. It collects rainwater and part of it is a green house with a composter enabling the bus to be used to grow food effictently. The bus aloso houses a library of books and texts on ecologyoily and self-sufficincy and a computer terminal with internet access, a parinter and a photocopier available for public use wherevbver the bus stops. Visitors can photocpay any texts they wish to take away. Geocruiser visited several cities in the UK and in Europe. 
!Nitsch Herman.
Austrian performance artist who pioneered Viennese Actionism which is the name he gave to his live art practise. This consisted of solo works and also evnets or happenings that involved a large number of people. Nitschs performances usually take the form of some kind of slef divised ritual, echoing religious, pagan or tribal sacrificial events and usually involves a degree of self infitced pain or endurance, and often large amounts of animal blood and carcasses. The events are conducted with great solemnity and conviction. 
The large scale events have taken place in various locations, sometimes outdoors. In one a number of volunteers were crucified. Often the participants are covered in blood. Nitsch thinks fo the blood as paitns, as a record fo the event, and produces large numbers fo paitnigns and works on paper as well as photo and video.
Nitsch is perhaps best known for a performance in which he reputedly cut off (or at least mutiliated) his own penis. This is doucmnetaino by a series of photographs which show Nitsch bandaging various parts of his body.
!Noble Paul.
Bristh artist born in the 1960s who makes pencil drawings, video and amimation. Noble's earliest work consisted of bookjackets made for exisiting books which altered or reposititoned the book in some way, usually through humour or puns, echoing the British playwright Joe Orton's illicit alteration of libray books.
The main body of Noble's work to date depicts his creation fo a fictititous town called Nobson New Town. The arichitecture of Nobson is constructed on the blueprints of a typographical font created by Noble whose capital letters are three dimensional blocks, enabling Nobson to draw arrangements of buidlsinbg that spell out words. These blocks have the appearance of modernistic concrete stuructures and the letters are hard to read but decipherable.
The Nobson project is an indictment of the way of life in the west, a swiftian satire describing an urban and rural dystopian with utopian elements. The name Nobson is descriptive of the phallic forms of some of the buildings, and the phallic form is put to use every where from tree stumps to drawbridges. There are no people in the drawings.
Noble's drawings have been made since 1997 and show every aspcest of life in Nobson from the dwellings, the park, the cemetery, the factories, the sewage works, the school, the hospital, in fact every amentity and facilty a small town would have. There is also a large house for the artist which has a number of rooms and a tower. The drawings are made in pencil and are about 3 metres high, extremely detailed and finley drawn so that the whole drawing can take a long while to peruse. Any words spelled out by the buildings are readable from a distance only. Noble also makes small drawings showing details from Nobson.
The words in the drawings usually spell out a political or idealogical statement or quotation that deals in some way with the life of ordinary working people.
Noble has also represented Nobson as a photomural, for which he made a tent in the shape fo a letter from the Nobson typeface and pitched in in a forest. Woodand animals are added to the photpgraph digitally as if this were a rural idyll. He has made a digitally animated drawing too, which shows the factorys of Nobson in a static single shot view, with very little happening save for smoke plumes emitting from chimneys and a train travelling across periodically. 
!Noble Tim and Webster Sue.
Bristih artist partners born in the late 1960s who make sculpturee and installation. Noble and Webster's work is an unashamed unapologietc ode to money, creation, fame and ego combined with traditional forms, crafts and materials. Their illuminated signs which lean against the gallery wall or are moutned on it are an example of this. These may take the tform fo a dollar sign entirely made of white light bulbs, or of tattoo designs such as a heart stabbed by a dagger with a word on on a ribbon, Las Vegas style.
Noble and Welbster made an installation fro which they made life size models of themselves as apes, cast in fibreglass and walking on a pristine white undulating gallery floor. Antohre consists of an enormous pile of rubbish, with a strong light projected at it, casting a shadow on the wall behind to revela the profiles fo the artists, created from careful arrangement of the rubbish but only visible as a shadow.

O'keefes achievement was, throught her flower paitnigns, to turn a traditionally delicate and female subject into large bold and even aggressive or threatening forms.
!Ocean Humphrey.
British artist who makes semi-abstract paitnigns.
!Offili Chris.
British artist born in the late 1960s who became known and initially notorious for his pattern paintignis and paitnigns of balck women and men with elephant dung attached in the mid 1990s. Offilis figurative paintings are complex in their execution. A psychedilic pattern is painted first, then a figure added, picked out and filled in exclusivyely with concnentric lines of tiny dots fo paint in many colours which follow the contours of the figure. His earlier works have cutouts from pornographic magazines collaged on to the surface. The whole paitning is then covered with a layer of poured plastic resin, adding a physical depth ot the surface. Elelphant dung soaked in resin is attached tot eh canvase, sometimes with words and names picked out in coloured map pins on them. The paintings rest against the wall on two lumps of dung.
Ofili mostly made paintings of women in this series which he described as goddesses, buesutful highly stylised head and shoulders portraits of woman wearing jewellery, including a black Madonna which created a furore amongst the amercian Christian right for it juxtapostitgon of the virgin mary with elephant dung. He also made a number of full length portraits of male characters including one called Captian Shit, a black superhero. Ofili continned to paint women and to make a large number of watercolours on paper, drawings and prints, until the early 00s. 
He then made a series of paintings depicting a man and a woman, as lovers, but also as iconogrpahci symbols of Africa and its people. Ofili;s continued to use the dung to represent his connection with his mother country (he had begun to use it after his first trip to see where his family had come from). The lovers paintings are made in the same way, but there are fewer collaged elements. Flowers and leaves are added to the backgrounds and the paintings are more reflective.
In 2002 he made a serieis of 13 works featuring a monkey, paitnied in the same way. These are installed in an especially contructed timber lined room resembling a darkended chapel. Illuminated so that they have an icon like quality each paitigns is based on a different colour and titled Mono Azul, Mono Or for example, meaning Blue Monkey Gold Monkey. The twelve monkeys, six on each side of the room, depict the apostles, and the gold monlkey at the end of the room is Christ. This work is typically both irreverent and reverent.
All of Ofili's work is characterised by a sense of the profound combined with a highly decorative surface and a dry humour. He plunders popular culture and mixes influence in the same way that hip hip music does. Hip hop is often cited as an importance influnec on Ofili's work. 
!Olde Wolbers Saskia.
Duthc artist born in around 1970 who makes videos based on models that she constructs in her studio whcih have a spoken narrative oviceover. Olde Wolbers takes a true story, often from a newspaper, a tale of a soemoens life or something that happerned to someone, and elaborates it, writing a first person narrative which Olde Wolbers chooses a person or an actor to read it. She imagines a scene in which this tale might unfold and constructs a trhee dimensional model, which she then films. The models are made from a variety of materils - one model of an underground car park also resembles the frosted interitor of a friedge but in fact is made from textured plastic sheeting and plastic bottles and cups. Antohter of a hospital ward is made from a pet cage dipped in hopusehold gloss paint and submerged in a tank, filmed underwater. The paint dirps but stays in place, creating an eerie white scene.
Olde Wolbers films have an eerie often futuristic quality which is at odds both with the prosaic materials fo their construction and wtih the true story narrative of the voiceover. The narrative becomes extraordinary both in the writing and in the reading, Watching, the viewer has to grapple with interpreting what they see, which seems very real yet palpably is not and relating it to what they hear. 
!Oldenburgh Claes.
American artist born in the 1940s who is known for his soft scultures. Which he began to make in the sixties. Along with Warhol, Oldenburhg was the main pop art sculptor. Oldenburgh worked in a number of different media dand also made a large number of working drawings. His soft sculptures are made of fabric, sewn and stuffed in the manner of soft toys, using canvas which he then painted or vinyl. The sculptures represent vastly enlarged objects such as a light switch made in white vinyl, a drum kit made in paitned canvas and a hamburger, accurately scaled up and constructed but stuffed loosely so that they sagged and collapsed. The light switch (about 1 metre square) hangs drooping on the wall, the drum kit (3 metres across) flopps in a heap on the floor. Oldenburgh also made many painted clay sculptures, roughly modelled and paitned, some cast in bronze, of which one of a hamburger is the best known. 
In the 1970s Oldenburhg began ot make public sculpure and also made a large number of designs, plans and sketches for mounumental works which were not always realised. These include a 30 metre high lipstick momument, a building based on the head of mickey mouse and one based on an electrical plug.

!Op de Bek Hans.

!Opie, Julian.
Bristihh artist born around 1960 who makes works in a range of media. Opie's early work consisted of painted cut outs made in folded and bent sheet metal. They stand on the floor and represent objects such as a pile books, telephones, even a paitning. The objects are paitned in a cartoon style with black outlines and they Opies to solution to his idea of making painting into sculpture This work demonstrated an interest in generic representation which has sustained throughout his career.
In the late 1980s and early 1990s Opie made a large number of suctpures fabricated from industrial materials. These fall into two groups - case, chest or freezer like constructions and glass screen constructions. The case or chest works are made from sheet metal with aluminium fittings - edging, feet, ventilation grilles etc. They are ambiguous since they are evidently non functional, with no discernible means of opening them, yet they refer visually only to functionality. In them Opie refers both to modernist design and to minimla sculpture in particular referrint to the work of Donald Judd. 
The glass screen works are arrangemnts of toughened glass in aluminium frames to resemble doors and glass walls that might be seen at an airport or other public space. The glass often has discs etched on it in a row at eye level. These arrangements engage the viewer in a familiar experience of negotiaon of glass barriers, whilst considering the formal arrangement as a sculpture.
Opie made a number of works taken from the symbols and pictograms used in signage, developing an idea of the point at which the individual becomes generic and vice versa. 
Opie has made many paitngins and prints, usually screen prints. The road paintings, Imagine You Are Driving, (around 60cm high) were made after he drove across America, and show roads reduced to the minimum of detail - a balck road, with white lines and green verges with blue sky, each rendered in absoulutey flat acrylic paint with hard edges. These piatnings conflate the experience of driving with the experience of the computer game and make the case for the roadscape being the contermporary landscape paiting. A series of night paintings are made in balck with blurry circles of yellow, white and red light indicating road lamps and car headlights and rear lights. 
Opies Computer game paintings (similar sized and also 2m high versions made) are a development of the road paintings, representing the same experience of driving but this time through a version of a computer generated urban environment smoothed out so that there is no detail at all. 
Opie took this approach into three dimensions with his model village sculptures, immaculatey made and spray painted buildings with almost no detailingt so that they become generid 
In the late 1990s continuing to work the idea of the generic Opie made a series of digital drawings of people he knew, removikng all the detail from a head and shoulders photograph to produce a generic image in balck on a brightly coloured background that actually retained a great deal o findividuality. These images were used on an album cover and became known to a much wider public. He also began to make digital animations, taking the same principle and applying it to the moving image. He filmed a person moving and reduced this image to the bare minimum, resulting in a moving pictogram which still retained some of the characteristics of the individual on which it was based.
!Oppenheim Dennis.
American artist working from the late 1960s onwards, much of Openheims work was site specific land art with a performative endurance element. For example in one of Openheim's best known works, Trench, he suspends his outstretched body by pressing with his hands and feet agiasnt the top of two opposite walls of a purpose built trench. In another he lies in the sun until he is burned and changes colour, calling this the act of makng the artwork.  
!Oppenheim Meret.
Surrealist artist working in France at the beginning of the twentieth century who made an iconic sculpture of a fur covered cup, saucer and spoon, as well as one of a pair fo white high heeled shoes upsidedown on a silver salver trussed with string and with paper frills on the ends of the heels. Oppenheim's objects provide a contrast to the work of many of her male contemporaries. She also made a pair of leather gloves, the conventional stiched ridges on the backs of each made to resemble the veins on the back of a hand.
!Orozco Gabriel.
Brazilian sculptor who made a work called Ping Pond Table, consisting of two ping pong tables cut in half where the net would be and arranged in a cross shape with a fishpond in the vacant square area in the middle. Four players can play simultaneously, the pond replacing the net as the barrier to be crossed by the ball.

!Otto Knapp Silke.
German artist working in Britain born in the late 1960s who makes paintings using watercolour on canvas. Otto Knap's earliest work in the 1990s was a series of female portraits taken from fashion magazines followed by a seiries of American landscapes sometimes in panoramic format often taken from Marlbofro advertisements and form her own photogrhaps. These became more impressionistic until she began to paint in watercolour on a white ground, making a number of groups of landscapes between 40 and 80 cm high. The watery paitn is applied gestrually pooling on the surface creating an often illusiory image. 
A Los Angeles series shows the city by day and by night, as does a group of Las Vegas paintings. Otto Knap then made a number of 'vegetation' paintings depicting the greenness of parks, gardens and galsshouses such as Kew with enough detail hinted at for the viewer to interpret the image as a landscape, as well as some yellow desert paintings. More redcently Otto Knap has made a number of paintings of show girls, again in watercolour, the colours predominantly red.

!Oursler Tony.
US artist who became known for his video installations in the early 1990s. Oursler filmed himself and other people talking. He made scarecrow like figures with white pillows for heads, some life size some doll sized. The videos are projected on to the pillows, the projector either wall mounted or placed on the floor directly in front of the figure. The projectors are very much part of the work. The figures then 'speak' to the viewer, usually stream of consciousness babble, often sounding as if they are talking to some kind of therapist or analyist. Sometimes they become distressed and scream. Although patently unreal, the figures seem very real and the viewer has a poignant attatchment to them. In one case the projection is onto a heap of clothes as if the person who inhabited them has gone and there is just a soul remaining. In another a pillow on a sofa might carry the projection, or one squashed under a toppled chest of drawers, or stuffed into a suitcase. The general impression is of often torutrued souls whispering and babbling in uncomfortable positions.
!Paik Nam June.
American artist who was one fo the first to make and show video as a sculpture. Paik was perhaps the first artist to broadcoast his work on national tv, which he did by purchasing advertising slots. The work most often associatied with him is a robot out of tv monitors. Paik foresaw the ubiqutiey of television culture from its earliest days.

!Perriton Simon.
British artist born 1962 makes paper cut-outs on a large scale. These often but not always doily folded designs, and although at first glance they appear as lacy patterns, they are in fact thematic, for example a mushroom cloud in circular symmetry with CND symbol, barbed wire, mixed in with floral patterning to create complex intricate and skilled interwoven patters of great delicacy and ephemerality. The cut-outs may be single or multicoloured and are made by sticking paper together to the required size and then cutting out the design with a scalpel.
Perriton sometimes layers versions or the same cutout one colour on top fo antoher. The cutouts are always displayed unframed and unmournted,l hanging from pins in the wall.
!Perry Grayson.
British artist who made ceramic pots and vases richly decorated with drawn images, collaged photographic and printed elements, huge variety of glazes and gold and silver leaf. the pots seem like precious decorative objects but on closer inspection are revealed to bear highly politicised messages about issues that concern the artist fro example abuse, events that have caught his notice. This juxtaposition of luxury objet d'art with hard hitting message makes the vases transcend their genre. Perry is a transvestite who nearly always appears in public as a woman. She has many identities, a Russian babushka, a Victorian lady, and often carries a porcelain doll dressed in a matching costume. Perry has also made photographic works - as a woman in her various alter egos, and embroideries. The costumes themselves are made by Perry and are also part of the work.
!Pettibon Raymond.
American artist who makes pen and ink drawings. Pettibons main inspirations are William Blake, surfing, film and cartoons. His drawings often use the cartoon format fo a balck outlined box, with text at the botton hand written in capitals, as well as thought bubbles. There is rarely a drawing without text. The transient medium and sometime jokey style he uses and the subject he chooses to explore belie the seriousness of Pettibon's intent. He often alludes to the larger issues of life, death and the sublime. Surfing is Pettibons passion and the surfer in his work symobolese the imsignifacanc of human kind in the face of nature. The surfer might for example be shown at to top fo a huge wave, and have a thought bubble with an eixstnetial thought wirttten in it. Another drawing might feature a gun, or a light bulb with a caption. Some of the captions read like lines from a 40s film noir.
Pettibons drawings are either around 25 or 60 cm high, made on white paper, usually exhibited pinned to the wall unframed.

Peyton always paints on board, quite small (around 50 cm high maximum) in portrait format, using translucent colour rapdildy and simply applied. The white paitng used for the ground and the coloured paint often drips over the edge of the board.

!Picasso Pablo.
Picasso is a Spanish artist usually named as the greatest modern artist in the world. Picasso was born in the late nineteenth century and made his greatest works in the 1930s and 40s. He died in the 1970s.
Picasso's work progressed through a number of different periods and he made work in a variety of media. His skill at drawing is consistently evident, but he was equally good at sculuptre.
Of Picassos early career, his Blue period is the best known. This describes a number of figurative paintings that have a blue light cast on them, for example a boy with a dove in his hand, and a young boy dressed as a Harlequin balancing on a ball. The dove and the clown, paritcuarly Harlequin and Peirrot were recurring motifs throughout Picassos life.
In the early 1900s Picasso, along with Brauq and Gris, would have seen African sculutres for the first time when a collection was exhibited in paris. This, combined with Cezannes experiments with the picture plane, led the three artists to make the first cubist works. 
Althogh Cubism is most readily attributed to Picasso, it was Gris who in fact made the first Cubist collage. Picasso made a number of these collages featuring bottles, guitars, and also some small scultures including one of a shelf with some domestic objects on it made from rough bits of wood, painted with a bobble braid tacked along the front. 
Picasso made a great number of Cubst paintings, including some figure studies in which he tries to represent every facet of a three dimensional figure in two dimensions. These paintings are realery more that a metre high and predominalnty brownish or greyish. 
During this period Picasso began to develop his mature style to reach its greatest achievement in two paintings, Guernica and the Demoiselles D'Avignon. Guernica was painted in 1917 to commemorate General Francos massacre of a Spanish village. In it Picasso wanted to show the horror of war and suffering, and he was conscious of making a painting which would get attention. He made the painting immediately after the event and it was intended to be given to the town, although it never was. Geurnica is a very large paitnign, 3 meters by 6, which shows a number of figures based clearly on African sculpture (for example Dogon masks) paitnied in black, purple, white and grey. It is an image of horror, of death and suffering, the figures have mask like faces, and their limbs are intertwined so that the whole painting operates as an abstracgt composition as well.
It was the first time a modern artist had made such an explicit painting that was so deliberately intended to shock. Reproducitons fo Gurenican were instantly printed in newspapers all over the world.
Demoiselles D'Avignon is composesd along similar lines but depicts a more pleasant scene of naked women in the countryside, painted in flesh tones and greens. However the women have the same mask like faces as the figures in Guernica. A film was made of Picasso making this paitning, showing how he drew out the figures with continous lines, never correcting himself.
Picasso painting many portraits, often of the various women in his life, including Dora Maar, one of his lovers. 
As well as carved and assembled sculpture, Picasso made a number of surrealist objects, such as a bull's head made from a bycyce seat and a pair fo handlebars for horns.
Drawing underpinned everything that Picasso did, and he made a large number fo them. In particualy a number of balck line drawings of the Minotaur, a myth which fascninated him. Later in he made clouloured lithographs which look like pastel drawings which are very well known, such as a simple hand holding a bunch of flowers and a dove with an olive branch in its beak. 
In his exploration of every medium Picasso included ceramics, producing a great many plates, vases, and other objects, which he painted on, and also, surpisingly Silverware - he made a series of tooled and embossed silver platters.

Pierson Jack
American artist who makes photographs, paintings, sculptures and installation. Pierson's work is diverse - his photographs are of everyday scenes given a fairy tale twist. His paintings are really photographs printed on to the canvase using a three colour printing process that transfers pigment in discrete dots of colour. His installations are various, examples are a section of wall and floor painted yellow with a cabinet with a record player on it, some LPS whdi the viewr is invited to play and a chair, and a section of the wall and floor paitned purple with a made up single bed placed on the floor well away from the wall on which the viewer is invited to lie.
Peirson's sculpture in the main consists of phrases, sayings, and truisms spelled out with found signage lettering on the wall. The lettering is all in different styles materials and sizes, some old, some new, some Perspex, some moulded plastic, some painted or gilded wood, some cast or cut metal.
!Piggot Hadrian.
Britihs artist best known for a body of work he made in the 1990s. Piggot crafted porcelain sculptures that took the form of bathroom sinks, for example, their forms changed to smooth rounded abstract shapes, convex where they should be concave, entirely without function yet with the addition of chrome taps and other fittings they became surreal objects. A plughole might be placed on a bulging shape where no water could ever collect. Piggot had leather cases made with satin lined moulded inserts to hold the sculptures, adding to their surrealism but inferring an useful portability. He also made some cargvings from bars of soap.
Piggot late began to make photographs of lanscape.

!Pittman Lari.
American painter who became extremely popular in the 1990s for his intensly intricate and complex abstract patingins. Pittman draws on a multiplicity of sources, borrowing with impunity and putting together intense compositions that owe somethynig variously to fabric design, the 1950s, architectural drawing, pop art, cartoons, and post modern painting. It is impossigle to locate the source of the elements of a Pittman painting because there are just so many elements and they are so perfrctly graphically intertwined.
But what makes Pittman's work particaraly distinctive is the method he uses to make them. The main design is outlined and painted in flat colour. This in itself produces a complex very brightly coloured paiingtin. Then dots of paint, squeezed onto the canvas (the dots no more than 2mm across) are usedl to outline some areas, fill in others. The dots are three dimensional and it becomes hard to believe that the patoingin could have been made by hand, although it clearly has. Pittmans paitnigns function as a bold design with an overall structure as well as a surface of patterns within pattersn that seems to have more in common with embroidery than painitng.
!Plackman Carl.

!Polke Sigmar.
A German artist who began working in the 1960s. Polke is famous for painting on tea towels (also on bedsheets, table cltohrs and curtains) In fact he tends to eschew canvas as as gournd altogether, sometimes working on resin panels as well as a huge variety of woven and printed textiles. Much of his work has photogrphaci screen printing as well as hand painting. The screen print is usually a fragment of a vastly enlarged image from a newspaper, the dots that make up the image as big as a coin, printed in balck. Polke's hand painted is usually black outlines of figures and objetcts. 
Polke's paintings depict such subjects as war, classical myth, weaponry, rural idyll and are notoriousl resistant to intoerpretaiopn. Many of the images are taken from newspapers as if to address the proliferation of imagery and the difficulty fo assimlilatign it. The fabric and pattern of the background is carefully chosen. Sometimes two or more contrasting panels are stitched together. Polke is a painter who is concerned with surface and image, what is real and not real, how an image is read, in what order and how narrative reading can be imposed and obstrcued. 
In the late 90s and early 00s, Polke pursued his interest in enlarging printed images by making use of digital images and large format printing to produce 20m wide enlarged newspeper images, printed on vinyl coated fabric.
!Pollock Jackson.
The best known of the American abstract expressionist painters and argulaby the best known modernist painter of all time. Pollock was born in the 1920s and died in the early 70s in a car accident. 
Pollock began working in the 1950s and at the end of the decade he began to experiment with pouring paint. Placing the canvas on the floor he began to use the brush to pour housepaint from, not touching the canvas at all. This was a radical step, since no one had ever made a paitnign without touching the canvas and with so little control over the result. Pollock became very skilled in this way of applying paitn, using bigger and bigger canvases and more colours.
These works made him a sensation. Nobody had ever seen anything like them. A photographer Hans Namuth made a seminal film of Pollock showing him pouring paitn on the canvas which included a section filmed from below through glass. It became known as action paitning. The paintings are up to 2 metres high and very wide. They are all composed of an all over 'pattern' of drips, applied in a dymanic but highly controlled way, so that an overall design is evident. Far from relinquisiheg control, Polloack became master of the technique. The combinations of colours are subtle, usually involving balck and white with one or two other colours. One of the best known is Blue Poles, whicih has two vertical blue stripes amidst the swrilign drips.
Pollock made many public commissions and his work was takien up by the American government as beign a pinnacle of modernims and representiong American freedom. Unwittingly Pollocks work was used as US propaganda against the soviet Union, when the CIA covertly funded an international touring exhibition fo the work of the abstract expressionists designed to be compared directly and unfavoruabley iwht soviet socialist realist art. The abstract expressionists represented freedom of expression at a time when the government wanted to promote American as the land of freedom and thus perpetuate anti-communist sentiment.
Pollock was married to Lee Krasner, also an abstract painter. Krasner saw Pollock through many years of increasing alcholosim and infodieltiy, taking him to live and work in a house on Long Island to help him stop dringking, until she felt forced to leave him. However she continued to care for him. Krasner subjugated her own careet to Pollocks and it seems reasonable to suppose that he was unlikely to have become successful or even made the action paintings without her support.
!Pondick Rona.
Birtish artist who makes sculpture by reupholstering and altering furniture. A chair might have legs like an animal for example. A sofa might have its shape altered to allulde to the female form. Pondick's work is influenced by surrealism in the way she transforms an everyday object.
!Poons Larry.

For example a gravestone or the pages of an open book might carry the text, which is painted as if it were a printed typeface. Poons paints in a precise way using flat shiny colours and hard edges. 
!Poussin Nicholas.
French artist who painted classical themes, Poussin made precisely drawn and richly coloured paintings that appear quite static and almost theatrical. He was rediscovered in the early twentieth century by the art historian Antony Blunt who wrote a catalogue raisonne. 
!Prendergast Kathy.

!Prieto Monique.
American artist who paints large flat-coloured abstracts which look at first as if they are accidental 'pours' of colour but which in fact are drawn on a computer first. Prieto says that she evolved this method of working after the birth of her children to speed up the production process, enable her to do some preparatory work at home and make the most of her time. The paintings are cool, calm and reflective.
!Prince Richard.
American artist who took the legacy of pop art, and developed it, working from the 1970s onwards. Prince makes a variety of types of work, but is most usually associated with two: the Marlvboro add and the joke paitngns. These illustrate Prince's prime interest in generic forms. 
The Marlboro work is a reproduced advertisement for Marlboro cigarettes with the cowboy on the horse removed. What remains is still instantly recognisable because of the brand name, and the type of landscape. It is a while before the viewer realises that the cowboy is not there, since the image is so familiar and this si what they expect to see. This reveals clearly how embedded in visual memoty the advertismetn is.
Prince has made the joke paintings throughout his career. They are very simply a joke painted on a plain coloured canvas in a san serif typeface. The colours, typefaces and size of paitnign varies, he has made some that are 4 metres high. The letters are usually black or white, but Prince has also made paintings in which the lettering is very indistinct. The jokes are mostly quite funny.
Prince has also made a large number of paintings using photographic screen printing mixed with painting, and abstrct pattern paintings. His prolific output is distinguished by its variety, but also by consistency within that variety.
!Prior Lisa.
Ameircan artist who makes video works featuring her alter ego who is an expert in any chosen field. Prior's films borrow the styel of corportate or promotional video, and she appears as a psychiatrist fro example, telling the viewer about a certain psychiatric condition. Or about the mental health of Vietnam veterans. Or about her own dating experiences. Prior's persona is compelling but ultimately fallible and not without humour. More recently Prior has made a work which has more formal concerns. She filmed a dog shaking itself, then looped this short section and slowed it down. The sound of the bell on the dogs collar is transformed and the dogs shaking appears graceful and deliberate.
!Pyman James.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes peincil drawings on paper. Pyman's drawings are often large, up to one and a ahlf meters high, and depict a variety of subjects rangin from groups of people to landscapes or even just a view of waves on water. They are drawn in a soft but precise way, the pencil smudged to create soft shaded areas. 
!Quinn Mark.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture. Quinn's best known work is Blood Head, a cast of his own head made from several pints of his frozen blood. This is displayed in a refrigerated vitrine on a plinth. Quinn's work is mostly concened with the human body, life and death. A companion work to Blood Head is Bread Head, a bust modelled from bread dough and baked. Quinn pursued his interest in preservation through freezing in other works. He used a species of frog which hibernates at sub zero temperatures in suspended animation, displaying the frog in a block of ice in a refregieartaed vitrine. He also made a larger work using a mass of frozen flowers. Quinn also cast his own body and used it to produce a thin latex skin, which he hung form a hook in the ceiling, as if it were his own flayed skin. More redcently Quinn has been pursuing his work with flowers and making photographs.
